# 24. Sūrat al-Nūr

Sixty-four verses. Medinan. It takes its name from the expression **ٱللَّهُ نُورُ ٱلسَّمَٰوَٰتِ وَٱلْأَرْضِ** in the thirty-fifth verse and from the **parable of light** built in that verse.

This sūra sets side by side two things whose standing together in the Qurʾān looks surprising at first: **the rulings that govern the gravest accusations a society can make** and **the densest image of light in the Qurʾān.** The first thirty-four verses of the sūra speak of things that must be covered, of things that must not be made public, of doors that are not entered without leave, of glances that are to be lowered. Then the thirty-fifth verse comes and lights a lamp.

**This is the first thing that can be said about the sūra as a whole, and I record it as my own reading:** Sūrat al-Nūr takes as its subject the difference between **seeing** and **looking.** The glance is lowered (30-31), the house is covered (27-29), an accusation is bound to four witnesses (4, 13), a thing heard is not carried in the mouth (15-16) — and in the middle of all these restrictions a light is lit. The sūra, that is, narrows man's **power of sight** while binding his **capacity to see** to something else.

The sūra is also unique in this commentary in another respect: **it is the one place in the Qurʾān where a sūra names itself "sūra"** (the first verse). I take this up in detail in the section below.

## The name of the sūra — *nūr*

**نُور** — root **ن-و-ر**: **light.**

The analysis of the word was made at `071-nuh.md`, 71/16; I rely on that treatment. The note there was this: *nūr* is light **itself** — what illuminates, what removes darkness; *sirāj* is the **body that produces** the light. The Qurʾān distributes these two words consistently and keeps the distinction.

From the same root:

| Word | Sense |
|---|---|
| **نُور** (*nūr*) | Light, brightness |
| **نَار** (*nār*) | Fire |
| **مُنِير** (*munīr*) | That which illuminates |
| **مَنَارَة** (*manāra*) | The high place where a light is set — whence the English "minaret" |
| **أَنَارَ / نَوَّرَ** | To illuminate |

**And there is a pair inside the root:** *nūr* (light) and *nār* (fire) come from the same root. The difference between them is that fire both burns and gives light; light only gives light. The thirty-fifth verse will dwell on this distinction: there it will be said of the olive oil that *"it would almost give light though no fire had touched it."*

**It should not be forgotten that the name was given later.** The names of sūras are not the text's claim but the tradition's choice. But I can say that this choice is apt, and its ground stands in the text: the root *nūr* occurs in the sūra not only in the thirty-fifth verse but in the thirty-ninth and fortieth as well, and there it is named together with **its opposite** (*wa man lam yajʿali'llāhu lahū nūran fa-mā lahū min nūr*). The sūra's central word is thus built once within the sūra and once negated.

I record this as **my own reading**; the places of the words can be counted in the text.

## Its being Medinan, and the historical frame

There is no disagreement that the sūra was revealed in the Medinan period, and the text itself supports this:

- There is an order of rulings and an authority to apply them: penalties are commanded to a community (2, 4), the procedure of witness is regulated (4, 6-9, 13).
- An accusation spreading within a community is on a scale that would shake the whole of it (11-20).
- There are house doors, rooms, the hour of the midday rest, the coming and going of servants and of children (27-29, 58-59): a settled city life.
- Around the Prophet there is an assembly where business is done in common, and an order of general mobilisation (62-63).
- There is an anxiety about security and a promise of "safety after fear" (55).

**I say nothing definite about which year the sūra belongs to.** In the sources it is widely transmitted that it falls around the fifth or sixth year after the emigration; there is disagreement over the detail, and this commentary does not give datings it cannot establish. What can be said is that the sūra presupposes **a settled Medinan society.**

## The structure of the sūra

The sūra consists of seven blocks. The boundaries of the blocks are marked by changes of address and by the calls *yā ayyuhā'lladhīna āmanū*.

| Block | Verses | Subject | The sign that opens the block | Key words |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **I** | **1** | The sūra's presentation of itself | *Sūratun anzalnāhā* | *faraḍnāhā*, *āyātin bayyināt* |
| **II** | **2-10** | The penalties for adultery and for slander; witness; *liʿān* | *Al-zāniyatu wa'l-zānī* | *jalda*, *raʾfa*, *shuhadāʾ*, *shahādāt* |
| **III** | **11-26** | The *ifk*: the spreading of an accusation, and its undoing | *Inna'lladhīna jāʾū bi'l-ifk* | *ifk*, *ʿuṣba*, *talaqqī*, *shuyūʿ*, *buhtān* |
| **IV** | **27-34** | House, leave to enter, the glance, covering, marriage and freedom | *Yā ayyuhā'lladhīna āmanū lā tadkhulū* | *istiʾnās*, *ghaḍḍu'l-baṣar*, *azkā*, *tūbū* |
| **V** | **35-45** | The parable of light and its counter-parables; the witness of the universe | *Allāhu nūru'l-samāwāti wa'l-arḍ* | *mishkāt*, *sarāb*, *ẓulumāt*, *tasbīḥ* |
| **VI** | **46-57** | The distance between word and deed; obedience; the promise | *Laqad anzalnā āyātin mubayyināt* | *muʿriḍūn*, *samiʿnā wa aṭaʿnā*, *istikhlāf* |
| **VII** | **58-64** | The manners of leave within the house, and the manners of common business | *Yā ayyuhā'lladhīna āmanū li-yastaʾdhinkum* | *ʿawrāt*, *ḥaraj*, *amrin jāmiʿ* |

**The most striking thing about the structure is that the fourth block (27-34) stands immediately before the central verse (35), and the seventh block (58-64) at the end of the sūra.** The matter of asking leave is taken up twice: once **on entering a house from outside** (27-29), once **on passing from room to room within the house** (58-59). Between them lies an entire section of light.

I record this as **my own reading**; the places of the verses stand in the text.

## One half of the sūra covers, the other half uncovers

The weave of Sūrat al-Nūr is built upon a contrast, and this contrast can be verified from the text:

| What is covered, closed | What is opened, shown |
|---|---|
| Accusation — closed off by the condition of four witnesses (4, 13) | The penalty — carried out in the open, with a group as witness (2) |
| What is heard — its being carried in the mouth is forbidden (15-16) | The verses — *mubayyināt*: making clear, making plain (34, 46) |
| The house — not entered without leave (27-29) | The mosques — **raised up** so that His name may be mentioned in them (36) |
| The glance — lowered (30-31) | The lamp — lit (35) |
| Adornment — not displayed (31) | The heavens and the earth — named with the light of God (35) |

**The left column restricts man's appetite for showing; the right column shows what is to be shown.** The sūra does not only say "do not look" — it says "look here."

I record this reading as **my own reading**. Its ground is the places of the verses in the two columns.

## A note of method: what is not done in this section

Sūrat al-Nūr is one of the sūras where this commentary's rules of style most need to be observed. I therefore write at the outset what is not done:

**One. No legal ruling is issued.** From the second, fourth, sixth and thirty-first verses of the sūra, broad discussions have arisen in classical jurisprudence. In this section **the disagreements of those discussions are transmitted in tables**, none is preferred, and none is binding. To decide about the practical application of a ruling is not the business of this text.

**Two. No debate of criminal law is entered.** Neither how the penalties are to be applied today, nor whether they are to be applied, nor the current controversies around them are the subject of this text. What is treated is **the language of the verses**: the roots of the words, the word order, the sequence.

**Three. The reports about the *ifk* affair are not narrated.** That the verses from eleven to twenty point to an event is plain, and in the sources that event is transmitted in detail. **In this section no personal name is given, no discussion is entered about who was accused, and no details of the reports are transmitted.** The reason is the verse itself: the text has not given the names of persons. In the twenty-sixth verse it says *ulāʾika mubarraʾūna mimmā yaqūlūn* — "they are free of what is said" — and the most correct thing this commentary can do is to stop where the verse stops. **What will be treated is the frame the verses themselves give:** how a report spreads, how language works, and how "thinking it a small thing" is diagnosed.

**Four. No current politics and no current controversy is entered.** How the fifty-fifth verse (the promise of *istikhlāf*) and the thirty-first (covering) are used in the controversies of today is not the subject of this text.

**Five. The reader is not scolded.** The subjects of this sūra — slander, gossip, the glance, privacy — are the subjects that have produced the most scolding in the language of preaching. This section does not do that.

## The basmala

The word-by-word analysis of the basmala was made in the al-Fātiḥa section; I do not repeat it here. The numbering of the verses counts without the basmala.

One point particular to this sūra may be recorded: **in the first verse of a text opened with the names al-Raḥmān and al-Raḥīm there stands the verb *faraḍnāhā* — "we cut and determined".** This nearness between mercy and the drawing of a limit was recorded at the head of `049-hucurat.md` as well. In al-Nūr the relation is a step plainer: most of the limits laid down through the sūra are drawn **around something that needs protecting** — a person's standing, the inside of a house, the intimacy of a family.

---

## 24/1 — سُورَةٌ أَنزَلْنَٰهَا وَفَرَضْنَٰهَا وَأَنزَلْنَا فِيهَآ ءَايَٰتٍۭ بَيِّنَٰتٍ لَّعَلَّكُمْ تَذَكَّرُونَ

*Sūratun anzalnāhā wa faraḍnāhā wa anzalnā fīhā āyātin bayyinātin laʿallakum tadhakkarūn*

"This is a sūra We have sent down and **whose rulings We have determined precisely.** In it We have sent down clear verses, that you may take heed."

### The place in the Qurʾān where a sūra names itself "sūra"

This verse is one of the most striking openings in the Qurʾān in point of syntax, and the reason is this:

**The Qurʾān uses the word *sūra* in many places.** The addressees are asked to "bring a sūra like it" (al-Baqara 2/23, Yūnus 10/38); it is told what men did when a sūra was sent down (al-Tawba 9/64, 9/86, 9/124; Muḥammad 47/20). **But in all these places the word points from outside at a text being spoken of.**

**Here, however, the sūra names itself.** The subject of the sentence is the text itself: *sūratun* — "this is a sūra". And immediately after, two verbs come about that sūra: *anzalnāhā* (we sent it down) and *faraḍnāhā* (we determined it).

**The explanations the lexicographers record about the root of the word diverge, and none is certain:**

| Explanation | Root | Ground |
|---|---|---|
| **From a city wall** | س-و-ر | *Sūr* — the wall that surrounds a city. A sūra too encloses a portion, sets its bounds. |
| **From height, from a rank** | س-و-ر | *Siwār* — a bracelet; *tasawwur* — to climb up over. A sūra is a rank, a step. |
| **From a remaining piece** | س-أ-ر | *Suʾr* — what is left over of a thing. A sūra is a piece separated from a whole. |

**I prefer none of them, and none is binding.** But what the three explanations have in common may be recorded: **all three arrive at the idea of a "separated portion"** — whether walled around, or set apart as a step, or broken off from a whole.

**And this is directly connected with the verse's second verb.**

### وَفَرَضْنَٰهَا — to cut and determine

**Root: ف-ر-ض.** And the concrete sense of the root carries the whole weight of the verse.

**The primary sense of the root: to notch, to cut a nick, to set a mark by cutting into an object.** To cut the notch in which a bowstring will sit, to nick a measure into a rod — that is the verb.

**This root was analysed at `002-bakara.md`, 2/68** (*lā fāriḍun wa lā bikr* — in the description of the cow, in the sense "advanced in age"); the note there was this: *fāriḍ*, of an animal, means "past bearing, of a determined age", and from the same root come *farḍ* and *farīḍa*: **an obligation determined precisely, with its limit drawn.**

The derivatives of the root show how the concrete sense spread:

| Word | Sense | Its link with the concrete core |
|---|---|---|
| **فَرْض** (*farḍ*) | An obligation determined precisely | Marked by notching: its place is fixed |
| **فَرِيضَة** (*farīḍa*) | A determined share, a determined amount | A portion cut off and set apart |
| **فَرْض** (in a bow) | The notch at the end of the bow | The root itself, directly |
| **فِرَاض** | The place where a thing has been cut, set apart | — |
| **مَفْرُوض** | Determined, set apart | — |

**And the Qurʾān uses this root in the division of inheritance and in the fixing of a dower:** *naṣīban mafrūḍan* — "a determined share" (al-Nisāʾ 4/7); *fa-niṣfu mā faraḍtum* — "half of what you determined" (al-Baqara 2/237). **In both places the word comes together with a number, a fraction, an amount.**

**So *faraḍnāhā* means this: what is inside this sūra was not left indefinite; its bounds were drawn, its shares were set apart, its measure was notched.**

**And this agrees exactly with the content of the sūra.** In Sūrat al-Nūr there are rulings given with numbers: **a hundred** (2), **eighty** (4), **four** (4, 6, 8, 13), **five** (7, 9), **three** (58). Few other sūras in the Qurʾān carry so many numbers.

**I record this as my own reading, and its ground can be counted:** the root of the verb *faraḍnāhā* is "to notch and set a measure", and the sūra does in fact set measures.

**A variant reading is transmitted in the sources:** it is recorded that beside *wa faraḍnāhā* (undoubled) the word is also read **with the consonant doubled.** The doubled reading is Form II and conveys **multiplicity, going into detail**: "we determined it in detail", "we determined many rulings one by one". **Since I cannot state with certainty which reader read which, I write no names**; this is the practice adopted in this commentary. The two readings do not exclude one another: the one conveys the precision of the determining, the other its detail.

### Two *anzalnā*s: the frame the syntax builds

In the verse the verb *anzalnā* occurs **twice**, and its two objects differ:

| | Object | What is said |
|---|---|---|
| **The first** | *hā* — **the sūra itself** | *Anzalnāhā wa faraḍnāhā* — we sent it down and determined it |
| **The second** | *āyātin bayyināt* — **the verses within it** | *Anzalnā fīhā* — we sent down within it |

**The verse thus names the whole and the part separately:** first the sūra as a unit, then what is in it, item by item.

**This is the finest point of syntax in the verse, and it is worth recording.** The text claims its own wholeness: this is not a set of scattered rulings brought together — it is a text **sent down as a unit.** And this claim is a ground for those who hold that the sūra really has a single weave.

I record this as **my own reading**; that the verb occurs twice and that its objects differ is the wording of the text.

### ءَايَٰتٍۭ بَيِّنَٰتٍ — "clear verses"

**بَيِّنَات** — root **ب-ي-ن**. The root was treated in detail at `049-hucurat.md`, 49/6 (*fa-tabayyanū*); the note there was this: the concrete sense of the root is **separation, the space between two things** (*bayn*), and *bayān* is an operation of **separating** — separating what is mixed, making it distinguishable.

**So *bayyināt* does not mean "easy verses" — it means "verses that distinguish".** Verses that separate one thing from another, that make a boundary visible.

**And the word will return three more times in the sūra:** *āyātin mubayyināt* (34), *āyātin mubayyināt* (46), *yubayyinu'llāhu lakumu'l-āyāt* (58, 59, 61). The distribution of the root through the sūra shows how the sūra names its own work: **separating.**

**That it comes indefinite is worth noting too:** *āyātin bayyinātin* — "certain clear verses". There is no article. The rule the classical lexicographers record is that the indefinite here conveys **magnification**; I impose no preference.

### لَّعَلَّكُمْ تَذَكَّرُونَ — the purpose

**تَذَكَّرُونَ** — root **ذ-ك-ر**, Form V: **to try to remember, to call a thing back to one's own mind.**

**The choice of the form matters.** The verse does not say *taʿlamūn* (that you may know), it says *tadhakkarūn*. *Dhikr* is the bringing back of what has been forgotten; not learning but **remembering.**

**This says something, when the content of the sūra is considered.** The subjects of Sūrat al-Nūr — guarding a person's standing, not spreading what one has heard, not entering without leave, lowering the glance — are none of them things man has never known. **Instead of giving new knowledge, the verse speaks of calling back something known.**

And the word will return in the twenty-seventh verse: *dhālikum khayrun lakum laʿallakum tadhakkarūn* — at the end of the manners of asking leave, the same verse-ending.

### Context — a sūra opening with a heading

**It is rare for a sūra in the Qurʾān to open in this way.** Sūras generally begin with a group of letters, with an oath, with praise, with an address, or directly with the subject. Here there is **a sentence of presentation**: "this is such and such, it was done thus, and this is what for."

**And the presentation prepares what comes immediately after.** In the second verse a very heavy ruling will come. The first verse says in advance where that ruling comes from and what kind of thing it is: **sent down** and **determined.** Neither a custom men produced, then, nor a piece of advice open to interpretation.

**I record this as an observation about the word order.** Setting a frame before a heavy ruling is a structure seen also in the long legislative sections of `002-bakara.md`: before the ruling, the nature of the ruling is stated.

### What this says to the present

What this verse says to the present stands in the root of the second verb.

*Faraḍnāhā* — to notch, to set a measure. **A thing's having a measure means that it is not without limit.** And the whole subject of the sūra is the domains in which man of himself recognises no limit: speech, curiosity, the glance, accusation.

All of these, left to themselves, tend to grow. A word grows, a curiosity grows, an accusation grows. **The sūra's first sentence cuts a notch into the growing.**

And the last word of the verse says how this will work: *tadhakkarūn* — that you may remember. **The limit, then, is laid down not as a barrier coming from outside but as a measure remembered from within.**

---

## 24/2 — ٱلزَّانِيَةُ وَٱلزَّانِى فَٱجْلِدُوا۟ كُلَّ وَٰحِدٍ مِّنْهُمَا مِا۟ئَةَ جَلْدَةٍ وَلَا تَأْخُذْكُم بِهِمَا رَأْفَةٌ فِى دِينِ ٱللَّهِ إِن كُنتُمْ تُؤْمِنُونَ بِٱللَّهِ وَٱلْيَوْمِ ٱلْءَاخِرِ وَلْيَشْهَدْ عَذَابَهُمَا طَآئِفَةٌ مِّنَ ٱلْمُؤْمِنِينَ

*Al-zāniyatu wa'l-zānī fa'jlidū kulla wāḥidin minhumā miʾata jalda, wa lā taʾkhudhkum bihimā raʾfatun fī dīni'llāhi in kuntum tuʾminūna bi'llāhi wa'l-yawmi'l-ākhir, wa'l-yashhad ʿadhābahumā ṭāʾifatun mina'l-muʾminīn*

"The woman who commits adultery and the man who commits adultery — flog each of them a hundred strokes. And let no pity for them take hold of you in the religion of God, if you believe in God and in the Last Day. And let a group of the believers witness their punishment."

### First, a note: what is not done in this section

This verse and those that follow gave rise to one of the broadest discussions in classical jurisprudence. **In this section the disagreements of that discussion are transmitted, none is preferred, and none is binding.** How the penalty is to be applied today, whether it is to be applied, and the current controversies around it are not the subject of this text.

**What will be treated is the language of the verse:** the roots of the words, their forms, their order, and the verse-ending.

### The first observation on the word order: the woman first

**The verse begins with *al-zāniyatu* — the feminine first.**

This is not the usual order in the Qurʾān's verses of ruling. In the fourth verse the ruling on slander comes with *alladhīna yarmūn* (masculine plural); the ruling on theft at al-Māʾida 5/38 comes with *al-sāriqu wa'l-sāriqa* (the masculine first).

**In the classical commentaries several explanations are given for this order:**

| Explanation | Ground |
|---|---|
| **Verse-ending and sound** | The flow of the sentence and its agreement with the close of the verse |
| **The weight in the ascription of the act** | That the consequence of the act is more visible upon the woman |
| **The relation of the order of the verses to the order of the subject** | That in the following verses, and in the sūra as a whole, it is the party slandered who is named |

**I prefer none of them, and none is binding.** The one certain thing is that the order stands so in the text.

**But an observation about the word order can be made, and I record it as my own reading:** the verse names the two parties **separately and equally**, and then says the equality once more with *kulla wāḥidin minhumā* — "each of them". **That the ruling is bound to both parties in the same measure is thus said three times:** the two nouns, the dual pronoun (*minhumā*), and *kulla wāḥidin*.

### فَٱجْلِدُوا۟ — the root ج-ل-د and the skin

**Root: ج-ل-د.** And the concrete sense of the root is directly the name of an organ.

**جِلْد (*jild*) — skin.** In Turkish the borrowed word *cilt* means both skin and the leather binding of a book, and from there a volume of a book as well — each part of a book bound in leather.

**And the verb is derived from the noun:** *jalada* — **to strike the skin.** The literal sense of the verb, then, is not "to punish" but "**to strike his skin**".

The derivatives of the root:

| Word | Sense |
|---|---|
| **جِلْد** (*jild*) | Skin |
| **جَلْدَة** (*jalda*) | A single stroke upon the skin |
| **جَلَد** (*jalad*) | Endurance, hardness, the capacity to bear |
| **جَلِيد** (*jalīd*) | Firm, enduring; and ice (water hardened) |
| **تَجَلُّد** (*tajallud*) | To show fortitude, to bear up |

**The choice of the word is worth recording, and this is one of the most important points in the language of the verse.**

In Arabic the general verb for striking is *ḍaraba*. The verse does not use it. The verb it uses **carries in its own name where the stroke will reach: the skin.**

**In the classical juristic literature a conclusion has been drawn from this word**, and I transmit it as it is transmitted: because the verb points to the skin, it has been said that the penalty **must not go past the skin** — that is, that the stroke must not maim or do lasting harm. Over the detail of the application — the force of the stroke, the kind of instrument, which parts of the body are to be avoided, the case of the sick and of the pregnant — there is **broad disagreement** among the schools, and none of it is given here as a ruling.

**And the root's second cluster of senses stands in an interesting place:** *jalad* — endurance; *tajallud* — fortitude. From the same root come both "to strike the skin" and "to bear up". The connection the lexicographers draw is this: **the skin is the body's enduring layer** — the first to meet what comes from outside, and the thing that protects what is within.

I record this as an observation about the root; I build no interpretation of the verse upon it.

### مِا۟ئَةَ جَلْدَةٍ — the giving of a number

**The verse gives a number: a hundred.**

This is the direct counterpart of the verb *faraḍnāhā* in the first verse. The root **ف-ر-ض** — to notch, to set a measure. **And here the notch is cut: a hundred.**

**The function of the number is a limit.** The penalty is not left indefinite, not left to the discretion of the one who applies it. Not an upper limit but **a fixed number** is given.

**And that the word *jaldatan* comes in the singular is worth noting:** *miʾata jalda* — "a hundred **one stroke**". In Arabic the numbers from three to ten take a plural specifier; a hundred takes a **singular** one. This is a rule of grammar, not a subtlety; but its outcome is worth recording: in saying "a hundred strokes" the sentence leaves the word in the form of **a single stroke.**

### وَلَا تَأْخُذْكُم بِهِمَا رَأْفَةٌ — the difference between *raʾfa* and *raḥma*

**رَأْفَة — root ر-أ-ف.** This root was analysed at `059-hasr.md`, 59/10, and at `057-hadid.md`, 57/9; I rely on those treatments.

The note there was this, and it sits exactly here:

> The lexicographers draw a distinction between *raḥma* and *raʾfa*: **mercy is general and may sometimes contain severity** (as a treatment that gives pain in order to heal a patient); ***raʾfa* is the refusal to give pain at all, the refusal to hurt.** The definition recorded in `057-hadid.md` is sharper still: *raʾfa* is above all the feeling of **not being able to bear that another should be harmed.**

**And the verse's choice of word is built exactly upon this distinction.**

| | *Raḥma* (ر-ح-م) | *Raʾfa* (ر-أ-ف) |
|---|---|---|
| Scope | Wide; the whole of the wish for another's good | Narrow; not bearing that pain be given |
| Can it contain severity | **Yes** — a treatment may give pain | **No** — not to hurt at all |
| Its source | Wishing another's good | Not bearing the sight |
| In the verse | Not forbidden | **This is what is forbidden** |

**What the verse forbids is not mercy.** What is forbidden is that *raʾfa* — the inability to bear witnessing pain — **should stop the ruling.**

And this distinction agrees with the language of the word elsewhere: the Qurʾān uses *raʾfa* positively of God and of the Prophet (al-Ḥadīd 57/9, al-Ḥashr 59/10, al-Tawba 9/128). **The word, then, is not bad in itself.** What is bad is its shifting a measure that has been laid down.

**The form of the verb says this too: *lā taʾkhudhkum* — "let it not take hold of you, let it not seize you".** Root **أ-خ-ذ**: to take, to hold, to seize. *Raʾfa* is thus pictured here as **an agent**: something that takes hold of a man and binds his hand.

**The sentence does not say "do not pity". It says "let your pity not take hold of you".** The feeling is not forbidden; the feeling's stopping the act is forbidden.

I record this distinction as **my own reading**; its ground is the form of the verb: *akhadha* — to take hold.

### فِى دِينِ ٱللَّهِ — where the qualification stands

**This is the part of the sentence most easily passed over: *fī dīni'llāh* — "in the religion of God".**

The prohibition is not absolute but **qualified.** It is not said "show no pity anywhere": it is said where **the application of a determined ruling** is at issue.

**And this qualification joins with *faraḍnāhā* in the first verse.** What has been determined stays as the one who determined it determined it. *Raʾfa* is free in the domains not determined; in what has been determined it cannot alter the measure.

### إِن كُنتُمْ تُؤْمِنُونَ بِٱللَّهِ وَٱلْيَوْمِ ٱلْءَاخِرِ — the position of the condition

**The conditional clause is placed at a noteworthy point.** Not after the command of a hundred strokes, but after the qualification **that pity must not stop the ruling.**

The condition, that is, is tied not to the carrying out of the penalty but to **the feeling's not disturbing the measure.**

**And the particular naming of the Last Day carries a logic:** the inability to bear that a ruling should give pain arises from a gaze that stays **within this world.** The mention of the hereafter lengthens the range of the gaze.

I record this as **my own reading**; the position of the condition and its content are the wording of the text.

### وَلْيَشْهَدْ عَذَابَهُمَا طَآئِفَةٌ مِّنَ ٱلْمُؤْمِنِينَ — the qualification of openness

**طَآئِفَة — root ط-و-ف: to turn, to go round about a thing.** *Ṭawāf* is from the same root. A *ṭāʾifa* is the **group** that goes round a place, that is present there. The word was analysed at `049-hucurat.md`, 49/9 (*ṭāʾifatāni mina'l-muʾminīn*); I rely on that treatment. The note there: the word **states no number**, it conveys an indefinite group; in the classical sources broad disagreement is transmitted about its minimum.

**And the function of the command here needs dwelling on, because it appears to stand in tension with the sūra as a whole.**

From beginning to end the sūra commands **covering**: do not enter without leave (27), lower your glance (30-31), do not spread what you have heard (15), bind an accusation to four witnesses (4). **But here the very opposite is asked: that the penalty be seen.**

**The explanations widely transmitted in the classical commentaries about the purpose of this command are these:**

| Explanation | Its content |
|---|---|
| **Deterrence** | A penalty that is seen has more effect than one heard of |
| **Oversight of the one who punishes** | The application is not hidden but done before witnesses; the measure cannot be exceeded |
| **The public declaring of the ruling** | That the matter should not stay at the level of gossip but be closed |

**I impose no preference.** But the second explanation's link with what precedes the verse may be recorded: the preceding clause regulated the feeling of the one who applies the penalty (*raʾfa*). **The verse thus limits the applier both from softening and from exceeding the measure: the one by a condition, the other by a witness.**

I record this as **my own reading**.

**And there is one further detail in the wording:** in saying *"let them witness their punishment"* the verse says *ʿadhābahumā* — "their punishment". The word *jazāʾ* or *ḥadd* is not used. The weight of the word is not softened.

### The disagreements in classical jurisprudence — transmitted, not preferred

The juristic discussions arising from this verse are broad. **The table below is meant to show the main divisions transmitted in the classical sources. None of them is this text's preference and none is binding. In the detail there are differences of view within each school as well.**

| Question | The main directions transmitted |
|---|---|
| **The case of the married person** | The great majority of the classical juristic tradition has set the ruling on the married person (*muḥṣan*) apart, grounding it on the sunna. **Against this, views holding that the ruling is only the penalty in the verse are also transmitted in the history of Islamic thought**, and this is a question debated in the works of theology and of legal theory. |
| **Whether banishment (*taghrīb*) is to be added** | Some jurists have said that a year's banishment is added to the flogging; others have counted this not a required additional penalty but a matter left to the ruler's discretion (*taʿzīr*). About women there has been further disagreement. |
| **The way the offence is established** | Four witnesses, or confession. Broad disagreement is transmitted over the stringency of the conditions of witness and over whether a confession may be withdrawn. |
| **The number of the group that is to witness** | There is more than one view about the least number the word *ṭāʾifa* conveys; different numbers, beginning from one, are transmitted in the sources. |

**This table is not a legal opinion and cannot be read as a guide to practice.** The reason it is placed here is the method's rule about disagreement: the disagreement is not concealed, but no preference is imposed.

**And one further thing must be recorded:** the fourth verse comes immediately after this one and binds **the accusation itself** to a sanction. The sūra, that is, in laying down the penalty also closes the road that leads to it. This is the most important move in the sūra's arrangement, and it will be taken up in detail at the fourth verse.

### What this says to the present

What this verse says to the present can be written without entering the debate about penalties and without scolding the reader; what is treated are **the words.**

**One. The general logic of the *raʾfa* qualification.**

The distinction the verse draws — the separating of feeling from measure — is a distinction that works outside the application of a ruling too. When a measure is laid down, what breaks it is most often not ill will but **a momentary softening.** The verse does not name this a bad thing; its name is *raʾfa*, and the word is used in the Qurʾān of God as well. All that is said is this: **a measure that has been determined is not re-determined according to feeling.**

**Two. The witness qualification works in two directions.**

A sanction applied in the open makes the one who applies it visible too. **A penalty applied in secret is a penalty whose measure cannot be checked.** The verse's *ṭāʾifa* qualification can be read for the side of it that secures this — and this reading agrees with what the sūra's later verses repeat: this sūra is from beginning to end about **the checking of claims and of power.**

**Three. And a limit.**

From this verse no conclusion follows that "nothing ought to remain hidden"; the rest of the sūra says exactly the reverse. The verse regulates **what comes after a ruling has been given.** The stage **before** the ruling — suspicion, accusation, gossip — will be closed off in the sternest terms in the sūra's later verses.

---

## 24/3 — ٱلزَّانِى لَا يَنكِحُ إِلَّا زَانِيَةً أَوْ مُشْرِكَةً وَٱلزَّانِيَةُ لَا يَنكِحُهَآ إِلَّا زَانٍ أَوْ مُشْرِكٌ وَحُرِّمَ ذَٰلِكَ عَلَى ٱلْمُؤْمِنِينَ

*Al-zānī lā yankiḥu illā zāniyatan aw mushrikatan wa'l-zāniyatu lā yankiḥuhā illā zānin aw mushrik, wa ḥurrima dhālika ʿala'l-muʾminīn*

"The man who commits adultery marries none but a woman who commits adultery or a woman who associates; and the woman who commits adultery is married by none but a man who commits adultery or a man who associates. This has been forbidden to the believers."

### The verse's place in point of order

**In the second verse the order began with the woman; here it begins with the man.** Two verses in succession, in two different orders.

**This sets up a balance in the wording, and I record it as an observation:** the pair of verses makes it hard to draw any superiority or precedence from which party is named first. The order changes between the two verses.

### The disagreement over the kind of sentence

**This verse is one of the places in the Qurʾān most disputed as to the kind of sentence it is**, and the source of the disagreement is this: the sentence is built **in the indicative**, but at its end there stands a **prohibiting** clause (*wa ḥurrima dhālika*).

| Reading | The kind of sentence | Its sense |
|---|---|---|
| **1** | **A statement** — the description of a state of affairs | One who does this act in fact comes together with such a person; the verse describes a fact |
| **2** | **A performative** — a ruling | This is a prohibition of marriage, and the *ḥurrima* at the end says so |
| **3** | **A rebuke** — a putting-down | The sentence is one of style, framed to devalue the act |
| **4** | **Belonging to a limited case** | Revealed about a particular situation, and conveying no general ruling |

**This disagreement takes up wide space in the classical works of commentary and of jurisprudence.** Among the schools too there is division over the marriage ruling drawn from this verse: some jurists have drawn a binding prohibition of marriage from it, while the majority have not taken it so and have explained the verse in another way.

**I make no preference, I issue no legal ruling, and none of these is binding.** I content myself with recording that the disagreement exists.

### نَكَحَ — root ن-ك-ح

The sense the lexicographers record: **one thing's joining with another, interlocking with it.** *Tanākaḥati'l-ashjār* — the branches of the trees interlaced. The word is used both of the contract of marriage and of union, and occurs in the Qurʾān in both senses.

**Which sense is meant in the verse is part of the disagreement above.** That the word carries two senses is one of the reasons the sentence can be read in two ways.

### The function of the verse in the arrangement

**What the sentence does is to put both parties in the same scale.**

The verse consists of two clauses, and the two are like **each other's reflection in a mirror**:

| | Subject | Predicate | Limit |
|---|---|---|---|
| **The first** | *al-zānī* | *lā yankiḥu* | *illā zāniyatan aw mushrikatan* |
| **The second** | *al-zāniya* | *lā yankiḥuhā* | *illā zānin aw mushrikun* |

**The symmetry is exact.** And the work of the symmetry becomes visible when it is read together with the sūra's later verses: in this sūra there is no burden laid on one side alone. In the second verse the penalty was bound equally to both parties; here the description is bound equally to both; in the sixth verse the oath of *liʿān* will be left equal in number for both.

**I record this as my own reading;** the symmetry itself is the wording of the text and can be counted.

### A note on style

**To use this verse as an instrument for ranking people runs against the sūra's own logic.**

The reason lies within the sūra: the fourth verse is the verse that binds the naming of someone with this attribute — unless four witnesses are brought — **to a penalty.** The sūra, that is, takes as its addressee, one verse later, the man who picks up the language of the third verse and fastens it to a person.

This is the same measure recorded in `049-hucurat.md`: **the text is an instrument of diagnosis, and the diagnosis is applied first to oneself.**

---

## 24/4 — وَٱلَّذِينَ يَرْمُونَ ٱلْمُحْصَنَٰتِ ثُمَّ لَمْ يَأْتُوا۟ بِأَرْبَعَةِ شُهَدَآءَ فَٱجْلِدُوهُمْ ثَمَٰنِينَ جَلْدَةً وَلَا تَقْبَلُوا۟ لَهُمْ شَهَٰدَةً أَبَدًا وَأُو۟لَٰٓئِكَ هُمُ ٱلْفَٰسِقُونَ

*Wa'lladhīna yarmūna'l-muḥṣanāti thumma lam yaʾtū bi-arbaʿati shuhadāʾa fa'jlidūhum thamānīna jaldatan wa lā taqbalū lahum shahādatan abadā, wa ulāʾika humu'l-fāsiqūn*

"And those who cast a charge at chaste women and then do not bring four witnesses — flog them eighty strokes, and never again accept any testimony from them. It is they who are the transgressors."

### The most important point in the sūra's arrangement

**The place of this verse explains the whole weave of Sūrat al-Nūr, and this is the observation about arrangement most dwelt on in this section.**

The second verse laid down a penalty for an offence. **The third framed a sentence about the parties to that offence. And the fourth bound the ascribing of that offence to someone to a penalty.**

The order, then, is this:

| Verse | What is done |
|---|---|
| **2** | A penalty is laid down for the act |
| **3** | A sentence of ruling is framed about the act |
| **4** | **Ascribing the act to someone** is bound to a penalty |
| **5** | A door of repentance is opened to the one who ascribed it |

**The meaning of this order is this: immediately after forbidding an offence, the text forbids the use of that offence's *name* as a weapon.**

**And to see how unusual this is, it is enough to look at the general structure of legal texts.** A penal norm generally defines the act and lays down its sanction. **The binding of the accusation itself to a sanction is a further and a distinct step** — because it takes as its subject the misuse of the penal order itself.

**The balance the verse sets up is visible in the numbers too:**

| | Act | Penalty |
|---|---|---|
| **Verse 2** | Adultery | **A hundred** strokes |
| **Verse 4** | An unproved charge | **Eighty** strokes |

**The penalty for slander, then, is four-fifths of the penalty for the act charged.** The number is smaller — but it is on the same scale and of the same kind, and to it is added **a permanent loss of a right** (the rejection of testimony).

**I record this as my own reading, and its ground is the numbers themselves:** the verse places an unproved accusation not as a light fault but as something **comparable in weight with the offence itself.**

**And one further observation:** in the second verse *raʾfa* (pity) was forbidden. In the fourth there is no such qualification. What comes in its place is **the exception of repentance** (5). The qualifications around the two rulings look in different directions: the one prevents the application from slackening, the other opens what comes after it has been applied.

### يَرْمُونَ — to throw

**Root: ر-م-ي.** Its concrete sense is **to throw, to hurl** — a stone, an arrow, an object.

**And this is the most concrete picture in the language of the verse.** The verse does not say "they slander", "they lie", "they accuse". **It says "they throw".**

The derivatives of the root:

| Word | Sense |
|---|---|
| **رَمَىٰ** (*ramā*) | He threw, he hurled |
| **رَمْي** (*ramy*) | A throwing |
| **مِرْمَاة** | The thing thrown; a target |

**The Qurʾān uses the same verb of an arrow and of a stone:** *wa mā ramayta idh ramayta wa lākinna'llāha ramā* (al-Anfāl 8/17) — there too what is thrown is something concrete.

**The picture carries three things, and all three are a description of slander:**

1. **What is thrown goes from a distance.** The thrower does not touch the target; he strikes with a word.
2. **What is thrown cannot be recalled.** Once out of the hand, the thrower's control is over.
3. **What is thrown leaves a mark.** A sign stays where it struck.

**And this picture goes on living in language:** in English too one speaks of *hurling* an accusation, of *slinging* mud, of *throwing* dirt at a man. The same verb, the same picture. I record this as an observation about the language.

**The verb's being used without a stated object is also worth noting:** *yarmūna'l-muḥṣanāt* — "they throw at chaste women". **What they throw is not said.** In Arabic, when this verb is left absolute in such a context, the content of the charge is understood from the context. **The text does not repeat the charge itself.**

I record this as an observation about the word order, and I add this: **the verse does not itself utter the words it forbids.**

### ٱلْمُحْصَنَٰت — those who are protected

**Root: ح-ص-ن.** This root was analysed at `059-hasr.md`, 59/2 (*ḥuṣūn* — fortresses); I rely on that treatment. The definition there: **to make firm, to take into protection, to make unreachable.**

| Word | Sense |
|---|---|
| **حِصْن** (*ḥiṣn*) | A fortress — a place not entered from outside |
| **مُحْصَن / مُحْصَنَة** | Protected, taken into protection |
| **تَحْصِين** | The making firm |
| **حَصَان** | A chaste woman; and a horse of good breed |

**The choice of the word describes what slander does.** A *muḥṣana* is, in the root of the word, one who is **protected like a fortress.** And slander is the **throwing** of something over that wall (*yarmūna*).

**The picture the two words build together is this: the piercing of an area protected from within by something thrown from outside.**

**I record this as my own reading;** the concrete senses of the two roots stand in the dictionary.

**And this picture is the same as the picture recorded in `049-hucurat.md`.** There, at 49/4, there were those who called out from behind the wall: the wall stood in its place but was crossed by a voice. Here too the fortress stands in its place but is crossed **by a word thrown.**

**There is disagreement over the scope of the word, and it needs to be transmitted:**

| Question | The views transmitted |
|---|---|
| **Whether the ruling covers men as well** | The verse uses the feminine plural. The great majority of the classical juristic tradition has counted the ruling valid for men too; language, analogy and context are given as grounds. |
| **Which attributes *muḥṣana* covers** | Broad disagreement is transmitted over which of the conditions — marriage, freedom, chastity, sanity and majority, being Muslim — the word contains. |

**I transmit these as views that are transmitted; I make no preference, and none is binding.**

### بِأَرْبَعَةِ شُهَدَآءَ — four witnesses

**Here is the heart of the verse, and its place in the arrangement must be recorded.**

**The condition is not to prove the slander — it is to prove *the charge*.** The verse is built thus: *yarmūna … **thumma** lam yaʾtū bi-arbaʿati shuhadāʾ*. **The penalty, that is, comes not because the charge has been proved false, but because the charge has not been proved true.**

**This shows where the burden of proof is placed: on the one who makes the claim.**

**And the particle *thumma* is worth noting.** Not *wa* but *thumma* — "then". In Arabic this particle conveys that **time has come in between.** The one who throws the charge is thus given a span in which to bring his witnesses; the ruling is framed not immediately after the charge but after the chance of proof has gone unused.

I record this as an observation about the word order.

**That the number is four:** in no other matter does the Qurʾān require four witnesses. The general ruling on witness at `002-bakara.md`, 2/282, is two men. **This, then, is the heaviest condition of proof in the Qurʾān.**

**And the consequence of this weight is this, and it must be said openly: the condition is so heavy that in practice it makes proof all but impossible.** In the classical works of jurisprudence this weight has been dwelt on separately, and the conditions of the witnessing (seeing the same event, at the same moment, in the same manner) have been kept extremely narrow.

**In the classical sources a widespread conviction is transmitted that this is the result of an intention:** the purpose of the ruling is that the act, **so long as it stays hidden**, should not be pursued and should not be brought before the community. I transmit this conviction as a view that is transmitted.

**And the verses' own arrangement agrees with that conviction, and I record this as my own reading:** the nineteenth verse will announce a heavy recompense for *"those who love that indecency should **spread** among those who believe."* **The sūra, that is, holds a line that aims not at the act but at the publishing of the act**, and the condition of four witnesses is the first link in that line.

### فَٱجْلِدُوهُمْ ثَمَٰنِينَ جَلْدَةً — the penalty

**The same root: ج-ل-د.** The same verb as in the second verse, the same form, the same specifier.

**The sūra's two penalties are expressed with the same word.** This is a fact of the arrangement, and it shows this: the text places slander not as a separate category but as **a violation of the same kind.**

### وَلَا تَقْبَلُوا۟ لَهُمْ شَهَٰدَةً أَبَدًا — the second sanction

**This is the second and more lasting half of the penalty, and its logic stands within itself.**

The man had come forward **with a claim to have witnessed**: he had said "I saw". And he could not support his claim with four witnesses. **The result is the loss of the competence to bear witness.**

**The sanction, then, is chosen from the kind of the violation itself.** The weight of the word of the man who has misused his word is taken away.

**I record this as my own reading;** its ground is that the word in the two clauses is from the same root: *shuhadāʾ* (witnesses) and *shahādatan* (testimony). **The verse uses the same root in both the condition and the penalty.**

**أَبَدًا** — "never, for ever". The word conveys that the sanction is without term. **And the fifth verse will come directly upon this word.**

### وَأُو۟لَٰٓئِكَ هُمُ ٱلْفَٰسِقُونَ — the third sanction

**فَاسِق — root ف-س-ق.** This root was analysed in detail at `049-hucurat.md`, 49/6; I rely on that treatment. The note there was this: the concrete sense of the root is **a date's coming out of its skin**; *fisq* is not the name of a sin but the name of a **position** — a thing's being outside where it ought to be inside.

**And the place of the word here forms a ring with the one in al-Ḥujurāt:**

| Verse | Who is *fāsiq* | What it does |
|---|---|---|
| **al-Ḥujurāt 49/6** | The one who **brings** the report | A position that requires the report to be verified |
| **al-Nūr 24/4** | The one who **cannot prove** the charge | A position in which the weight of his word is lost |

**The two verses look at the same matter with the same word:** the carrier of an unverified word. al-Ḥujurāt regulated him from the side of **the receiver** (*fa-tabayyanū* — verify); al-Nūr regulates him from the side of **the source.**

**I record this complementarity as my own reading**, and at the end of the sūra I shall give in a separate table how the two sūras complete each other.

**And the work the three sanctions do together:**

| Sanction | What it touches |
|---|---|
| Eighty strokes | **The body** |
| The rejection of testimony | **Social standing** |
| The name *fāsiq* | **Religious standing** |

All three together reduce **the weight in every domain** of the man who uses his word without measure.

### What this says to the present

**One. That the accusation is itself an act.**

The ground the verse lays down is this: **to charge a man with something is an act.** Not the expression of an opinion, not a guess, not a question. And because it is an act it has consequences.

This is the distinction most often lost in everyday speech. The man who puts a claim about most often sees himself only as "passing it on", "saying it", "conveying what he heard". **The verse's verb does not recognise that position: *yarmūn* — they throw.**

**Two. Where the burden of proof lies.**

The verse does not ask the accused to prove the charge false. **It asks the accuser to prove the charge true.**

This distinction points in the same direction as the logic of what is called in legal thought the presumption of innocence. **This is not a claim of identity** — what the verse builds is not a procedure of trial but a regulation of accusation. But what the two structures have in common is this: **a claim that cannot be proved is not counted proved; and the maker of an unproved claim pays a price.**

**Three. The meaning of one qualification: *abadā*.**

The permanent rejection of testimony looks very heavy at first. And it is heavy. **But the fifth verse comes directly after and opens a door upon that weight.** To read the two verses apart is to read both of them wrongly.

---

## 24/5 — إِلَّا ٱلَّذِينَ تَابُوا۟ مِنۢ بَعْدِ ذَٰلِكَ وَأَصْلَحُوا۟ فَإِنَّ ٱللَّهَ غَفُورٌ رَّحِيمٌ

*Illa'lladhīna tābū min baʿdi dhālika wa aṣlaḥū fa-inna'llāha ghafūrun raḥīm*

"Except those who repent after that and set things right. God is truly forgiving, merciful."

### What the exception attaches to — one of the most famous debates in legal theory

This short verse is the most cited example of a debate that has a name of its own in the Islamic tradition of legal theory: **when an exception follows several clauses, does it apply to all of them or only to the last?**

In the fourth verse there were three rulings:

1. Eighty strokes
2. That testimony is never to be accepted
3. The name *fāsiq*

**And the fifth verse begins with *illā*. The question is: which of these does the exception apply to?**

| View | What the exception applies to | The result |
|---|---|---|
| **1** | **Only to the last** (the name *fāsiq*) | The man who repents is not counted a *fāsiq*; but his testimony is still not accepted |
| **2** | **To the last two** (testimony + *fāsiq*) | The man who repents has both the name lifted and his testimony accepted |

**The first view is grounded on the rule that in Arabic an exception following clauses that come one after another applies to the *nearest* of them.** The second is grounded on the rule that the exception applies to **all the clauses**, and appeal is also made to the general ruling on repentance.

**This division is a question of legal theory over which the schools differ, and it is debated in the classical works of jurisprudence by way of this verse.** In this section **no preference is made, no legal ruling is issued, and none of these is binding.**

**But what the two views have in common may be recorded:** both agree that **the penalty itself** (the eighty strokes) falls outside the scope of the exception. Repentance, that is, does not retroactively lift a sanction that has been laid down. Where they part is over whether **the lasting part of the sanction** is lifted.

### Two verbs: تَابُوا۟ and أَصْلَحُوا۟

**The condition of the verse is not one verb but two, and both are worth noting.**

**تَابَ — root ت-و-ب.** Its concrete sense is **to turn, to turn back.** The word was taken up in `066-tahrim.md` and at `049-hucurat.md`, 49/12. *Tawba* is to turn from one direction and pass into another; it is the name not of a feeling but of **a movement.**

**أَصْلَحَ — root ص-ل-ح.** The root was analysed at `049-hucurat.md`, 49/9-10; I rely on that treatment. The note there: *iṣlāḥ* is **to repair what has been spoiled, to make it workable again.** The word conveys not an intention but **a work.**

**The coming of the two verbs together fixes the content of the condition:**

| Verb | Its direction | What it requires |
|---|---|---|
| ***tābū*** | Inward, upward — a turning | A change of direction |
| ***aṣlaḥū*** | Outward — repair | **The setting right of what was spoiled** |

**And without the second verb the first is not counted sufficient.** This is the most concrete note in the verse's arrangement: slander has left **a damage outside**, and turning alone does not remove that damage.

**In the classical works of jurisprudence it has been debated what this second verb means** — whether it is retracting his word, or setting his own condition right, or remaining upon the straight road for a long time. **I content myself with transmitting this disagreement; I make no preference.**

**The same pair occurred in `049-hucurat.md` too, in the discussion of repentance for backbiting**, and a similar division was recorded there as well: in repenting for the violation of another's right, what is to become of the party violated.

### مِنۢ بَعْدِ ذَٰلِكَ — "after that"

**The demonstrative is worth noting: *dhālika* — "that".** What it points to is not said openly: the slander, the penalty, or both at once?

**In the classical commentaries all three possibilities are mentioned.** The leaving of the reference indefinite leaves indefinite — that is, **wide** — the stage at which the door of repentance stands open.

I record this as an observation about the word order.

### فَإِنَّ ٱللَّهَ غَفُورٌ رَّحِيمٌ — the choice of the verse-ending

**The sūra's first verse-ending comes here, and it is a verse-ending of forgiveness.**

The sūra had opened with two heavy penal rulings. **The first pair of divine names comes after those rulings, and inside a note about repentance.**

**غَفُور** — root **غ-ف-ر**: to cover. *Mighfar* is from the same root: what covers the head and protects it. The root's sense of "covering" was recorded in `047-muhammed.md`, where it was compared in a table with the other roots of covering.

**And the word's place here is worth noting:** immediately after penalising a man's laying bare **another's** fault, the sūra closes with the name of God that **covers.**

**I record this as my own reading;** its ground is that the root of the word *ghafūr* is "to cover" and that the subject of the verse is "laying bare". The two words stand face to face in the same pair of verses.

### What this says to the present

**One. A door set beside a permanent sanction.**

The fourth verse said *abadā* — "never". The fifth says *illā* — "except". **One verse later, a sanction without term is bound to a condition.**

This has a general logic, and it may be recorded thus: **the permanent identification of a man with a single fault is not absolute even within the order the text itself establishes.** There is a sanction, it is heavy, and it has a door.

**Two. And the condition of the door: not remorse alone, but repair.**

The verse asks for two verbs. In today's terms this corresponds to something like this: to retract a claim is not the same thing as never to have put it about. The word has gone into circulation. **The verse's second verb (*aṣlaḥū*) means the work to be done where the word left off.**

**Three. A limit: this verse is not an instrument for settling accounts.**

To use the verse in order to measure whether another man's repentance is valid runs against the whole logic of the sūra. The verse binds the condition of the one who repents **to God**: *fa-inna'llāha ghafūrun raḥīm.*

---

## 24/6-7 — وَٱلَّذِينَ يَرْمُونَ أَزْوَٰجَهُمْ وَلَمْ يَكُن لَّهُمْ شُهَدَآءُ إِلَّآ أَنفُسُهُمْ فَشَهَٰدَةُ أَحَدِهِمْ أَرْبَعُ شَهَٰدَٰتٍۭ بِٱللَّهِ إِنَّهُۥ لَمِنَ ٱلصَّٰدِقِينَ · وَٱلْخَٰمِسَةُ أَنَّ لَعْنَتَ ٱللَّهِ عَلَيْهِ إِن كَانَ مِنَ ٱلْكَٰذِبِينَ

*Wa'lladhīna yarmūna azwājahum wa lam yakun lahum shuhadāʾu illā anfusuhum fa-shahādatu aḥadihim arbaʿu shahādātin bi'llāhi innahū la-mina'l-ṣādiqīn · Wa'l-khāmisatu anna laʿnata'llāhi ʿalayhi in kāna mina'l-kādhibīn*

"And those who cast a charge at their wives and have no witnesses but themselves — the testimony of such a man is four testimonies sworn by God that he is of the truthful. And the fifth, that the curse of God be upon him if he is of the liars."

### A note: only the structure and the language are treated here

***Liʿān* (لِعَان) is a subject of its own in classical jurisprudence, and there is broad disagreement among the schools about its consequences.** In this section **no juristic detail is entered into and no ruling is issued.** What will be treated is the **structure** and the **language** of the verses.

### The verse's link with the fourth

**It opens with the same verb: *yarmūn* — they throw.** The very verb of the fourth verse.

**But the condition changes:** in the fourth verse four witnesses were required; here there is the qualification *wa lam yakun lahum shuhadāʾu illā anfusuhum* — "if they have no witnesses but themselves".

| | 24/4 | 24/6 |
|---|---|---|
| Verb | *yarmūn* | *yarmūn* |
| Object | *al-muḥṣanāt* — general | *azwājahum* — **their wives** |
| The way of proof | **Four witnesses** | **Four oaths** |
| If it cannot be proved | Eighty strokes | **The other party swears too** |

**The verse thus sets apart the one case in which the heavy condition of proof laid down in the fourth verse cannot be applied**, and there it puts an oath in the place of a witness.

**That the number is kept is worth noting: four.** The number of witnesses was four; the number of oaths is four as well. **The text does not change the weight required for proof — it changes only its kind.**

I record this as an observation about the word order.

### The structure itself: four plus one

**The structure of *liʿān* is the same for both parties, and this symmetry is the most striking side of the verses:**

| | The man (6-7) | The woman (8-9) |
|---|---|---|
| **Number of oaths** | Four | Four |
| **Their content** | *innahū la-mina'l-ṣādiqīn* — that he is of the truthful | *innahū la-mina'l-kādhibīn* — that he is of the liars |
| **The fifth** | *laʿnatu'llāhi ʿalayh* — **the curse** | *ghaḍabu'llāhi ʿalayhā* — **the anger** |
| **Its condition** | *in kāna mina'l-kādhibīn* | *in kāna mina'l-ṣādiqīn* |

**The symmetry is all but exact, and the only difference lies in the word of the fifth oath: *laʿna* and *ghaḍab*.**

**لَعْنَة — root ل-ع-ن:** to drive away, to expel. A *laʿna* is a being driven far from mercy — at the centre of the word stands **distance.**

**غَضَب — root غ-ض-ب:** anger. At the centre of the word stands **a turning towards.**

**The classical commentaries have met the difference between the two words with several explanations, and I prefer none of them:**

| Explanation | Its content |
|---|---|
| **1** | There is no marked difference of degree between the two words in point of meaning; the verse-ending and the harmony of sound have been observed |
| **2** | The words have to do with the difference in the position of the two parties |
| **3** | *Ghaḍab* is counted the heavier, and this has to do with the second party's being the one who speaks last |

**These are transmitted explanations; I impose no preference, and none is binding.**

### فَشَهَٰدَةُ أَحَدِهِمْ أَرْبَعُ شَهَٰدَٰتٍ — a point of word order

**The sentence does not say "let him swear four times". It says "his testimony is four testimonies".**

**The oath, that is, is named with the word *shahāda*.** What was required in the fourth verse was *shuhadāʾ* (witnesses); what is required here is *shahādāt* (testimonies).

**The same root, in two different forms:** the one a **person** (four separate human beings), the other an **act** (four separate declarations).

**I record this as my own reading, and its ground is the form of the words:** the text puts a man's own declaration in the place of four separate testimonies, and does so **without changing the word.** The oath is placed here not as an expression of feeling but as **a procedural act.**

### What the verses do

**What this regulation does is to rescue both parties from a deadlock.**

The condition of the fourth verse — four witnesses — is in practice all but impossible for a husband. **Had the condition been applied as it stands, two results would follow:** either the claimant would receive eighty strokes for failing to prove his claim, or the claim could never be voiced at all.

**The verse opens a third way:** the claim is stated within a procedural frame, the other party answers within a procedural frame of the same weight, and the matter is closed **by leaving the truth of the oaths to God.**

***Liʿān*, then, is not a procedure of proof but a procedure of *exit*.** Which of the two is telling the truth is not settled by a ruling.

**I record this as my own reading;** its ground is that the oaths of both parties are qualified by a conditional clause (*in kāna*) — that is, the text does not itself announce which of the two is telling the truth.

**Over the juristic consequences — the state of the marriage, paternity, the possibility of coming together again, the ruling where one refuses to swear — there is broad disagreement among the schools. None of it is transmitted in this section and no ruling is issued.**

---

## 24/8-9 — وَيَدْرَؤُا۟ عَنْهَا ٱلْعَذَابَ أَن تَشْهَدَ أَرْبَعَ شَهَٰدَٰتٍۭ بِٱللَّهِ إِنَّهُۥ لَمِنَ ٱلْكَٰذِبِينَ · وَٱلْخَٰمِسَةَ أَنَّ غَضَبَ ٱللَّهِ عَلَيْهَآ إِن كَانَ مِنَ ٱلصَّٰدِقِينَ

*Wa yadraʾu ʿanha'l-ʿadhāba an tashhada arbaʿa shahādātin bi'llāhi innahū la-mina'l-kādhibīn · Wa'l-khāmisata anna ghaḍaba'llāhi ʿalayhā in kāna mina'l-ṣādiqīn*

"And it averts the punishment from her that she testify four testimonies sworn by God that he is of the liars. And the fifth, that the anger of God be upon her if he is of the truthful."

### وَيَدْرَؤُا۟ — "it averts"

**Root: د-ر-أ.** Its concrete sense is **to push, to thrust back, to repel.** *Daraʾa* — to push a thing away. From the same root come *dirʾ* (in the sense of pushing) and *tadāruʾ*.

**The choice of the verb is worth recording.** The verse does not say "the woman too swears"; it says **"it pushes the punishment away from her".**

**The work of the woman's oath, that is, is not to prove a claim — it is to thrust back something that is coming.** The verb is chosen from the field of defence.

**And this fixes the position the verse gives to the two parties:**

| Party | Verb | Its direction |
|---|---|---|
| **The one who charges** | *yarmūn* (ر-م-ي) | **To throw** — forward |
| **The one charged** | *yadraʾu* (د-ر-أ) | **To push** — backward |

**The two verbs run in opposite directions, and both are physical.** I record this as an observation about the language; the concrete senses of the two roots stand in the dictionary.

### ٱلْعَذَاب — which punishment?

**The verse does not say which punishment it means in saying "the punishment"**, and this is a point debated in classical jurisprudence. **That debate is not entered in this section.**

What can be recorded is a linguistic observation: the word is the same word as *ʿadhābahumā* in the second verse. **The sūra names the punishment always with the same word.**

### وَٱلْخَٰمِسَةَ — a difference of case

**The readings *al-khāmisatu* (nominative) in the seventh verse and *al-khāmisata* (accusative) in the ninth are recorded in the sources, and this difference between the two verses is taken up in the works on the variant readings.**

That the difference does not change the meaning, and consists only in a choice of case within the construction of the sentence, is recorded in the classical sources. **Since I cannot state with certainty which reader read which, I write no names.**

### The completion of the symmetry

**The eighth and ninth verses repeat, word for word, the structure of the sixth and seventh.** Only three things change: the subject, the word of the fifth oath (*laʿna* / *ghaḍab*), and the direction of the conditional clause.

**This repetition is a wholeness rarely seen in the Qurʾān's verses of ruling, and by itself it says something: the procedural position of the two parties is one and the same.**

I record this as an observation about the word order; the repetition itself is the wording of the text and can be compared.

---

## 24/10 — وَلَوْلَا فَضْلُ ٱللَّهِ عَلَيْكُمْ وَرَحْمَتُهُۥ وَأَنَّ ٱللَّهَ تَوَّابٌ حَكِيمٌ

*Wa law lā faḍlu'llāhi ʿalaykum wa raḥmatuhū wa anna'llāha tawwābun ḥakīm*

"And were it not for God's bounty towards you and His mercy, and that God is ever-relenting, wise…"

### A condition whose answer is not spoken

**The most striking side of this verse is that the sentence is left unfinished.**

*Law lā* — "were it not for" — is a particle of condition and calls for an answer: "were it not for it, **such and such would be**". **But the answer is not spoken.**

In Arabic this is called *ḥadhf jawāb al-sharṭ*, and the rule the lexicographers record is this: **the omission of the answer conveys the answer's greatness and its unspeakableness.** What is not said is heavier than what is said; the reader is asked to complete it himself.

**And this pattern will be repeated four times in the sūra:**

| Verse | Expression | The answer |
|---|---|---|
| **10** | *Wa law lā faḍlu'llāhi ʿalaykum wa raḥmatuh…* | **Not spoken** |
| **14** | *Wa law lā faḍlu'llāhi ʿalaykum wa raḥmatuhū fi'l-dunyā wa'l-ākhira…* | *La-massakum … ʿadhābun ʿaẓīm* — **spoken** |
| **20** | *Wa law lā faḍlu'llāhi ʿalaykum wa raḥmatuh…* | **Not spoken** |
| **21** | *Wa law lā faḍlu'llāhi ʿalaykum wa raḥmatuhū mā zakā minkum min aḥadin abadā* | **Spoken** |

**Four repetitions, balanced by two spoken and two unspoken answers.** I record this as an observation about the word order; the places of the repetitions can be counted in the text.

### The verse's place in the block

**This verse is the close of the second block (2-10) and serves as a seal.**

The block had opened with heavy rulings. **The close names the quality standing behind those rulings: *faḍl* (bounty), *raḥma*, *tawwāb*, *ḥakīm*.**

**تَوَّاب** — root **ت-و-ب**, an intensive form: **He who accepts turnings again and again.** That in Arabic the word can be used both of the servant and of God is worth noting: the servant "turns", God "accepts the turning" — the same verb, in two directions.

**حَكِيم** — root **ح-ك-م**: to judge; and the concrete sense of the root is **the bit set in an animal's mouth** (*ḥakama*), that is, **to rein in.** The root was named at `049-hucurat.md`, 49/8.

**And the name's place here is worth noting:** at the end of a block of rulings there comes a name **from the root of ruling.** The sūra seals the measures it has laid down with the name of the one who laid them.

### The block as a whole (2-10)

| Verse | What is regulated | Key word |
|---|---|---|
| **2** | A penalty for the act | *jalda*, *raʾfa*, *ṭāʾifa* |
| **3** | A sentence of ruling about the act | *nikāḥ* |
| **4** | **A penalty for the charge** | *yarmūn*, *arbaʿa shuhadāʾ* |
| **5** | A door of repentance for the one who charged | *tābū*, *aṣlaḥū* |
| **6-9** | A procedure of exit where proof is impossible | *shahādāt*, *yadraʾu* |
| **10** | The seal | *faḍl*, *raḥma*, *tawwāb*, *ḥakīm* |

**The logic of the block is this, and I record it as my own reading:** the text forbids an act, then forbids the use of that act's **name** as a weapon, then opens a way out for the one case in which proof is impossible, and at the end names repentance and mercy.

**The block, then, is not a series of penalties — it is a series of *limitations*.** What is limited, in order: the act, the charge, and the ruling itself.

**And what comes after the block will show why this logic is built so tightly:** in the eleventh verse it will be told how an accusation spreads within a community.

---

## 24/11 — إِنَّ ٱلَّذِينَ جَآءُو بِٱلْإِفْكِ عُصْبَةٌ مِّنكُمْ لَا تَحْسَبُوهُ شَرًّا لَّكُم بَلْ هُوَ خَيْرٌ لَّكُمْ لِكُلِّ ٱمْرِئٍ مِّنْهُم مَّا ٱكْتَسَبَ مِنَ ٱلْإِثْمِ وَٱلَّذِى تَوَلَّىٰ كِبْرَهُۥ مِنْهُمْ لَهُۥ عَذَابٌ عَظِيمٌ

*Inna'lladhīna jāʾū bi'l-ifki ʿuṣbatun minkum, lā taḥsabūhu sharran lakum, bal huwa khayrun lakum, li-kulli'mriʾin minhum ma'ktasaba mina'l-ithm, wa'lladhī tawallā kibrahū minhum lahū ʿadhābun ʿaẓīm*

"Those who brought the *ifk* are a band from among you. Do not think it an evil for you; rather it is a good for you. For each man among them is what he earned of the sin. And for the one among them who took upon himself the greater part of it there is a great punishment."

### First, a note: what is not narrated in this section

**The verses from eleven to twenty point to an event that is transmitted in detail in the sources.**

**In this section the details of the reports about that event are not transmitted, no personal name is given, and no discussion is entered about who was accused.**

The reason has two layers, and it needs to be written openly:

**One. The verses themselves give no name.** *Alladhīna jāʾū* — "those who brought". *ʿUṣbatun minkum* — "a band from among you". *Alladhī tawallā kibrahū* — "the one who took upon himself the greater part of it". **The text is nameless from beginning to end.** And the namelessness stands here not as a lack but as a choice: the verses close the matter in the twenty-sixth by saying *ulāʾika mubarraʾūna mimmā yaqūlūn* — "they are free of what is said".

**Two. This commentary's general practice.** The reports around an event diverge from one another at the level of detail; and, more importantly, **this sūra itself takes as its subject what it is to speak about named persons.** For a section to be written by doing the very thing the verse it treats forbids would be an inconsistency.

**What will be treated is the frame the verses themselves give:** how a report spreads, how language works, and how "thinking it a small thing" is diagnosed.

### ٱلْإِفْك — root أ-ف-ك

**The root was analysed at `046-ahkaf.md`, 46/11 and 46/28, and also at `029-ankebut.md`, 29/17 and 29/61; I rely on those treatments.**

The note there was this: **the concrete sense of the root is to turn a thing from its direction, to turn it inside out.** As was recorded in al-Aḥqāf: *"the word used for 'fabrication' means 'turned about'."* In al-ʿAnkabūt the root's use in two directions was given in a table: *takhluqūna ifkā* (active — they fabricate) and *fa-annā yuʾfakūn* (passive — they are turned about). The sentence there was this: *"what they fabricate becomes in the end the thing that turns them about."*

**And the choice of the word here uses exactly the picture this root carries.**

The verse does not say *kadhib* (a lie). The word *buhtān* will come in the sixteenth verse, but not here. **The word used here is *ifk*: the thing turned about.**

**The difference is this:**

| Word | Root | What it conveys |
|---|---|---|
| **كَذِب** (*kadhib*) | ك-ذ-ب | A word that does not answer to the truth |
| **إِفْك** (*ifk*) | أ-ف-ك | What has been **turned about** — its direction reversed |
| **بُهْتَان** (*buhtān*) | ب-ه-ت | A slander that leaves the hearer **stunned** |

***Ifk* is not the fabricating of a thing out of nothing — it is the turning inside out of something that exists.** That is the picture in the root: the bottom goes on top, the front to the back.

**And this picture describes how slander works.** A slander is generally not built from nothing; it is built **by turning the direction of real material.** An event happened, a person was seen somewhere, a word was said — and their direction is reversed.

**I record this as my own reading;** its ground is the concrete sense of the root, and that sense was recorded independently in `046-ahkaf.md`.

### عُصْبَة — a band bound together

**Root: ع-ص-ب.** Its concrete sense is **to bind tightly, to wrap.**

| Word | Sense |
|---|---|
| **عَصَبَ** (*ʿaṣaba*) | He bound tightly, he wrapped |
| **عَصَب** (*ʿaṣab*) | Nerve, sinew — the fibre that binds the body |
| **عِصَابَة** (*ʿiṣāba*) | A cloth wound round the head; and a group standing together |
| **عَصَبِيَّة** (*ʿaṣabiyya*) | Blind attachment to a group — the word English has taken over as "asabiyya" |
| **عُصْبَة** (*ʿuṣba*) | A band bound to one another |

**The choice of the word is one of the verse's most important diagnoses.**

The verse does not say *qawm* (a people), *farīq* (a party), *nās* (people) or *ṭāʾifa* (a group). **It says *ʿuṣba*: those bound to one another.**

**The spreading of a report, that is, is described not as the independent movement of scattered individuals but as the movement of *a bound band*.**

**The idea of "binding" in the root of the word tells the social mechanics of slander, and I record this as my own reading:** an accusation does not spread on its own. Among the people who carry it there is already a bond — a common circle, a common enmity, a common expectation. **And the report flows along that bond.**

**Moreover the same root gives the word *ʿaṣabiyya***, and this word conveys the taking as one's measure not a man's truthfulness but **whether he is on one's own side.** Since the two words are from the same root, I record this as an observation about the root and draw no ruling from it.

### مِّنكُمْ — "from among you"

**This is the heaviest word in the verse, and it consists of two letters.**

**Those who brought the slander are not from outside but from within.** And this is exactly the measure recorded in `049-hucurat.md`: there too the behaviours corrected were the behaviours of those who believe, and the address was turned inward upon them.

**This word settles from the outset how the verses are to be read.** To take up the section and use it to describe an outside group is to turn the direction of the address on its head.

### لَا تَحْسَبُوهُ شَرًّا لَّكُم بَلْ هُوَ خَيْرٌ لَّكُمْ — an unlooked-for sentence

**Here is the most unlooked-for place in the verse's arrangement.**

A slander that shakes a community is spoken of, and the text says this: **"do not think it an evil for you; rather it is a good for you."**

**Three notes must be entered so that the sentence is not misunderstood:**

**One. The sentence does not declare the slander a good thing.** At the end of the same verse it says *ʿadhābun ʿaẓīm* of those who spread it; in the nineteenth verse *ʿadhābun alīm*, in the twenty-third *luʿinū fi'l-dunyā wa'l-ākhira*. **The ruling about the act is of the utmost severity.**

**Two. To whom does the pronoun *lakum* refer?** The one addressed is not those who brought the slander — it is **the whole of the community.** By saying *lā taḥsabūhu* (a plural imperative) the verse opens the address to the community.

**Three. What is the "good"?** The verse does not say; but the following verses give an answer: **the whole of the verses from twelve to twenty is a lesson in method drawn from this event.** What is "good", then, is not the event itself — it is **the measure that comes after the event.**

**I record this as my own reading, and its ground is the arrangement of the sūra:** had the verses only meant to record an event, the verses from twelve to eighteen would not have been needed. The whole of those verses builds a **method.**

**And one further observation:** a similar sentence structure occurs at `002-bakara.md`, 2/216 — *wa ʿasā an takrahū shayʾan wa huwa khayrun lakum.* The two verses do the same work: the ruling upon an event is separated from the outcome of that event.

### لِكُلِّ ٱمْرِئٍ مِّنْهُم مَّا ٱكْتَسَبَ مِنَ ٱلْإِثْمِ — the dividing of shares

**This clause divides the responsibility within a group among individuals, and it is one of the places in the verse that must be read most carefully.**

**At the head of the eleventh verse a *group* had been described (*ʿuṣbatun*). And now the verse binds each person within that group separately to account: *li-kulli'mriʾin minhum* — "for every man among them".**

**The group, that is, does not dissolve responsibility.** A thing's being done in company does not free a man of his own share.

**ٱكْتَسَبَ — root ك-س-ب, Form VIII.** The root was recorded in `042-sura.md`. **And the difference of form is a point the classical lexicographers dwell on:**

| Form | Verb | The tendency the lexicographers record |
|---|---|---|
| **Triliteral** | *kasaba* | Earning in general |
| **Form VIII** | *iktasaba* | Earning **that involves effort and intent** |

**I give this distinction as a tendency the lexicographers record; that it is not an absolute rule is also stated in the sources.** But that the form in the verse is Form VIII is worth recording: the carrying of a report is placed as something a man does **by his own effort.**

### وَٱلَّذِى تَوَلَّىٰ كِبْرَهُۥ — "the one who took upon himself the greater part"

**The verse sets up a difference of degree within the group.**

**تَوَلَّىٰ — root و-ل-ي:** to be near, to take upon oneself, to take a matter in hand. A *walī* is one who takes a matter in hand; a *wālī* is one who governs. **Here the sense is: the one who takes upon himself the largest part of a business.**

**كِبْر** — root **ك-ب-ر**: greatness. **Two readings of the word are recorded in the sources: *kibrahū* and *kubrahū*.** Both mean "the greatest share of it"; the difference is in the form. **Since I cannot state with certainty which reader read which, I write no names.**

**And the correspondence of this difference of degree in the arrangement is this:**

| Who | What he receives |
|---|---|
| Every member of the group | *ma'ktasaba mina'l-ithm* — **as much as he earned** |
| The one who took upon himself the greater part | *ʿadhābun ʿaẓīm* — **a great punishment** |

**The verse thus does two things at once:** it exempts no one from responsibility, but neither does it put everyone in the same place.

**I record this as my own reading, and its ground is that the two clauses stand side by side.** And I add this: this is the distinction most often lost when a report spreads. **The one who first says a word, the one who carries it insistently, the one who repeats it once and the one who listens in silence do not stand in the same place** — but none of them is entirely outside it either.

---

## 24/12 — لَّوْلَآ إِذْ سَمِعْتُمُوهُ ظَنَّ ٱلْمُؤْمِنُونَ وَٱلْمُؤْمِنَٰتُ بِأَنفُسِهِمْ خَيْرًا وَقَالُوا۟ هَٰذَآ إِفْكٌ مُّبِينٌ

*Law lā idh samiʿtumūhu ẓanna'l-muʾminūna wa'l-muʾminātu bi-anfusihim khayran wa qālū hādhā ifkun mubīn*

"When you heard it, ought not the believing men and the believing women to have thought well of their own, and to have said: 'This is a plain slander'?"

### لَوْلَا — the particle of reproach

**In Arabic *law lā* is used in two distinct functions, and the two must be kept apart:**
