| Function | What follows it | Sense |
|---|---|---|
| **Condition** (*law lā imtināʿiyya*) | A nominal clause | "were it not for…" — in verses 10, 14, 20, 21 |
| **Reproach, urging** (*law lā taḥḍīḍiyya*) | A verbal clause | **"ought you not to have…?"** — here and in verses 13 and 16 |

**And the second function, used of a past act, conveys a *reproach*:** you did not do what you ought to have done.

**The particle occurs seven times in the sūra and divides in two:**

| Reproach (*law lā* + verb) | Condition (*law lā* + noun) |
|---|---|
| **12** — ought you not to have thought well | **10** — were it not for God's bounty |
| **13** — ought they not to have brought four witnesses | **14** — were it not for God's bounty |
| **16** — ought you not to have said "it is not for us to speak of this" | **20** — were it not for God's bounty |
| | **21** — were it not for God's bounty |

**Three reproaches and four conditions, interlocked.** And the two series run in and out of each other: 10 (condition) → 12, 13 (reproach) → 14 (condition) → 16 (reproach) → 20, 21 (condition).

**I record this as an observation about the word order;** the places of the particle can be counted in the text. **And what the structure does is this:** beside every reproach a note of bounty is set. The text reproaches, but does not leave the reproach alone.

### إِذْ سَمِعْتُمُوهُ — "when you heard it"

**The note of time is worth remarking: *idh samiʿtumūh* — "the moment you heard it".**

**The verse binds what ought to have been done to a *moment*:** the first instant the report reaches the ear.

**And this is exactly where the variant reading at `049-hucurat.md`, 49/6, looks.** There the two readings were given in a table: *fa-tabayyanū* (verify) and *fa-tathabbatū* (hold your ground). And the note there was this: ***tathabbut* is the precondition of *tabayyun*** — verifying takes time, and to gain time one must halt.

**al-Nūr 24/12 says what is to be done in that moment of halting.**

### ظَنَّ … بِأَنفُسِهِمْ خَيْرًا — "to think well of their own"

**This is the expression in the verse that most needs dwelling on, and it carries a difficulty of translation.**

Literally: "let the believing men and the believing women think good **about their own selves**".

**And there are two readings of what *bi-anfusihim* means:**

| Reading | To whom *anfusihim* refers | The sense of the clause |
|---|---|---|
| **1** | **To one another** — "themselves" means the members of the community | "Ought you not to have thought well of one another?" |
| **2** | **To their own selves** — each member about his own side | "Ought you not to have thought of your own community as of yourself?" |

**The two readings do not exclude one another, and both are transmitted in the classical commentaries.** I impose no preference.

**But what the two readings have in common is what gives the expression its force:** the word *anfus* puts one believer's judgement about another in the same place as **his judgement about himself.**

**And this pattern occurred exactly so at `049-hucurat.md`, 49/11:** *wa lā talmizū **anfusakum*** — "do not find fault with yourselves". The note there was this, and it sits here exactly: that the object chosen is *anfusakum* means that the one found fault with is named not "the others" but "yourselves".

**The two sūras use the same word for the same work:**

| Verse | Expression | What it does |
|---|---|---|
| **al-Ḥujurāt 49/11** | *lā talmizū **anfusakum*** | It counts the fault-finding as done to oneself |
| **al-Nūr 24/12** | *ẓanna … bi-**anfusihim** khayrā* | It counts the good opinion as held about oneself |
| **al-Nūr 24/61** | *fa-sallimū ʿalā **anfusikum*** | It counts the greeting as given to oneself |

**The same word in three verses, with three different verbs.** This is a fact of the arrangement and can be counted.

### "Ought you not to have…?" — the form of the command

**The most striking side of the verse is how it frames its command.**

The verse does not say:
- "Verify the report you have heard"
- "Do not spread it"
- "Be silent"

**It says: *ought you not to have thought well and said, "this is a plain slander"?***

**What is asked, then, is not a passive silence but a *judgement*.** There are two verbs: *ẓanna* (to think) and *qālū* (to say). **The one within, the other without.**

| Verb | Where | What |
|---|---|---|
| ***ẓanna … khayrā*** | Within | To frame a good opinion |
| ***qālū hādhā ifkun mubīn*** | Without | To say it |

**And the order is worth noting: first the inside, then the outside.** The word comes as the uttering of an opinion already framed.

**I record this as my own reading, and I ground it on the chain at `049-hucurat.md`, 49/12.** There the chain was built thus: **suspicion → prying → backbiting**, three steps growing from inside outward. And what was recorded there was this: *"The prohibition does not begin with backbiting. It begins with suspicion — that is, where no one can see, and where no harm has yet come to be."*

**al-Nūr 24/12 builds the *positive* counterpart of the same chain:**

| | al-Ḥujurāt 49/12 — the forbidden chain | al-Nūr 24/12 — the chain that is asked for |
|---|---|---|
| **First step** | *al-ẓann* — ill opinion (in the mind) | ***ẓanna … khayrā*** — good opinion (in the mind) |
| **Second step** | *lā tajassasū* — prying | *(no prying is needed)* |
| **Third step** | *lā yaghtab* — spreading on the tongue | ***qālū hādhā ifkun mubīn*** — **refusal** on the tongue |

**The two verses show two directions of the same mechanism.** al-Ḥujurāt cuts the chain at its very beginning; al-Nūr builds a counter-chain in the same place.

**I record this comparison as my own reading;** the order of the steps in the two verses stands in the text and can be compared.

### هَٰذَآ إِفْكٌ مُّبِينٌ — the sentence that is to be said

**مُبِين** — root **ب-ي-ن**, the active participle of Form IV: **what is clear; and what makes clear.** In Arabic the word can be understood both intransitively and transitively.

**And this is the same root as *āyātin bayyināt* in the first verse.** The sūra had presented its own verses as *bayyināt* (distinguishing); here the believers are asked to say of a report that it is *mubīn* (plain).

**The text, that is, hands the addressee the very word of measure it uses of itself.** I record this as an observation about the root.

**And the definiteness of the sentence is worth noting.** The verse does not say "perhaps it is not true", "let us not be sure", "let us wait". **It asks for a sentence of judgement: *hādhā ifkun mubīn*.**

At first sight this may look to stand in tension with the command of *tabayyun* (verify) at `049-hucurat.md`, 49/6. **But the two commands look at different situations, and the difference must be recorded:**

| | al-Ḥujurāt 49/6 | al-Nūr 24/12 |
|---|---|---|
| The subject of the report | **The act of a community** — public, verifiable | **The privacy of a person** — its verification bound to four witnesses |
| What is commanded | *Tabayyun* — verify | **Reject it** |
| The reason | The report may be true and may call for action | **Without four witnesses the report already has no standing (24/13)** |

**The two verses do not contradict one another; what fixes the difference between them is the condition of proof that will be stated in the thirteenth verse.** If a report, by definition, is not held to exist in law unless it is proved by four witnesses, then there is no "verifying" to be done about it either.

**I record this distinction as my own reading;** its ground is the wording of 24/13.

### What this says to the present

**One. The question of the default position.**

What the verse builds is not a counsel of "thinking positively". What it builds is a **default position**: when a report arrives, the mind of the one who receives it is not a blank page — it starts from somewhere. **The verse fixes that starting point.**

And the practical consequence is this: to say "who knows?" in the face of a claim is in fact not a neutral position. **It is to let the claim take room in the mind without carrying its burden of proof.**

**Two. That silence is not enough.**

The verse does not command silence; it commands the saying of a sentence. **This is a concrete distinction that comes out of the arrangement.**

When a report is spreading, those who do not carry it but do not oppose it either do not slow the speed of its spread. **What the verse asks for is the breaking of the chain** — and a chain is broken not by the links staying still but by one of them changing direction.

**Three. And a limit.**

This verse does not mean that no claim is ever to be taken seriously. The sūra itself has built a procedure of proof (4, 6-9, 13). **What the verse closes off is the claim that circulates *outside* the procedure of proof** — the one that goes about not in court but in the mouth.

---

## 24/13 — لَّوْلَا جَآءُو عَلَيْهِ بِأَرْبَعَةِ شُهَدَآءَ فَإِذْ لَمْ يَأْتُوا۟ بِٱلشُّهَدَآءِ فَأُو۟لَٰٓئِكَ عِندَ ٱللَّهِ هُمُ ٱلْكَٰذِبُونَ

*Law lā jāʾū ʿalayhi bi-arbaʿati shuhadāʾ, fa-idh lam yaʾtū bi'l-shuhadāʾi fa-ulāʾika ʿinda'llāhi humu'l-kādhibūn*

"Ought they not to have brought four witnesses to it? Since they did not bring the witnesses, it is they who, in God's sight, are the liars."

### The fourth verse returns into the arrangement

**The verse repeats the condition of the fourth verse exactly as it stood: *arbaʿati shuhadāʾ*.**

**And the place of the repetition is worth noting:** the fourth verse had laid down a **ruling**; the thirteenth applies that ruling to an **event.** The sūra, that is, works the rule it has laid down upon the case it has itself recounted.

**I record this as an observation about the word order;** the identity of the expression in the two verses is the wording of the text.

### فَأُو۟لَٰٓئِكَ عِندَ ٱللَّهِ هُمُ ٱلْكَٰذِبُونَ — "in God's sight"

**Here is the qualification in the sentence that must be read most carefully: *ʿinda'llāh* — "in God's sight".**

**The verse identifies the failure to prove with lying. But it does so with the qualification *ʿinda'llāh*.**

**In the classical commentaries the function of this qualification has been dwelt on, and the explanations transmitted are these:**

| Explanation | Its content |
|---|---|
| **1** | The qualification conveys that the ruling is given not on the **legal** plane but on the **divine** one |
| **2** | The qualification does not exclude the possibility that the word is in fact true, and yet conveys that the ruling is framed so |
| **3** | The qualification reinforces the certainty of the ruling: "in God's sight" — that is, truly |

**I impose no preference, and none is binding.**

**But what lies beneath all three explanations is the same, and I record it as my own reading:** an unproved claim, **even if it is true**, has the standing of a lie from the moment it goes into circulation. For no one who hears it has anything in hand with which to verify it.

**This is the sharpest statement of the matter of the burden of proof recorded at the fourth verse.** The verse does not say "they lied" — it says "they are liars"; that is, it states a position. And what fixes the position is not the content of the claim but **the absence of its proof.**

### فَإِذْ — "since"

**The particle is worth noting: *fa-idh* — "since, given that".**

The sentence draws a **conclusion.** And the manner of the drawing is logical: a condition had been laid down (four witnesses), the condition was not met, the ruling followed.

**This is the working of the verb *faraḍnāhā* in the sūra's first verse.** A measure had been notched; the measure is applied.

---

## 24/14 — وَلَوْلَا فَضْلُ ٱللَّهِ عَلَيْكُمْ وَرَحْمَتُهُۥ فِى ٱلدُّنْيَا وَٱلْءَاخِرَةِ لَمَسَّكُمْ فِى مَآ أَفَضْتُمْ فِيهِ عَذَابٌ عَظِيمٌ

*Wa law lā faḍlu'llāhi ʿalaykum wa raḥmatuhū fi'l-dunyā wa'l-ākhirati la-massakum fī mā afaḍtum fīhi ʿadhābun ʿaẓīm*

"Were it not for God's bounty towards you and His mercy in this world and in the hereafter, a great punishment would have touched you for what you plunged into."

### أَفَضْتُمْ — "you plunged into it"

**Root: ف-ي-ض.** And the concrete sense of the root is one of the most striking word-pictures in this section.

***Fāḍa* — a vessel's overflowing, water's going over the brim and spreading.** *Fayḍ* — an overflowing, abundance. *Ifāḍa* — to make overflow, to pour out.

**And the Qurʾān uses this verb in the setting of the pilgrimage:** *fa-idhā afaḍtum min ʿArafāt* (al-Baqara 2/198) — "when you **pour out** from ʿArafāt". What is described there is a great crowd's **pouring** from one place to another.

**The use of the word here is a verb of water chosen for a word:**

| Word | Its concrete picture |
|---|---|
| **فَاضَ** | A vessel's overflowing |
| **إِفَاضَة** | Making overflow; a crowd's pouring out |
| **فَيْض** | Flood, abundance |

**The verse, that is, describes the spreading of a report as an *overflowing*.**

**And this picture carries three things, which I record as my own reading:**

1. **What overflows has passed the vessel's bound.** A word has gone outside the place it belonged to.
2. **An overflow cannot be steered.** Water that has spilled does not go by a road the spiller has fixed; it finds its own way.
3. **An overflow is the act of a crowd.** A drop does not overflow; a mass does.

**And that the verb comes in Form IV rather than Form V is worth noting: *afāḍa* — transitive.** The verse names its addressees not as the water that overflows but as **those who made it overflow.** The word did not spread of itself; it is not told as "it spread" — it is said, **you made it overflow.**

**This agrees with the whole logic of the sūra:** *talaqqawnahū bi-alsinatikum* in the fifteenth verse, *yuḥibbūna an tashīʿa* in the nineteenth. **In all three verses the spreading is set down not as a passive occurrence but as an act.**

### فِى ٱلدُّنْيَا وَٱلْءَاخِرَةِ — where the qualification stands

**There is a grammatical point in the verse, debated in the classical commentaries: what does the phrase *fi'l-dunyā wa'l-ākhira* attach to?**

| Reading | What it attaches to | Its sense |
|---|---|---|
| **1** | To *faḍl* and *raḥma* | "His bounty and His mercy in this world and in the hereafter" |
| **2** | To *ʿadhābun ʿaẓīm* | "a punishment would have touched you in this world and in the hereafter" |

**Both readings are transmitted in the classical sources; I impose no preference, and neither is binding.**

**But the first reading's agreement with the position in the sentence may be recorded:** the phrase comes immediately after *faḍl* and *raḥma*. I give this as an observation about the word order, not as a preference.

### مَسَّ — "to touch"

**Root: م-س-س.** Its concrete sense is **to touch, to make contact** — the lightest contact.

**And the choice of the verb is not a softening but a sharpening.** The verse does not say *la-nazala ʿalaykum* (it would have come down upon you) or *la-akhadhakum* (it would have seized you). **It says "it would have touched you" — and what touches is described as *ʿaẓīm*.**

**Only the *grazing* of the thing described as "great", then, is at issue.**

I record this as an observation about the style. **The same verb will return in the thirty-fifth verse:** *wa law lam tamsashu nār* — "though no fire had touched it". In both places the same verb, in the same sense of the lightest contact.

---

## 24/15 — إِذْ تَلَقَّوْنَهُۥ بِأَلْسِنَتِكُمْ وَتَقُولُونَ بِأَفْوَاهِكُم مَّا لَيْسَ لَكُم بِهِۦ عِلْمٌ وَتَحْسَبُونَهُۥ هَيِّنًا وَهُوَ عِندَ ٱللَّهِ عَظِيمٌ

*Idh talaqqawnahū bi-alsinatikum wa taqūlūna bi-afwāhikum mā laysa lakum bihī ʿilmun wa taḥsabūnahū hayyinan wa huwa ʿinda'llāhi ʿaẓīm*

"When you were taking it up on your tongues, and saying with your mouths what you had no knowledge of, and thinking it a small thing — while in God's sight it is great."

### One of the densest sentences in the sūra

**This verse describes the mechanics by which a report spreads in four verbs, and each of the four does a distinct work in the arrangement:**

| Order | Verb | Where | What it does |
|---|---|---|---|
| **1** | *talaqqawnahū* | **The tongue** | Taking up, taking over the report |
| **2** | *taqūlūna* | **The mouth** | Saying the report |
| **3** | *mā laysa lakum bihī ʿilm* | **Knowledge** | The note that there is nothing in hand |
| **4** | *taḥsabūnahū hayyinā* | **Appraisal** | Thinking it a small thing |

**The four steps build a chain, and the last link of the chain is the cause of the others.** I take this up at the end of the verse.

### تَلَقَّوْنَهُۥ بِأَلْسِنَتِكُمْ — taking it up from tongue to tongue

**Root: ل-ق-ي.** Its concrete sense is **to meet, to encounter, to come upon a thing.** *Liqāʾ* — a meeting. *Talaqqī* (Form V) — **to receive, to take up and make one's own, to take over.**

**The Qurʾān uses the same form elsewhere:** *fa-talaqqā Ādamu min rabbihī kalimāt* (al-Baqara 2/37) — "Adam **received words** from his Lord". There too the verb conveys the receiving of a word, its being met and made one's own.

**And the most striking side of the usage here is the object of the preposition**: *bi-**alsinati**kum* — "you were taking it up **with your tongues**".

**This is an unlooked-for combination in the language.** A thing is normally received **with the ear.** The verse binds the receiving to the **tongue.**

**And this combination describes how a report spreads. Thus:**

A report is not received by hearing and left there. **The act of receiving is completed only when it is repeated.** A man has not really "received" a report — he has received it the moment he says it to another. Saying is part of receiving.

**The verse, that is, removes the distance between hearing and saying and joins the two in a single verb: *talaqqī bi'l-lisān*.**

**I record this as my own reading, and its ground is the expression itself:** that the instrument of the verb "to receive" is given as the tongue stands in the wording of the text, and it is out of the ordinary.

**The explanations given for this expression in the classical commentaries look in a similar direction:** one man's taking the report over from another, its going from mouth to mouth, everyone taking it from the one before and giving it to the one after. **I transmit these explanations as views that are transmitted.**

**A variant reading is transmitted in the sources:** it is recorded that the word is also read **تَلِقُونَهُ** (*talikūnahū*). This reading comes from the root **و-ل-ق**, and the sense the lexicographers record for it is **to say a thing quickly and without stopping, to keep telling it.**

| Reading | Root | Sense |
|---|---|---|
| **تَلَقَّوْنَهُ** | ل-ق-ي | **To take over** — to receive from another and make one's own |
| **تَلِقُونَهُ** | و-ل-ق | **To keep saying** — to turn it over in the mouth |

**Both readings are transmitted in the sources; since I cannot state with certainty which reader read which, I write no names.**

**And the two readings complete each other:** the one describes **how the report comes** (by being taken over), the other **how it goes** (by being said without ceasing). I record this complementarity as my own reading.

### وَتَقُولُونَ بِأَفْوَاهِكُم — the second organ

**The verse names two organs, and both are worth noting: *alsina* (tongues) and *afwāh* (mouths).**

**The expression "you say with your mouths" looks at first like a needless repetition.** Saying is done with the mouth in any case.

**But the Qurʾān uses this expression for a definite work, and this was recorded earlier in this commentary.** At `033-ahzab.md`, 33/4, the same pattern occurs: *dhālikum qawlukum bi-afwāhikum* — "that is your word **with your mouths**". The context there was an ascription that had no reality.

**The work of the pattern, therefore, is this, and it is explained in this direction in the classical commentaries too:** the qualification *bi'l-fam* conveys that the word stays **only in the mouth**, that there is no reality behind it. It comes out of the mouth but does not rest on any knowledge.

**And the verse's next clause says this outright: *mā laysa lakum bihī ʿilm* — "what you have no knowledge of".**

**The work of the two organs in the arrangement:**

| Organ | Verb | Its work |
|---|---|---|
| **لِسَان** (tongue) | *talaqqī* — to receive | The report's **entry** |
| **فَم** (mouth) | *qawl* — to say | The report's **exit** |

**The verse, that is, pictures man as a pipe through which the report passes: it goes in at one end, comes out at the other, and nothing is done to it in between.**

**The name of what is not done is given in the verse: *ʿilm*.**

**I record this as my own reading;** its ground is that the two organs are named separately and that the note of the absence of knowledge is set between them. The order stands in the wording of the text.

### مَّا لَيْسَ لَكُم بِهِۦ عِلْمٌ — "what you have no knowledge of"

**The expression is a pattern repeated in the Qurʾān** and occurred earlier in this commentary: *wa lā taqfu mā laysa laka bihī ʿilm* (al-Isrāʾ 17/36) — "do not pursue what you have no knowledge of".

**And the pattern here uses a language of possession: *laysa lakum bihī ʿilm* — "you *have* no knowledge of it".**

Knowledge, that is, is placed as **something held in the hand.** For a man to be able to speak on a matter he must have something in hand. **And in slander there is nothing in the hand at all — the fourth verse had already bound this to a condition: four witnesses.**

**And the measure recorded at `049-hucurat.md`, 49/12, works here exactly:** the note there was this — *"The Qurʾān does not censure supposition in itself. What it censures is the putting of an unproved assumption in the place of certain knowledge."*

**al-Nūr 24/15 shows how that "putting in the place of certain knowledge" comes about:** as a word passes from tongue to tongue it gains no knowledge — but it gains **the look of knowledge.** For what is said by many people appears, by the very frequency of its being said, to have been verified.

**I record this as my own reading.**

### وَتَحْسَبُونَهُۥ هَيِّنًا وَهُوَ عِندَ ٱللَّهِ عَظِيمٌ — "you were thinking it a small thing"

**This is one of the strongest sentences in the sūra and deserves separate treatment.**

**هَيِّن** — root **ه-و-ن**. Its concrete sense is **lightness, ease, smallness of weight.** *Hawn* — softness, not being heavy. *Hīn* / *hāʾin* — counted worthless. *Ihāna* — to make light of a man, to devalue him. The Turkish *hain* comes from this root, though its sense has drifted.

**The word is used in the Qurʾān of God's power as well:** *huwa ʿalayya hayyin* (Maryam 19/9) — "that is easy for Me". **The word, then, is not negative in itself; it conveys a *measure of weight*.**

**And what the verse builds is a contrast of measures:**

| | Man's measure | The measure in God's sight |
|---|---|---|
| **The word** | *hayyin* — light | *ʿaẓīm* — great |
| **The root** | ه-و-ن | ع-ظ-م |
| **The concrete sense of the root** | Smallness of weight | **Bone** (*ʿaẓm*) — the body's hard, heavy frame |

**The relation of the root ع-ظ-م with *ʿaẓm* (bone) was recorded in `069-hakka.md`.** The picture that appears when the two roots are set side by side is this: **lightness on the one hand, a skeleton on the other.**

**And what the sentence does is to make a diagnosis.**

**The verse does not say that those who spread the slander were of ill intent.** What it says is finer and far more common: **they held the measure wrongly.**

**The importance of this diagnosis lies here, and I record it as my own reading:**

A man doing something he knows to be wrong feels he must defend himself. **But doing something he supposes to be of no importance he offers no resistance at all** — for in that case there is not even a decision.

**And this is exactly what makes the mechanics of a report's spread possible.** The crowd that spread the slander was not, taken one by one, doing a great evil: each of them was only saying a sentence. **And each one's own share was, in his own eyes, *hayyin*.**

**But the verse had said in the eleventh that the shares are divided:** *li-kulli'mriʾin minhum ma'ktasaba mina'l-ithm.* **The text, that is, establishes this: the share may be small; but to have a share is not to be exempt from a share.**

**And it is said who holds the measure: *ʿinda'llāh*.** The qualification is the same as in the thirteenth verse: there too the ruling was given with the qualification *ʿinda'llāh*.

### The distance between هَيِّنًا and what is great: a sum

**The contrast of measures the verse builds has one further side, and I record it as my own reading.**

There is a reason for supposing it *hayyin*: **in a report that spreads, no one sees the whole of it.**

The one who first says it does not see where it will go. The link in the middle does not know through how many people it has already passed. The last link does not know the first source. **Every link sees only its own share, and its own share really is small.**

**But the harm is the sum of the shares, and no one sees the sum.**

**And the verse's qualification *ʿinda'llāh* sits exactly at this point:** the party that sees the sum is the party that holds the measure.

**I record this reading as my own reading;** its ground is that the verse has set the qualification *ʿinda'llāh* between *hayyin* and *ʿaẓīm*.

### al-Ḥujurāt and al-Nūr: two halves of the same matter

**These two sūras are the texts in the Qurʾān that treat the ethics of report and of speech in the greatest detail, and they complete each other.** Relying on the discussions of `049-hucurat.md` 49/6 and 49/12, I record the comparison:

| | **49. al-Ḥujurāt** | **24. al-Nūr** |
|---|---|---|
| **Subject** | The **verifying** of a report | The **proof** of an accusation, and its **spread** |
| **The kind of report** | *Nabaʾ* — about a community, calling for action | *Ifk* — about the privacy of a person |
| **The one addressed** | The **receiver** of the report (49/6: *fa-tabayyanū*) | Both the receiver (24/12) and the **spreader** (24/15, 19) |
| **The first act commanded** | Verifying / halting (*tabayyun* / *tathabbut*) | **Rejecting** (*hādhā ifkun mubīn*) |
| **The beginning of the chain** | **Supposition** (49/12) — in the mind | **Supposition** (24/12) — in the mind |
| **The middle of the chain** | **Prying** (49/12) — searching out | **Taking over** (24/15) |
| **The end of the chain** | **Backbiting** (49/12) — on the tongue | **Spreading** (24/19) |
| **Sanction** | None — a rule of **manners** | **Present** — eighty strokes, the rejection of testimony (24/4) |
| **Condition of proof** | None — "verify" | **Present** — four witnesses (24/4, 13) |
| **The comparison used** | **The flesh of a dead brother** (49/12) | **Overflowing water** (24/14), **a thrown stone** (24/4) |
| **Who holds the measure** | *Wa'llāhu baṣīrun bimā taʿmalūn* (49/18) | *Wa huwa ʿinda'llāhi ʿaẓīm* (24/15) |

**What the two sūras build together is this, and I record it as my own reading:**

**al-Ḥujurāt regulates *what is to be done* with a report; al-Nūr fixes *what an accusation is*.**

In al-Ḥujurāt the report may turn out to be true, and if it does one acts. **In al-Nūr the matter is different: a claim about the privacy of a person is not even a thing whose truth is to be looked into, so long as it is not proved by four witnesses.**

**The shared backbone of the two sūras, however, is the same: the word is stopped before it goes into circulation.** al-Ḥujurāt does this by waiting, al-Nūr by closing the door.

### What this says to the present

**One. There is no such thing as the innocence of the middleman.**

The verse's four verbs — to receive, to say, not to know, to count light — describe not a source but a **carrier.** **And the text takes the carrier as its addressee.**

This is the distinction most often lost in today's terms. There is a difference of degree between the man who first puts a claim about and the man who passes it on — the eleventh verse already recorded this (*alladhī tawallā kibrahū*). **But a difference of degree is not an exemption.**

**Two. The diagnosing of "thinking it a small thing".**

The most useful side of the verse is that it diagnoses not a behaviour but an **error of appraisal.** And the symptom of that error can be recognised: **not having stopped to think at all before saying a word.**

A man hesitates when he says something he thinks weighty. **If there is no hesitation, that word is for that man *hayyin*.** The verse gives a measure, and the measure can be read from within.

**Three. And a limit: this verse does not forbid speaking.**

What the verse forbids is the saying of **what one has no knowledge of**; not the saying of words. In the twelfth verse the saying of a sentence was **commanded.** The sūra asks not for silence but for the saying of **what one has in hand.**

---

## 24/16 — وَلَوْلَآ إِذْ سَمِعْتُمُوهُ قُلْتُم مَّا يَكُونُ لَنَآ أَن نَّتَكَلَّمَ بِهَٰذَا سُبْحَٰنَكَ هَٰذَا بُهْتَٰنٌ عَظِيمٌ

*Wa law lā idh samiʿtumūhu qultum mā yakūnu lanā an natakallama bi-hādhā subḥānaka hādhā buhtānun ʿaẓīm*

"When you heard it, ought you not to have said: 'It is not for us to speak of this. Glory be to You — this is a great slander'?"

### The same moment a second time

**The verse opens with the same note of time as the twelfth: *idh samiʿtumūh* — "when you heard it".**

**The sūra, that is, returns twice to the same moment and twice asks for a different sentence:**

| Verse | The sentence asked for | What it does |
|---|---|---|
| **12** | *hādhā ifkun mubīn* — "this is a plain slander" | A judgement **about the report** |
| **16** | *mā yakūnu lanā an natakallama bi-hādhā* — "it is not for us to speak of this" | A judgement **about oneself** |

**The two sentences look in two different directions, and this is the verse's most important point of arrangement.**

The sentence in the twelfth verse rejects **the truth of the report.** The sentence in the sixteenth, without entering the question of truth at all, rejects **the speaker's standing to speak.**

**I record this as my own reading, and its ground is the content of the two sentences.**

### مَّا يَكُونُ لَنَآ — "it is not for us"

**In Arabic the pattern conveys a lack of *standing*.** Literally: "it is not for us", "this does not belong to us". The Qurʾān uses the same pattern elsewhere: *mā yakūnu lī an aqūla mā laysa lī bi-ḥaqq* (al-Māʾida 5/116) — "it is not for me to say what is not my right".

**And the work of the pattern here is worth recording.**

The verse does not say "this is not true" or "we do not know this". **It says "this is not our business".**

**Two distinct positions, then, can be taken in the face of a report, and the sūra asks for both:**

| Position | Its ground | What it says |
|---|---|---|
| **Rejection grounded on knowledge** (12) | There is no evidence in hand | "This is a slander" |
| **Rejection grounded on standing** (16) | This domain is not mine | "It is not for me to speak of this" |

**Recording the second position is important, because it is wider than the first.**

We cannot always know whether a report is true. **But we can always know whether a report belongs to us.** The second question requires no knowledge.

**And this is the same as the logic of the prohibition of *tajassus* (prying) at `049-hucurat.md`, 49/12.** The measure recorded there was this: *"The difference lies in whose the knowledge is: where a lost brother is is no one's privacy; but a man's hidden condition belongs to him."*

**al-Nūr 24/16 builds the same measure from the side of speech:** if a piece of knowledge is not mine, then speaking about it is not mine either.

**I record this connection as my own reading.**

### تَكَلَّمَ — *kalām*

**Root: ك-ل-م.** And the root has an interesting side: **from the same root come both *kalima* (a word) and *kalm* (a wound).**

*Kalm* — a wound opened upon a body, a cut. The connection the lexicographers draw is this: **a word leaves a mark upon the one who hears it** — just as a wound does.

**I transmit this connection as an explanation the lexicographers record, not as a claim of derivation.** That the two words are from the same root stands in the dictionary.

**And the context here shows the reasonable side of the connection:** the verse is speaking of what a word does in a human being.

### سُبْحَٰنَكَ — an unlooked-for word

**Here is the most striking place in the verse, and it has been dwelt on in the classical commentaries.**

***Subḥānaka* — "glory be to You". Root س-ب-ح: to swim, to go flowing; and from there *tasbīḥ*: to keep a thing clear of what does not befit it.** The root has been taken up at many places in this commentary; it will return in the forty-first verse of the sūra.

**And the difficulty is this: why is a sentence of *tanzīh* said in the face of a report of slander?**

**The explanations transmitted in the classical commentaries are these:**

| Explanation | Its content |
|---|---|
| **1** | The word is used here not in its primary sense but as an expression of **wonder and astonishment**; this usage exists in Arabic |
| **2** | The word is in its full sense: God is held clear in the face of the enormity of what has been said |
| **3** | *Tanzīh* was called for because the claim in question touches a domain God protects |

**I impose no preference, and none is binding.**

**But an observation about the word order may be recorded, and I give it as my own reading:** the sentence the verse asks for has three parts, and each of the three looks in a different direction.

| Part | Direction | What it says |
|---|---|---|
| *Mā yakūnu lanā an natakallama bi-hādhā* | **Towards oneself** | This is not my business |
| *Subḥānaka* | **Upward** | This is not a thing to be said before the one who set the measure |
| *Hādhā buhtānun ʿaẓīm* | **Towards the report** | This is a slander |

**In a single sentence, then, the verse asks for three distinct judgements: of one's standing, of the measure, and of the report.**

### بُهْتَٰنٌ عَظِيمٌ — the slander that stuns

**Root: ب-ه-ت.** This root was analysed in `060-mumtehine.md`; I rely on that treatment. The note there:

> Root **ب-ه-ت**: **to strike dumb, to leave stunned.** *Buhtān* is not an ordinary lie: it is a slander so unlooked for that it **stuns** the one it is aimed at, one before which he does not know what to say. **The word itself describes the effect of the slander.**

And the usage at al-Baqara 2/258 was named there: *fa-buhita'lladhī kafar* — "the one who disbelieved was struck dumb".

**Now the three words in the sūra can be set side by side, and it is seen that each does a distinct work:**

| Word | Verse | Root | What it looks at |
|---|---|---|---|
| **إِفْك** | 11, 12 | أ-ف-ك — to turn about | **The structure of the slander** — the truth turned inside out |
| **بُهْتَان** | 16 | ب-ه-ت — to strike dumb | **The effect of the slander** — leaving the other without an answer |
| **فَاحِشَة** | 19 | ف-ح-ش — to exceed the measure | **The name of what is spread** |

**Three words in three different places, and none of the three is a needless repetition.** The sūra names the same event from three different sides: **what it is, what it does, and how it spreads.**

**I record this table as my own reading;** the places and roots of the words stand in the text.

**And one further observation: the sense of *buhtān* as "leaving without an answer" describes the condition of the one slandered.**

The heaviest side of a slander is that it cannot be answered. **That a thing not done was not done cannot be proved.** There is no evidence in hand, because a thing that did not happen leaves no trace either. **The one slandered is, for precisely this reason, *buhita* — struck dumb.**

**I record this as my own reading;** its ground is the sense in the root of the word, and that sense was recorded independently in `060-mumtehine.md`.

**And why the fourth verse puts the burden of proof upon the claimant is understood exactly here:** because the other side cannot prove.

---

## 24/17-18 — يَعِظُكُمُ ٱللَّهُ أَن تَعُودُوا۟ لِمِثْلِهِۦٓ أَبَدًا إِن كُنتُم مُّؤْمِنِينَ · وَيُبَيِّنُ ٱللَّهُ لَكُمُ ٱلْءَايَٰتِ وَٱللَّهُ عَلِيمٌ حَكِيمٌ

*Yaʿiẓukumu'llāhu an taʿūdū li-mithlihī abadan in kuntum muʾminīn · Wa yubayyinu'llāhu lakumu'l-āyāt, wa'llāhu ʿalīmun ḥakīm*

"God admonishes you never to return to the like of it, if you are believers. And God makes the verses clear to you. God is knowing, wise."

### يَعِظُ — admonition

**Root: و-ع-ظ.** The sense the lexicographers record: **to remind a man in a way that softens his heart and holds him back from a deed.** *Mawʿiẓa* — an admonition.

**In the definition of the word there are two elements at once: reminding and holding back.** *Waʿẓ*, that is, is not the giving of knowledge; it is the bringing back of something known, in a way that changes behaviour.

**And this is on the same line as *tadhakkarūn* in the first verse.** In both places the sūra speaks not of giving new knowledge but of calling back something known.

### أَن تَعُودُوا۟ لِمِثْلِهِۦٓ أَبَدًا — "that you never return to the like of it"

**Two words in the expression are worth noting.**

**The first: *li-mithlihī* — "to the like of it".** The verse does not say "do not return to this"; **it says "to anything like this".**

**This carries the scope of the ruling out of the event and over to the *kind*.** What is forbidden is not a particular report but everything of that kind.

**I record this as an observation about the word order** and find it in agreement with the measure recorded in `049-hucurat.md`: there too it was stated that the verses regulate not single events but **kinds of event.**

**The second: *abadā* — "never".** And this word occurred in the fourth verse too: *wa lā taqbalū lahum shahādatan **abadā***.

| Verse | What *abadā* attaches to |
|---|---|
| **4** | The **non-acceptance** of testimony |
| **17** | The **not returning** again |

**The two usages answer each other, and I record this as my own reading:** the one closes a door, the other a road. And between the two stands the note of repentance in the fifth verse.

### إِن كُنتُم مُّؤْمِنِينَ — the condition comes a second time

**This conditional clause occurs for the second time in the sūra.** The first was in the second verse: *in kuntum tuʾminūna bi'llāhi wa'l-yawmi'l-ākhir.*

| Verse | What the condition attaches to |
|---|---|
| **2** | That *raʾfa* should not stop the ruling |
| **17** | That one should not return to the same thing |

**In both places the condition is set against a *feeling* or a *habit*.** In the one, pity; in the other, the wish to carry a report.

I record this as an observation about the word order.

### وَيُبَيِّنُ ٱللَّهُ لَكُمُ ٱلْءَايَٰتِ — the return of the root

**The root ب-ي-ن returns here after the first verse**, and it will return again and again in the sūra: 34, 46, 58, 59, 61.

**The root's distribution through the sūra builds a weave:**

| Verse | Expression | Who / what |
|---|---|---|
| **1** | *āyātin **bayyināt*** | An attribute of the verses |
| **12** | *hādhā ifkun **mubīn*** | The judgement the believers are asked to say |
| **18** | *yu**bayyinu**'llāhu lakumu'l-āyāt* | God's act |
| **25** | *al-Ḥaqqu'l-**Mubīn*** | **A divine name** |
| **34** | *āyātin **mubayyināt*** | An attribute of the verses |
| **46** | *āyātin **mubayyināt*** | An attribute of the verses |
| **54** | *al-balāghu'l-**mubīn*** | The Prophet's task |
| **58, 59, 61** | *yu**bayyinu**'llāhu lakumu'l-āyāt* | God's act |

**The root occurs ten times in the sūra, and at each occurrence a work of *separating* is done.** This shows how the sūra names its own work.

**I record this as my own reading;** the places of the words can be counted in the text.

---

## 24/19 — إِنَّ ٱلَّذِينَ يُحِبُّونَ أَن تَشِيعَ ٱلْفَٰحِشَةُ فِى ٱلَّذِينَ ءَامَنُوا۟ لَهُمْ عَذَابٌ أَلِيمٌ فِى ٱلدُّنْيَا وَٱلْءَاخِرَةِ وَٱللَّهُ يَعْلَمُ وَأَنتُمْ لَا تَعْلَمُونَ

*Inna'lladhīna yuḥibbūna an tashīʿa'l-fāḥishatu fi'lladhīna āmanū lahum ʿadhābun alīmun fi'l-dunyā wa'l-ākhira, wa'llāhu yaʿlamu wa antum lā taʿlamūn*

"For those who love that indecency should spread among those who believe there is a painful punishment in this world and in the hereafter. God knows, and you do not know."

### The distinction the verse draws: not the doing, but the wanting it spread

**This verse draws one of the finest distinctions in the sūra — and perhaps in the Qurʾān — and it will be taken up here in detail.**

**The verse does not censure an act. It censures a *wish*. And what is wished is not an act either, but the *spreading* of an act.**

Let us open out the structure of the sentence:

| Layer | Expression | What |
|---|---|---|
| **1** | *al-fāḥisha* | The act itself |
| **2** | *an tashīʿa* | The **spreading** of that act |
| **3** | *yuḥibbūna* | The **wanting** of that spreading |

**What is censured is the third layer.**

**The verse, that is, speaks not of the one who commits the act but of the one who wants it to be heard that the act was committed.** And this man may not have committed the act himself — indeed, in the picture the verse builds, it is likely that he did not.

**Why this distinction matters so much I record in three items, and this is my own reading:**

**One. The verse names a type of harm.** The harm of an act is confined to the one who commits it. **The harm of an act's *spreading*, on the other hand, spreads over the whole community** — for what spreads is no longer the act but an image.

**Two. The verse points to an evil that goes about in a moral dress.** The man who spreads an indecency generally spreads it by condemning it. **The sentence "have you heard what happened" is, in form, a sentence of condemnation.** And precisely for that reason the spreader thinks himself set against the act.

**Three. And this is why the verse's word is *yuḥibbūn*: "they love".** The verb conveys not an act but a **leaning.**

### شَاعَ — the concrete sense of the root

**Root: ش-ي-ع.** This root was analysed at `054-kamer.md`, 54/51, at `037-saffat.md`, 37/83, and at `028-kasas.md`, 28/4; I rely on those treatments.

The note there was this: **the concrete sense of the root is to spread, to scatter, to go in every direction.** *Shāʿa'l-khabar* — the report spread. *Ashāʿa* — he spread it. **The Turkish expression *şuyu bulmak* ("to become widely known") is from the same root.**

**And the root's second sense: a body of people who follow someone, a party of partisans** (*shīʿa*, plural *shiyaʿ* and *ashyāʿ*).

**The sentence recorded in the al-Ṣāffāt section works exactly here:** *"at the centre of the word there are two things: to follow a person, and to spread that person's cause."*

**And the connection between the two senses of the root explains the mechanics of the verse, and I record this as my own reading:**

**The spreading of a thing and the forming of a group are named from the same root.** For when a report spreads, a bond forms of itself among those who carry it — a shared piece of knowledge, a shared stance, a shared subject of talk.

**And this joins with the word *ʿuṣba* in the eleventh verse:** there those who brought the slander were named "a band bound to one another". **In the nineteenth verse it is seen how that bond is formed: by spreading.**

| Verse | Word | Root | What it conveys |
|---|---|---|---|
| **11** | *ʿuṣba* | ع-ص-ب — to bind | The **state** of the group |
| **19** | *tashīʿa* | ش-ي-ع — to spread; and to take a side | The **act** of the group |

**The two words name the same fact from two sides.** I record this table as my own reading; the roots of the words stand in the dictionary.

### فَاحِشَة — an ugliness that exceeds the measure

**Root: ف-ح-ش.** The sense the lexicographers record: **a thing's being so ugly as to exceed its measure, its excess.** *Fāḥish* — excessive, beyond measure. *Fuḥsh* — ugliness.

**At the centre of the word stands "excess", and the word is not used only of an act.** In Arabic *fāḥish* is used of a price as well: a price that exceeds the measure.

**And the word will occur twice more in the sūra:** *al-faḥshāʾ* in the twenty-first verse (as what Satan commands), and the root in the thirty-third, in the domain named together with *bighāʾ*.

**The usage in the verse is worth recording:** the verse does not say *al-zinā*, it says *al-fāḥisha*. **What is named, that is, is not a particular act but a kind.** This widens the scope of the prohibition: what is condemned in its spreading is not one kind of act alone.

**And that the word *al-fāḥisha* comes definite draws a limit:** the verse takes as its subject not "the spreading of every kind of report" but the spreading of **what is of this kind.**

### فِى ٱلَّذِينَ ءَامَنُوا۟ — "among those who believe"

**The preposition is worth noting: *fī* — "within".** Not *bayna* (between) or *ʿalā* (upon).

**The spreading, that is, is described as something happening *within* a community.** And this looks in the same direction as the qualification *minkum* — "from among you" — in the eleventh verse.

**From beginning to end the sūra keeps the matter inside a community.** Not as an attack coming from outside but as a mechanism working within.

### يُحِبُّونَ — "they love"

**Root: ح-ب-ب.** Its concrete sense is **a seed, a grain** (*ḥabb*). The connection the lexicographers draw: love is what settles in the heart like a seed and strikes root.

**And the use of the verb in this verse is the place that must be read most carefully.**

**The verse does not censure an act: it censures a leaning.** And a leaning lies further within than an act — it is seen by no one, it cannot be measured, it cannot be proved.

**This is the same logic of "a circle narrowing from outside inward" recorded in `049-hucurat.md`.** It was recorded there that the sūra puts the least visible thing last. **al-Nūr does the same:** it begins from an act that could be proved by four witnesses and comes down to a leaning that could never be proved at all.

**And precisely for this reason the verse lays down no worldly sanction.** The end of the sentence is this: *lahum ʿadhābun alīmun fi'l-dunyā wa'l-ākhira* — in this world and in the hereafter. **This speaks not in the language of the court but in the language of the reckoning.**

**I record this as my own reading;** its ground is that the verse gives neither a procedure of proof nor a measure of penalty. In the fourth verse there were both; here there is neither.

### وَٱللَّهُ يَعْلَمُ وَأَنتُمْ لَا تَعْلَمُونَ — the close

**The verse-ending is like the seal of the whole block, and it is extremely short: "God knows, and you do not know."**

**And the arrangement of the sentence is worth noting:** the two parties are set side by side, the same verb is used (*yaʿlam* / *taʿlamūn*), the one affirmative, the other negative.

**This is the summary of the whole section.** In the fifteenth verse it had said *mā laysa lakum bihī ʿilm* — "what you have no knowledge of". The nineteenth repeats the same word in its last clause: *wa antum lā taʿlamūn.*

| Verse | Expression | What is not known |
|---|---|---|
| **15** | *mā laysa lakum bihī ʿilm* | About the content of the report |
| **19** | *wa antum lā taʿlamūn* | **In general** |

**The sūra, that is, first identifies the ignorance as confined to one event, then records it as a general position.**

I record this as an observation about the word order.

### What this says to the present

**One. Spreading in the form of condemnation.**

This is the most familiar thing the verse points to, and it can be said in language that does not wound: **the commonest way of telling an indecency is to tell it by condemning it.**

And this form clears the spreader in his own eyes. For the grammatical structure of the sentence he says is a sentence of condemnation. **But where the verse looks is not the structure of the sentence but its outcome: has a thing spread, or has it not?**

**The verse does not declare the man who does this to be evil.** The verb it uses is *yuḥibbūn* — it describes a leaning. And leanings go on working so long as the man himself does not notice them.

**Two. Where the distinction is placed.**

A thing's **happening** and that thing's **being heard of** are separate events, and their outcomes differ.

An act has been committed. If three people know of it, the harm is at one place. If three hundred know of it, the harm is at another — and **the increase of harm is the product not of the one who committed the act but of those who spread it.**

**The verse names this increase and binds a separate ruling to it.**

**Three. And the link with the sūra as a whole.**

This verse explains why the condition of four witnesses in the fourth verse is so heavy. **The condition is so heavy that bringing an act before the community is all but impossible.** And the nineteenth verse says the reason: what is asked to be brought is most often in the service not of justice but **of the spreading.**

**Four. A limit: this verse is not a command to cover things up.**

To read the verse as "let no evil be spoken of" runs against the sūra itself. The sūra has built a procedure of proof; the existence of a procedure presupposes that matters **can be brought** somewhere. And as was recorded at `049-hucurat.md`, 49/9, as the counterpart of the ninth verse, the Qurʾān commands that action be taken against wrongdoing.

**What the verse closes is not the road of justice — it is the road of gossip.** The difference between the two stands in the verse's own word: *tashīʿa* — to spread. **A matter taken to an authority does not spread; a matter recounted does.**

I record this distinction as my own reading.

---

## 24/20 — وَلَوْلَا فَضْلُ ٱللَّهِ عَلَيْكُمْ وَرَحْمَتُهُۥ وَأَنَّ ٱللَّهَ رَءُوفٌ رَّحِيمٌ

*Wa law lā faḍlu'llāhi ʿalaykum wa raḥmatuhū wa anna'llāha raʾūfun raḥīm*

"And were it not for God's bounty towards you and His mercy, and that God is most kind, most merciful…"

### A third time, a condition without an answer

**The pattern of the tenth verse is repeated here, and again its answer is not spoken.**

**But there is a difference, and it is worth noting:** in the tenth verse the close was *tawwābun ḥakīm*; here it is *raʾūfun raḥīm*.

| Verse | The close | Its kind |
|---|---|---|
| **10** | *tawwābun ḥakīm* | Repentance + ruling |
| **20** | *raʾūfun raḥīm* | **Kindness + mercy** |

**In the second verse *raʾfa* had been forbidden — on the condition that it should not stop the ruling.** And now the same root returns as a name of God: *raʾūf*.

**This is one of the finest rings in the sūra's arrangement, and I record it as my own reading:**

| Verse | Word | Whose | What |
|---|---|---|---|
| **2** | *raʾfatun* | **Man's** | The feeling asked not to stop the ruling |
| **20** | *raʾūfun* | **God's** | The name invoked after the ruling |

**The sūra thus separates this: a man's *raʾfa* cannot alter a measure that has been set; but the *raʾfa* of the one who sets the measure stands behind the measure itself.**

The definition recorded at `059-hasr.md`, 59/10, shows its use here: *raʾfa* contains **the refusal to give pain at all, the refusal to hurt.** And this is exactly the work done between the eleventh and the twentieth verses — a community has made a heavy mistake, and in correcting it the text has entered a note of bounty and mercy **four times** in ten verses.

**I record this as an observation of count: 10, 14, 20, 21.**

---

## 24/21 — يَٰٓأَيُّهَا ٱلَّذِينَ ءَامَنُوا۟ لَا تَتَّبِعُوا۟ خُطُوَٰتِ ٱلشَّيْطَٰنِ وَمَن يَتَّبِعْ خُطُوَٰتِ ٱلشَّيْطَٰنِ فَإِنَّهُۥ يَأْمُرُ بِٱلْفَحْشَآءِ وَٱلْمُنكَرِ وَلَوْلَا فَضْلُ ٱللَّهِ عَلَيْكُمْ وَرَحْمَتُهُۥ مَا زَكَىٰ مِنكُم مِّنْ أَحَدٍ أَبَدًا وَلَٰكِنَّ ٱللَّهَ يُزَكِّى مَن يَشَآءُ وَٱللَّهُ سَمِيعٌ عَلِيمٌ

*Yā ayyuhā'lladhīna āmanū lā tattabiʿū khuṭuwāti'l-shayṭān, wa man yattabiʿ khuṭuwāti'l-shayṭāni fa-innahū yaʾmuru bi'l-faḥshāʾi wa'l-munkar, wa law lā faḍlu'llāhi ʿalaykum wa raḥmatuhū mā zakā minkum min aḥadin abadan wa lākinna'llāha yuzakkī man yashāʾ, wa'llāhu samīʿun ʿalīm*

"O you who believe, do not follow the footsteps of Satan. Whoever follows the footsteps of Satan — he commands indecency and what is wrong. Were it not for God's bounty towards you and His mercy, not one of you would ever have been purified; but God purifies whom He wills. God is hearing, knowing."

### خُطُوَٰت — footsteps

**Root: خ-ط-و.** Its concrete sense is **to take a step.** *Khuṭwa* — a step, the distance between two feet.

**That the word comes in the plural, and that it is not a figure, is worth noting.** The verse does not say "do not follow Satan's road" — **it says "his footsteps".**

**And the difference between the two words describes a mechanism, which I record as my own reading:**

A **road** is seen from the start; one decides whether to enter it or not. **A *step*, on the other hand, is not a road by itself.** As one steps, where one is going is not visible; only the place of the next step is.

**And what is told between the eleventh and the twentieth verses is exactly a process that works step by step:** a report is heard (12), repeated (15), thought a small thing (15), spread (19). **At no stage does anyone say "I am now doing a great evil".**

**This is why the verse chooses the word *khuṭuwāt*, and the connection can be read from the arrangement:** the verse comes immediately after the diagnosis of *hayyinan* (thinking it a small thing) in the fifteenth.

**The form is worth noting too: *tattabiʿū* — Form VIII, *ittibāʿ*.** This form conveys here **a going after, a following of tracks.** The man is not looking ahead; he is looking at someone's tracks.

**And the expression is a pattern repeated in the Qurʾān:** the same expression occurs at `002-bakara.md`, 2/168 and 2/208, and also at al-Anʿām 6/142. **The same structure in all four places: *lā tattabiʿū khuṭuwāti'l-shayṭān*.**

### ٱلْفَحْشَآءِ وَٱلْمُنكَرِ — two words

**فَحْشَاء** — root **ف-ح-ش**: analysed at the nineteenth verse. **An ugliness that exceeds the measure.**

**مُنكَر** — root **ن-ك-ر**: **not to be recognised, to be found strange.** The root was analysed at `031-lokman.md`, 31/19 (*ankara'l-aṣwāt* — the most jarring of voices) and occurred at 31/17 as *wa'nha ʿani'l-munkar*; I rely on those treatments.

**The coming of the two words together sets up a scale:**

| Word | Root | Its measure |
|---|---|---|
| **فَحْشَاء** | ف-ح-ش — excess | **Its degree** — what exceeds the measure |
| **مُنكَر** | ن-ك-ر — not being recognised | **Its recognisability** — what men find strange |

**The one, that is, looks at quantity, the other at recognition.** The opposite of *munkar* is *maʿrūf*: what is known, recognised, accepted by everyone as right.

### مَا زَكَىٰ مِنكُم مِّنْ أَحَدٍ أَبَدًا — purification

**زَكَىٰ — root ز-ك-و.** This root has been taken up at many places in this commentary — `087-ala.md`, `091-sems.md`, `092-leyl.md`, `080-abese.md`. Its concrete sense: **to increase, to grow, to be fruitful; and to be cleansed.**

**The lexicographers draw the connection between the two senses of the root thus: a thing grows only when it has been cleared of foreign elements.** A crop yields nothing until it is cleared of weeds.

**And the word will return again and again in the sūra:**

| Verse | Expression | What |
|---|---|---|
| **21** | *mā zakā minkum min aḥad* | Purification |
| **28** | *huwa **azkā** lakum* | The outcome of the manners of asking leave |
| **30** | *dhālika **azkā** lahum* | The outcome of lowering the glance |
| **37** | *ītāʾi'l-**zakāt*** | The alms |

**The same root in four places, in three different tasks.** And in all three the same thing is said: **to hold back from a thing is the condition of increase.**

**I record this as my own reading;** the places of the words can be counted in the text and their repetitions seen.

**And the qualification in the verse's arrangement is important:** *mā zakā minkum min aḥadin abadā* — "not one of you would ever have been purified". **Purification, that is, is not set down as an outcome man reaches on his own.** The sentence goes on with an exception: *wa lākinna'llāha yuzakkī man yashāʾ.*

**This enters a note upon the whole of the section.** The verses from eleven to twenty-one were correcting a community's fault. **And the section closes by saying that those who were corrected did not achieve it themselves.**

**This note also settles how the sūra is to be read:** to take up the text and use it to sort the purified from the unpurified runs against the verse's last sentence.

---

## 24/22 — وَلَا يَأْتَلِ أُو۟لُوا۟ ٱلْفَضْلِ مِنكُمْ وَٱلسَّعَةِ أَن يُؤْتُوٓا۟ أُو۟لِى ٱلْقُرْبَىٰ وَٱلْمَسَٰكِينَ وَٱلْمُهَٰجِرِينَ فِى سَبِيلِ ٱللَّهِ وَلْيَعْفُوا۟ وَلْيَصْفَحُوٓا۟ أَلَا تُحِبُّونَ أَن يَغْفِرَ ٱللَّهُ لَكُمْ وَٱللَّهُ غَفُورٌ رَّحِيمٌ

*Wa lā yaʾtali ulu'l-faḍli minkum wa'l-saʿati an yuʾtū uli'l-qurbā wa'l-masākīna wa'l-muhājirīna fī sabīli'llāh, wa'l-yaʿfū wa'l-yaṣfaḥū, a lā tuḥibbūna an yaghfira'llāhu lakum, wa'llāhu ghafūrun raḥīm*

"And let not those of you who have bounty and means swear not to give to their kinsfolk, to the needy, and to those who emigrate in the way of God; let them pardon and let them overlook. Do you not wish that God should forgive you? God is forgiving, merciful."

### The verse's place: a call to pardon coming after the slander

**The verse's place in the sūra is as important as the verse itself.**

The verses from eleven to twenty-one took up a slander, described those who brought it in the heaviest terms, announced a penalty and built a procedure. **And the twenty-second verse comes directly after that section and says *go on giving*.**

**The one addressed by the verse is the party harmed by the slander.** This is understood from the subject of the sentence: *ulu'l-faḍli minkum wa'l-saʿa* — "those of you who have bounty and means". That is, the giving party, the one who has the means in hand.

**And this arrangement is one of the most unlooked-for moves in the sūra:**

| Verse | What is done |
|---|---|
| **11-21** | The slander is condemned; a sanction and a procedure are laid down |
| **22** | **Pardon is asked of the party that was harmed** |
| **23-25** | The condition of those who slandered is bound over to the hereafter |

**The text, that is, sets two rulings one after the other: the act is grave, and let the one harmed by the act pardon.**

**The logic of this is given in the verse's last sentence: *a lā tuḥibbūna an yaghfira'llāhu lakum* — "do you not wish that God should forgive you?"**

### يَأْتَلِ — the root أ-ل-و and two readings

**This is the most disputed word in the verse in point of language, and the root's two distinct senses lead to two distinct readings.**

**Root: أ-ل-و.** And the two clusters of sense the lexicographers record are these:

| Cluster of sense | Verb | An example of the usage |
|---|---|---|
| **1. To swear** | *ālā / iʾtalā* | *Aly* / *aliyya* — an oath. *Īlāʾ* — to swear not to do a thing |
| **2. To fall short, to hold back, to spare one's effort** | *alā yaʾlū* | *Lā yaʾlūnakum khabālā* (Āl ʿImrān 3/118) — "they spare no effort to do you harm" |

**And the verb *yaʾtali* in the verse (Form VIII) is understood in two ways, according to which of the two senses it carries:**

| Reading | The sense of the root | The sense of the sentence |
|---|---|---|
| **1** | **Oath** | "Let them not **swear** not to give" |
| **2** | **Falling short, holding back** | "Let them not **hold back** from giving, let them not fall short" |

**This disagreement is recorded openly in the classical commentaries and both readings are transmitted. I impose no preference, and neither is binding.**

**Moreover it is transmitted in the sources that the word is read in a different form as well** — in Form V (*yataʾalla*), again with the sense of swearing. **Since I cannot state with certainty which reader read which, I write no names.**

**What the two readings have in common may be recorded:** both forbid **the cutting off of giving.** Where they part is over whether the cutting off is done by an oath or in silence.

**And an observation: the first reading gives the cutting off a *form*.** An oath is an attempt to make a decision irreversible. **The first reading, that is, describes the tendency of a grievance to make itself permanent.**

I record this as my own reading.

**A note on the root:** the derivative **آلَاء** (*ālāʾ* — favours), formed of the same letters, was treated at `053-necm.md`, 53/55, and in the al-Raḥmān section. **The lexicographers are not agreed as to whether these derivatives come from a single root; I draw no connection from it here.**

### أُو۟لُوا۟ ٱلْفَضْلِ … وَٱلسَّعَةِ — two attributes

**The verse names its addressee with two attributes, and both are worth noting:**

**فَضْل** — root **ف-ض-ل**: **to increase, to be over and above; and superiority.** The concrete sense of the word is "the part left over"; English covers the two ends of this root with two unconnected words — *surplus* and *excellence*.

**سَعَة** — root **و-س-ع**: **breadth.** *Wāsiʿ* — wide; *saʿa* — breadth of means, material ease.

**The two words return again a few verses later in the sūra:**

| Verse | Word | Whose |
|---|---|---|
| **10, 14, 20, 21** | *faḍlu'llāh* | **God's** bounty |
| **22** | *ulu'l-faḍl* | **Man's** surplus |
| **32** | *yughnihimu'llāhu min **faḍlih*** | God's bounty |
| **38** | *wa yazīdahum min **faḍlih*** | God's bounty |

**And this distribution says something, which I record as my own reading:** the same word is used in one verse of what God gives to His servant, and in the next of a servant's giving to a servant. **The *faḍlu'llāhi ʿalaykum* of the twenty-first verse and the *ulu'l-faḍli minkum* of the twenty-second stand back to back.**

**This transition in the arrangement builds in advance the logic of the verse's last sentence:** you resist over giving what you were given; and you go on expecting to receive.

### وَلْيَعْفُوا۟ وَلْيَصْفَحُوٓا۟ — two verbs

**The verse uses two distinct verbs of pardon, and the difference between them is recorded by the classical lexicographers.**

**عَفَا — root ع-ف-و.** The root was analysed in `058-mucadele.md` and at `042-sura.md`, 42/40; I rely on those treatments. The note there: **the concrete sense of the root is to wipe out, to erase the trace** — as the wind erases footprints on sand. And its difference from *ghufrān*: **what is covered is still there but not seen; what is erased is no longer there.**

**صَفَحَ — root ص-ف-ح.** The root was analysed at `043-zuhruf.md`, 43/5 and 43/89; I rely on those treatments. The note there: **the concrete sense of the root is the name of a surface — *ṣafḥa*: the side of a thing, its flat face.**

**And the concrete senses of the two words describe two distinct acts:**

| Verb | Root | Its concrete picture | What it does |
|---|---|---|---|
| **عَفَا** | ع-ف-و | **To erase the trace** | It drops the claim — nothing is owed |
| **صَفَحَ** | ص-ف-ح | **To turn one's face, to turn the page** | It keeps the relation — no face is soured |

**The classical lexicographers count *ṣafḥ* as going further than *ʿafw***, and the ground stands in the picture of the root: to pardon is to drop a claim; *ṣafḥ* is, after that, **not to turn one's face away**, to bring the relation back to what it was.

**I transmit this distinction as an explanation the lexicographers record; I do not claim that it is an absolute rule.**

**And that the verse asks for both verbs together is worth noting.** A man may drop a claim of his and still turn his face from the person. **The verse asks for both at once.**

**And one further detail in the arrangement:** the command to pardon comes after the command **to go on giving.** The order, that is, is this:

| Order | Command |
|---|---|
| **1** | *an yuʾtū* — **go on giving** |
| **2** | *wa'l-yaʿfū* — pardon |
| **3** | *wa'l-yaṣfaḥū* — overlook |

**What belongs to the deed first, what belongs to the heart after.** The verse asks first that the hand should not stop.

**I record this as my own reading, and its ground is the order of the commands.** And I add this: had the order been reversed — first pardon, then go on giving — pardon would have been the condition of the act of giving. **In this order, giving is placed before pardon.**

### أَلَا تُحِبُّونَ أَن يَغْفِرَ ٱللَّهُ لَكُمْ — the question

**This is the strongest sentence in the verse, and it is framed as a question.**

**And the verb draws attention: *tuḥibbūn* — "do you not wish?".** The same verb occurred three verses earlier (19) in a context of censure: *yuḥibbūna an tashīʿa'l-fāḥisha* — "they **love** that it spread".

| Verse | What the loving is directed at |
|---|---|
| **19** | *an tashīʿa'l-fāḥisha* — the **spreading** of indecency |
| **22** | *an yaghfira'llāhu lakum* — God's **forgiving** |

**The same verb, three verses apart, with two opposite objects.** This is a repetition that can be verified from the text, and I record it as an observation about the word order.

**And the logic the question builds rests not upon reciprocity but upon *consistency*.**

The verse does not say "pardon so that you may be pardoned"; it sets up no exchange. **It says: you wish to be forgiven. Then you know what forgiving is.**

**I record this as my own reading;** its ground is the mood of the sentence: not a conditional but a question.

### What this says to the present

**One. A grievance making itself permanent.**

The verb in the verse's first reading — to swear — describes a behaviour: the turning of a grievance into a decision.

**And the logic of this can be understood.** A grievance grows less of itself with time. A decision does not. **An oath is a grievance's attempt to protect itself against time.**

The verse stands against this, and it does something striking: **it does not find the grievance unjust.** The verses from eleven to twenty-one condemned in the heaviest terms the act that caused the grievance. **The verse asks that a just grievance should not turn into a severance.**

**Two. On what plane the pardon is asked for.**

The verse does not lift the penalty. The sanction of the fourth verse stands where it is; the ruling that will come in the twenty-third stands where it is. **What is lifted is the requital that lies in a man's own hand.**

This distinction matters: **pardon does not take the place of justice. It stands beside justice, in a place of its own.**

**Three. And a note: this verse is not an instrument of pressure.**

To use the verse in order to tell another man "you must pardon" is to change the verse's addressee. **The verse's address is directed at the one who is to pardon — not at the one who expects something from him.**

And this agrees with the sūra's general measure: as was recorded in `049-hucurat.md`, **the text is an instrument of diagnosis, and the diagnosis is applied first to oneself.**

---

## 24/23 — إِنَّ ٱلَّذِينَ يَرْمُونَ ٱلْمُحْصَنَٰتِ ٱلْغَٰفِلَٰتِ ٱلْمُؤْمِنَٰتِ لُعِنُوا۟ فِى ٱلدُّنْيَا وَٱلْءَاخِرَةِ وَلَهُمْ عَذَابٌ عَظِيمٌ

*Inna'lladhīna yarmūna'l-muḥṣanāti'l-ghāfilāti'l-muʾmināti luʿinū fi'l-dunyā wa'l-ākhirati wa lahum ʿadhābun ʿaẓīm*

"Those who cast a charge at chaste, unsuspecting, believing women are cursed in this world and in the hereafter; and for them there is a great punishment."

### Three attributes

**The verse names the party slandered with three attributes, and all three are worth recording.**

| Attribute | Root | Its sense | What it looks at |
|---|---|---|---|
| **ٱلْمُحْصَنَات** | ح-ص-ن | Protected — like a fortress | **The actual condition** |
| **ٱلْغَٰفِلَات** | غ-ف-ل | Unsuspecting, to whom it never even occurred | **The state of mind** |
| **ٱلْمُؤْمِنَات** | أ-م-ن | Believing | **The condition in belief** |

**The second attribute is one of the most affecting words in the sūra and deserves separate treatment.**

### ٱلْغَٰفِلَٰت — those who are unaware

**Root: غ-ف-ل.** Its concrete sense is **to be unaware of a thing, not to hold it in mind, to have it escape one's notice.** *Ghafla* — inattention; *ghufl* — bearing no mark upon it, unbranded (used of a piece of land or of a camel).

**The second sense of the root is interesting: *ghufl* is what bears no trace or mark upon it.** An unbranded camel, a stretch of land with no track across it. **At the centre of the word, then, there is also the idea of "bearing no mark".**

**And in the verse's context this builds a picture:** the party slandered is the party upon whom there is no mark at all. **And slander is an attempt to set a mark where there is none.**

I record this as **my own reading**; the second sense of the root is a usage the lexicographers record.

**And the attribute has another side:** *ghāfilāt* means "those to whom such a thing never even occurred". **The attribute conveys, that is, not only that the act was not done but that there was not even a thought of it in the mind.**

**This sense of the word is widely recorded in the classical commentaries.**

### لُعِنُوا۟ — the passive voice

**The verb is passive: *luʿinū* — "they are cursed".** Its agent is not named.

**And the tense is perfect.** The ruling is announced not as something to be given in the future but as **already given.**

**The root ل-ع-ن** occurred in the sixth and seventh verses in the discussion of *liʿān*: *laʿnatu'llāhi ʿalayh.* **The same word, in two different places:**

| Verse | Who says it | Upon whom |
|---|---|---|
| **7** | **A man** — about himself, on a condition | Upon himself, if he is lying |
| **23** | **The text** — as a ruling | Upon those who cast the charge |

**What a man calls down upon himself conditionally in the seventh verse is announced as a ruling in the twenty-third.** This is an observation about the word order, and the places of the words stand in the text.

### The difference from the fourth verse

**The verse repeats the verb of the fourth (*yarmūn*) but announces a different outcome:**

| | 24/4 | 24/23 |
|---|---|---|
| Verb | *yarmūna'l-muḥṣanāt* | *yarmūna'l-muḥṣanāti'l-ghāfilāti'l-muʾmināt* |
| Outcome | **A worldly sanction** — eighty strokes, the rejection of testimony | **A ruling of the hereafter** — a curse, a great punishment |
| Condition | The failure to bring four witnesses | — |
| A note of repentance | **Present** (24/5) | — |

**The two verses give the counterpart of the same act on two different planes.** And the increase in the attributes (three of them) shows that the second verse takes as its subject a narrower case — the case in which innocence is certain.

**I record this as an observation about the word order.**

---

## 24/24 — يَوْمَ تَشْهَدُ عَلَيْهِمْ أَلْسِنَتُهُمْ وَأَيْدِيهِمْ وَأَرْجُلُهُم بِمَا كَانُوا۟ يَعْمَلُونَ

*Yawma tashhadu ʿalayhim alsinatuhum wa aydīhim wa arjuluhum bimā kānū yaʿmalūn*

"On the day when their tongues, their hands and their feet will bear witness against them concerning what they used to do."

### The return of the root of witnessing

**Up to now the root ش-ه-د has occurred four times in the sūra: at 2 (*wa'l-yashhad*), at 4 (*shuhadāʾ*, *shahādatan*), at 6 and 8 (*shuhadāʾ*, *shahādāt*), at 13 (*shuhadāʾ*).**

**And now it occurs for a fifth time — but the witness has changed.**

| Verse | Who is the witness | To what |
|---|---|---|
| **2** | *ṭāʾifatun mina'l-muʾminīn* | To the carrying out of the penalty |
| **4, 13** | **Four persons** | To the truth of the claim |
| **6-9** | **The man himself** — by oath | To his own claim |
| **24** | **The man's own limbs** | **Against the man himself** |

**The sūra builds a chain of witnessing, and at the end of the chain the witness and the accused meet in the same body.**

**I record this as my own reading;** the distribution of the root can be counted in the text.

### أَلْسِنَتُهُمْ — the tongues named first

**The order of the limbs is worth noting: tongue, then hand, then foot.**

**In similar verses of the Qurʾān the order may differ** (at Fuṣṣilat 41/20-21, hearing, eyes and skins; at Yā Sīn 36/65, mouths, hands and feet). **That the tongue is put first here has directly to do with the subject of the section.**

**For the act treated in the verses from eleven to twenty-three was an act done with the tongue:**

| Verse | Organ | Expression |
|---|---|---|
| **15** | **Tongue** | *talaqqawnahū bi-**alsinatikum*** |
| **15** | **Mouth** | *taqūlūna bi-**afwāhikum*** |
| **24** | **Tongue** | *tashhadu ʿalayhim **alsinatuhum*** |

**The same organ, nine verses apart, in two opposite tasks: first it carries the report, then it bears witness against the one who carried it.**

**I record this ring as my own reading;** the places of the words and their sameness are the wording of the text.

### تَشْهَدُ عَلَيْهِمْ — "against them"

**The preposition is worth noting: *ʿalā* — "upon, against".** In Arabic *shahida li* (he bore witness in someone's favour) is distinguished from *shahida ʿalā* (he bore witness against him). **Here it is the second.**

**And the picture the sentence builds is this:** a man is given up by his own body. There is no possibility of defence, because the other party is no stranger.

**This is the reverse of the word *buhtān* in the sixteenth verse, and I record it as my own reading:** there the one slandered could not answer, because he had no evidence in hand. **Here the one who slandered cannot answer, because the evidence is his own body.**

---

## 24/25 — يَوْمَئِذٍ يُوَفِّيهِمُ ٱللَّهُ دِينَهُمُ ٱلْحَقَّ وَيَعْلَمُونَ أَنَّ ٱللَّهَ هُوَ ٱلْحَقُّ ٱلْمُبِينُ

*Yawmaʾidhin yuwaffīhimu'llāhu dīnahumu'l-ḥaqqa wa yaʿlamūna anna'llāha huwa'l-Ḥaqqu'l-Mubīn*

"On that day God will pay them their due in full, and they will know that God is the plain Truth."

### يُوَفِّي — to pay in full

**Root: و-ف-ي.** Its concrete sense is **to fulfil a thing completely, without any lack.** *Wafāʾ* — to keep one's word in full. *Awfā* — he measured in full. *Tawfiya* — payment without deficiency.

**And the word is the opposite of the root treated in `083-mutaffifin.md`:** *taṭfīf* is short measure; *tawfiya* is full measure.

### دِينَهُمُ ٱلْحَقَّ — "their due"

**دِين** — root **د-ي-ن**. In Arabic the word has three fields of sense, and all three issue from this root:

| Sense | Example |
|---|---|
| **A debt, what is to be paid** | *dayn* — a debt |
| **Requital, recompense, judgement** | *yawmu'l-dīn* — the day of reckoning |
| **Religion, a way** | *dīn* |

**The sense here is the second: the recompense that is due.** The word's link with "debt" joins with the verb of the verse: *yuwaffī* — to pay in full. **The verse, that is, builds a scene of payment.**

**And the attribute *al-ḥaqq* is worth noting:** the recompense is not only "in full" but also "just". The two words reinforce one another: complete, and in its right place.

### ٱلْحَقُّ ٱلْمُبِينُ — the sūra's first pairing of divine names

**This is the only place in the sūra where the name *al-Ḥaqq* occurs, and the only place where the root ب-ي-ن comes together with a divine name.**

**And an observation about the word order may be recorded:** the section between the eleventh and the twenty-fifth verses opened with a **slander** and closes with the name **al-Ḥaqq al-Mubīn.**

| The head of the section | The end of the section |
|---|---|
| *al-ifk* (11) — what is **turned about** | *al-Ḥaqq* (25) — what is **true** |
| *ifkun **mubīn*** (12) — a plain slander | *al-Ḥaqqu'l-**Mubīn*** (25) — the plain Truth |

**The two pairs of words stand facing each other:** in the twelfth verse the believers were asked to say *hādhā ifkun mubīn*; in the twenty-fifth God is named *al-Ḥaqq al-Mubīn*. **The same adjective, upon two opposite objects.**

**I record this as my own reading;** the wording of the two expressions stands in the text and can be compared.

---

## 24/26 — ٱلْخَبِيثَٰتُ لِلْخَبِيثِينَ وَٱلْخَبِيثُونَ لِلْخَبِيثَٰتِ وَٱلطَّيِّبَٰتُ لِلطَّيِّبِينَ وَٱلطَّيِّبُونَ لِلطَّيِّبَٰتِ أُو۟لَٰٓئِكَ مُبَرَّءُونَ مِمَّا يَقُولُونَ لَهُم مَّغْفِرَةٌ وَرِزْقٌ كَرِيمٌ

*Al-khabīthātu li'l-khabīthīna wa'l-khabīthūna li'l-khabīthāt, wa'l-ṭayyibātu li'l-ṭayyibīna wa'l-ṭayyibūna li'l-ṭayyibāt, ulāʾika mubarraʾūna mimmā yaqūlūn, lahum maghfiratun wa rizqun karīm*

"The corrupt are for the corrupt, and the corrupt for the corrupt; the good are for the good, and the good for the good. Those are free of what is said. For them there is forgiveness and a generous provision."

### The disagreement over how the verse is to be understood

**This verse is one of the verses in the Qurʾān most disputed as to how it is to be understood, and the cause of the disagreement is that what the subjects of the sentence are is not stated.**

*Al-khabīthāt* and *al-ṭayyibāt* — feminine plural adjectives. **But what they are adjectives of is not said.** In Arabic this pattern can answer to **persons**, to **words**, or to **acts**.

**Three main readings are transmitted in the classical commentaries:**

| Reading | What *khabīthāt* / *ṭayyibāt* are | The sense of the sentence | Its ground |
|---|---|---|---|
| **1. Words** | **Speech** (*kalimāt*, *aqwāl*) | Bad words befit bad speakers, good words good speakers, and issue from them | The context: the *mimmā yaqūlūn* — "of what they say" — that follows the clause; the whole section is about **speech** |
| **2. Persons** | **Human beings** | Bad women befit bad men, good women good men | The masculine-feminine opposition of the words; the verse's being understood within the field of marriage |
| **3. Acts, states** | **Deeds** | Bad deeds belong to the bad, good deeds to the good | The general use of the word |

**No preference is imposed in this section, and none is binding.**

**But a note of method needs to be entered, and it is a direct requirement of the method (`STYLE.md`): no ruling about persons can be drawn from this verse.**

**The reason lies within the verse and can be shown from the arrangement:**

- The second half of the verse itself states the conclusion to be drawn from the first: *ulāʾika mubarraʾūna mimmā yaqūlūn* — **"they are free of what is said."** The point the sentence arrives at, that is, is not the classifying of anyone; it is the announcing of **the invalidity of something that has been said.**
- And the verse comes at the end of a whole section (11-26). The subject of that section, from beginning to end, is what it is **to lay down a ruling about people without evidence.**

**To use the verse in order to divide people into two groups, then, is the very thing the section it stands in forbids.**

**I record this as my own reading, and its ground is the second half of the verse.**

**And there is one further practical consequence:** to establish which group a person falls into, the condition of proof the sūra itself has laid down (four witnesses, 24/4 and 24/13) is required. **The verse, that is, cannot be read apart from the sūra's own procedure.**

### خَبِيث — root خ-ب-ث

**This root is analysed here for the first time in this commentary.**

The sense the lexicographers record: **a thing's being corrupt, foul, useless in its substance.** *Khubth* — badness, filth. *Khabath* — the dross that rises to the top when a metal is melted, the part that is thrown away.

**And this last usage gives the concrete picture of the root:** the scum that rises to the surface when gold or silver is melted, and is discarded, is called **khabath.**

**At the centre of the word, then, there is an idea of *separating out*:** the part that separates under fire and is thrown away.
