**I record this as an observation about the root** and add this: this picture in the word agrees with the structure of the verse's sentence. **The verse describes a separation** — but does not say how the separation comes about.

### طَيِّب — root ط-ي-ب

**This root was analysed at `002-bakara.md`, 2/168; I rely on that treatment.** The note there: **what is pleasant, clean, good; the word answers both to what is pleasant to the senses and to what is clean in its substance.**

**The opposition of the two roots:**

| | خ-ب-ث | ط-ي-ب |
|---|---|---|
| Its concrete picture | **Dross** — the scum that separates out in melting | **What is pleasant** — of taste, of smell, of substance |
| What it looks at | The corruption of the substance | The cleanness of the substance |
| Its field in the Qurʾān | Property, speech, deed, person | Property, speech, deed, person, place |

**That both roots are used in the same fields is one of the reasons for the indefiniteness in the verse.** The words do not, by themselves, settle what they are adjectives of.

### مُبَرَّءُونَ — "those who are held clear"

**Root: ب-ر-أ.** Its concrete sense is **to part from a thing, to slip free of it, to be delivered.** *Barāʾa* — a slipping free of an obligation. *Bāriʾ* — the Maker (who separates a thing out of nothing); this name was taken up at `059-hasr.md`, 59/24. *Bariʾa mina'l-maraḍ* — he recovered from the illness.

**The form is worth noting: *mubarraʾūn* — the passive participle of Form II.**

**It is not said "they cleared themselves". It is said "they have been held clear".** The agent is not named, but it is understood from the sentence's context.

**And this is the answer given to the matter of *buhtān* in the sixteenth verse, and I record it as my own reading:**

It was recorded there: **the one slandered cannot defend himself, because a thing that did not happen leaves no trace.** The twenty-sixth verse takes that defence **out of the man's hands** and puts it elsewhere: the passive voice.

**Acquittal, that is, comes not by being proved but by being announced.**

### مِمَّا يَقُولُونَ — "of what they say"

**The verse names in a single word the thing they are held clear of: *mā yaqūlūn* — "what they say".**

**At the end of the section, that is, the whole matter is reduced to one word: speech.**

**And this forms a ring with the section's opening:**

| Verse | Expression |
|---|---|
| **11** | *jāʾū bi'l-**ifk*** — they **brought** the slander |
| **15** | *ta**qūlū**na bi-afwāhikum* — you **say** with your mouths |
| **16** | *an nata**kallama** bi-hādhā* — to **speak** of this |
| **26** | *mimmā ya**qūlū**n* — **of what they say** |

**The same root (ق-و-ل) three times in the section, and the section closes with it.**

I record this as an observation about the word order.

### لَهُم مَّغْفِرَةٌ وَرِزْقٌ كَرِيمٌ — the close

**The section closes with two promises: *maghfira* and *rizqun karīm*.**

**كَرِيم** — root **ك-ر-م**: precious, generous, noble. The word was analysed at `049-hucurat.md`, 49/13 (*akramakum*).

**And the placing of the word *rizq* here is worth noting.** The subject of the section was a matter of standing; at its close a word of **material favour** comes. In the classical commentaries it is recorded that the attribute *karīm* qualifies the kind of the provision: provision given without being kept waiting for, without reproach, without deficiency.

---

## 24/27 — يَٰٓأَيُّهَا ٱلَّذِينَ ءَامَنُوا۟ لَا تَدْخُلُوا۟ بُيُوتًا غَيْرَ بُيُوتِكُمْ حَتَّىٰ تَسْتَأْنِسُوا۟ وَتُسَلِّمُوا۟ عَلَىٰٓ أَهْلِهَا ذَٰلِكُمْ خَيْرٌ لَّكُمْ لَعَلَّكُمْ تَذَكَّرُونَ

*Yā ayyuhā'lladhīna āmanū lā tadkhulū buyūtan ghayra buyūtikum ḥattā tastaʾnisū wa tusallimū ʿalā ahlihā, dhālikum khayrun lakum laʿallakum tadhakkarūn*

"O you who believe, do not enter houses other than your own until you have gained leave by **establishing familiarity** and by greeting their people. That is better for you, that you may take heed."

### The opening of the block and its place in the sūra

**With the twenty-seventh verse the sūra passes into a new block, and the passage is marked by an address: *yā ayyuhā'lladhīna āmanū*.**

**And the block is the exact counterpart of the block before it.**

The verses from eleven to twenty-six took as their subject a word's entering into a person's **privacy.** **The twenty-seventh verse takes up the same matter on the physical plane: a body's entering into a house.**

| Block | The boundary of what | The instrument of violation |
|---|---|---|
| **11-26** | The boundary of **standing** | Speech |
| **27-29** | The boundary of **the house** | The body |
| **30-31** | The boundary of **the body** | The glance |

**Three blocks, three distinct boundaries and three distinct instruments of violation.** The sūra repeats the same idea on three planes.

**I record this as my own reading;** the subjects of the blocks and their order stand in the text.

**And this is the direct continuation of the matter of privacy recorded in the section on the sūra in `049-hucurat.md`.** Two notes were entered there:

> **In the discussion of 49/4:** *"a physical boundary can be crossed by a voice. A door may be shut and a man may still disturb the one inside. Privacy is not kept by a wall alone; it is kept by the wall's being respected."*
>
> **In the discussion of 49/12:** *"The sūra takes up privacy twice: in the fourth verse as a physical boundary, in the twelfth as a boundary of knowledge."*

**al-Nūr takes up the same two layers — but reverses the order:** first the boundary of knowledge (11-26), then the physical boundary (27-29).

**And al-Nūr adds a third layer:** the **glance**, which is not in al-Ḥujurāt (30-31).

| | **49. al-Ḥujurāt** | **24. al-Nūr** |
|---|---|---|
| **Physical boundary** | 49/4-5 — calling from behind the chambers | 24/27-29 — entering a house without leave |
| **Boundary of knowledge** | 49/12 — prying | 24/4, 12-19 — an unproved claim and its spreading |
| **Boundary of the glance** | — | **24/30-31** |
| **The name of the boundary** | *ḥujurāt* (ح-ج-ر — to enclose) | *buyūt* (ب-ي-ت — the place where one passes the night) |
| **The form of the command** | Wait (*ṣabarū*, 49/5) | Ask leave (*tastaʾnisū*, 24/27) |

**The two sūras complete each other**, and I record this table as my own reading; the places and subjects of the verses stand in the text.

### بُيُوتًا غَيْرَ بُيُوتِكُمْ — the choice of the word

**بَيْت — root ب-ي-ت.** Its concrete sense is **to pass the night.** *Bāta* — he passed the night; *bayāt* — a passing of the night. **And *bayt* — the place where the night is passed.**

**That the word comes from this root is worth noting, and I record it as an observation about the root:** in Arabic a house is named not "the place one dwells in" or "the place one is protected in" but **"the place where one passes the night".**

**And the night is the time when a man is least protected.** The sūra's fifty-eighth verse will regulate this separately: three times will be enumerated at which leave must be asked, and two of them will have to do with the night.

**I record this connection as my own reading;** the sense of the root stands in the dictionary.

**And the wording of the expression is worth noting: *buyūtan ghayra buyūtikum* — "houses other than your houses".**

**The verse does not say "do not enter another's house"; it says "houses other than your own".** The boundary, that is, is drawn not by ownership but by **a person's own domain.** And *buyūtikum* is plural: a man may have more than one house, or the address is directed at the community.

### تَسْتَأْنِسُوا۟ — the root أ-ن-س and growing warm

**This is the most striking word in the verse and will be taken up here in detail.**

**Root: أ-ن-س.** The root was analysed at `028-kasas.md`, 28/29; I rely on that treatment. The note there:

> **ءَانَسَ — root أ-ن-س: to catch sight of, to grow warm.** From the same root come *ins* (man) and *uns* (familiarity). **The lexicographers record the word's sense of "to sense a light from afar".**

**The derivatives of the root:**

| Word | Sense |
|---|---|
| **إِنْس** (*ins*) | Man — the name of the kind |
| **إِنْسَان** (*insān*) | A human being |
| **أُنْس** (*uns*) | Familiarity, growing warm, becoming used to |
| **أَنِيس** (*anīs*) | One with whom a man grows warm, a companion |
| **ٱسْتِئْنَاس** (*istiʾnās*) | To seek familiarity; and to ask leave |
| **مُسْتَأْنِس** | Accustomed, warmed to |

**And now the verse's most important question: why is the verb of asking leave chosen from this root?**

**In Arabic there is a direct verb for "to ask leave": *istaʾdhana* (root: أ-ذ-ن).** And the sūra uses this verb in many places: in the twenty-eighth verse (*yuʾdhana lakum*), in the fifty-eighth (*li-yastaʾdhinkum*), in the fifty-ninth (*fa'l-yastaʾdhinū*), in the sixty-second (*ḥattā yastaʾdhinūh*).

**But in the twenty-seventh verse — that is, in the first and founding command — this verb is not used. *Tastaʾnisū* is used.**

**This is the most important point in the verse in point of language, and it has been dwelt on in the classical commentaries too.**

**What the roots of the two verbs say:**

| Verb | Root | The concrete sense of the root | What it conveys |
|---|---|---|---|
| **ٱسْتَأْذَنَ** | أ-ذ-ن | **The ear** (*udhun*) — to hear, to give leave | **A request for leave** — the asking of a permission |
| **ٱسْتَأْنَسَ** | أ-ن-س | **To grow warm, to establish familiarity** | **The seeking of familiarity** — the making of a relation |

**And the difference is this, which I record as my own reading:**

***Istiʾdhān* runs one way:** one party asks, the other gives or does not give. It is a transaction.

***Istiʾnās* runs both ways:** in its root there is a mutual growing warm. **To stand at the door and ask leave is not enough; the one behind the door must also not be discomposed.**

**The verse, that is, frames the act of asking leave not as a *procedure* but as a *relation*.**

**And its concrete counterpart is recorded thus in the classical commentaries** — I transmit them as explanations that are transmitted:

| Explanation | Its content |
|---|---|
| **1** | *Istiʾnās* is the making of one's arrival known: coughing, letting one's footfall be heard, calling out — giving the one inside the chance to make ready |
| **2** | *Istiʾnās* is the asking of leave itself; the word is used in that sense |
| **3** | *Istiʾnās* is trying to gather from the state of the one inside whether entry is fitting |

**I impose no preference.** But what the three explanations have in common is worth noting: **all of them include the visitor's making himself known beforehand.**

**And the measure recorded at `049-hucurat.md`, 49/4-5, is completed here:**

There those who called out were censured — **because there was no waiting.** And what was commanded there was patience: *wa law annahum ṣabarū ḥattā takhruja ilayhim.*

**al-Nūr 24/27 regulates the other side of the same matter:** there what was censured was **calling out from behind the wall**; here what is commanded is **establishing familiarity before the door.**

**The difference between the two verses lies in the direction of the voice, and I record this as my own reading:**

| | al-Ḥujurāt 49/4 | al-Nūr 24/27 |
|---|---|---|
| Whose the voice is | **The one outside** — forcing the inside | **The one outside** — making himself known |
| Its purpose | To **bring out** the one inside | To **give the one inside a chance to make ready** |
| The ruling | *Aktharuhum lā yaʿqilūn* | *Dhālikum khayrun lakum* |

**The same act (making a sound), with two different intentions, receives two opposite rulings.** The difference lies in whom the sound is made for.

### وَتُسَلِّمُوا۟ عَلَىٰٓ أَهْلِهَا — the greeting

**سَلَّمَ — root س-ل-م.** Its concrete sense is **to be sound, entire, without flaw.** *Salāma* — being clear of harm. *Salām* — a noun from this root.

**And the function of the greeting here is worth recording:** a greeting is an address, and **it is the acknowledgement of a person's existence.**

**The two verbs the verse asks for, then, do two distinct works:**

| Verb | Root | What it does |
|---|---|---|
| ***tastaʾnisū*** | أ-ن-س | **To make oneself known** — to announce one's arrival |
| ***tusallimū*** | س-ل-م | **To acknowledge the other** — to greet his presence |

**The one looks towards oneself, the other towards the one opposite.**

**And the verb from the root س-ل-م will return twice more in the sūra:** in the sixty-first verse, *fa-sallimū ʿalā anfusikum* — "greet yourselves". **There is a ring between the two verses, and it will be taken up at the sixty-first.**

### حَتَّىٰ — the definiteness of the boundary

**The particle is worth noting: *ḥattā* — "until".** In Arabic this particle conveys a **limit** (*ghāya*).

**The prohibition, that is, lasts up to a certain point, and when that point comes it is lifted.** The prohibition is not absolute but **conditional.**

**And the same particle is repeated through the sūra:**

| Verse | What *ḥattā* attaches to |
|---|---|
| **27** | *ḥattā tastaʾnisū wa tusallimū* — until you have established familiarity and greeted |
| **28** | *ḥattā yuʾdhana lakum* — until leave is given you |
| **33** | *ḥattā yughniyahumu'llāhu min faḍlih* — until God enriches them of His bounty |
| **62** | *ḥattā yastaʾdhinūh* — until they ask leave of him |

**In all four usages the particle conveys a waiting.** I record this as an observation about the word order.

### ذَٰلِكُمْ خَيْرٌ لَّكُمْ لَعَلَّكُمْ تَذَكَّرُونَ — a ring with the first verse

**The verse-ending is the same as that of the sūra's first verse: *laʿallakum tadhakkarūn*.**

**This verse-ending occurs twice in the sūra: at 1 and at 27.** And both occurrences stand in verses that open a distinct block of the sūra.

**I record this as an observation about the word order;** the places of the verse-endings can be counted in the text.

**And the expression *khayrun lakum* is worth noting too.** The same expression occurred in the eleventh verse: *bal huwa khayrun lakum.* **In both places it is said that something hard for the addressee is for his own good.**

### What this says to the present

**One. The root of the verb of asking leave.**

The root the verse chooses — *uns*, to grow warm — settles what asking leave is: **not a transaction but a relation.**

The practical counterpart of this is that leave may be asked and given in form, and yet what the verse asks for may not have come about. **To knock at a door and walk in is different from knocking at a door and waiting for the one inside to be ready.**

**Two. Where the boundary lies.**

The verse sets the door of the house as a boundary. **And as was recorded at `049-hucurat.md`, 49/4, a man's door today is not only the door of his house:**

> *"His telephone, his message box, the end of his working hours, his day off — each of these is a chamber: an enclosed, separated space."*

**What al-Nūr 24/27 adds is what is to be done in entering that space:** first make yourself known, then greet the other, then wait for leave. **Three steps, and all three before entering.**

**Three. And the addressee of the command.**

The verse does not tell the householder "lock your door". **It tells the one entering "ask leave".** The obligation lies not with the one who needs protecting but with the one who approaches the boundary.

**This is the same logic recorded at `049-hucurat.md`, 49/6:** there too the obligation lay not with the one who spread the report but with **the one who received it.** **Both sūras place the obligation upon the party whose behaviour is in his own hands.**

---

## 24/28 — فَإِن لَّمْ تَجِدُوا۟ فِيهَآ أَحَدًا فَلَا تَدْخُلُوهَا حَتَّىٰ يُؤْذَنَ لَكُمْ وَإِن قِيلَ لَكُمُ ٱرْجِعُوا۟ فَٱرْجِعُوا۟ هُوَ أَزْكَىٰ لَكُمْ وَٱللَّهُ بِمَا تَعْمَلُونَ عَلِيمٌ

*Fa-in lam tajidū fīhā aḥadan fa-lā tadkhulūhā ḥattā yuʾdhana lakum, wa in qīla lakumu'rjiʿū fa'rjiʿū, huwa azkā lakum, wa'llāhu bimā taʿmalūna ʿalīm*

"If you find no one in them, do not enter until leave is given you. And if you are told 'go back', then go back; that is more purifying for you. God knows what you do."

### Two possibilities, two rulings

**The verse fills the two gaps the twenty-seventh left:**

| Possibility | Ruling |
|---|---|
| **No one is in the house** | Do not enter — until leave is given |
| **You are told "go back"** | **Go back** |

**And what the two rulings have in common is worth recording: in both, the prohibition rests upon an *absence of answer* or upon a *refusal*.**

**The first possibility looks unnecessary at first:** if there is no one, there is no one to give leave either; so what is there to wait for?

**But the verse's logic is this, and I record it as my own reading:** the twenty-seventh verse had commanded the asking of leave. **And a man who gets no answer is inclined to count leave as given** — "there is no one, so I may go in". **The verse closes off that inference.**

**Silence, that is, is not leave.** This is a concrete note that can be read from the arrangement.

### وَإِن قِيلَ لَكُمُ ٱرْجِعُوا۟ فَٱرْجِعُوا۟ — accepting the refusal

**The second half of the verse does something finer than the first, and its arrangement is worth noting.**

**The same verb occurs twice: *irjiʿū* (go back) and *fa'rjiʿū* (then go back).** The one is the word spoken, the other the command given.

**The verse, that is, puts the householder's word directly in the place of a ruling.** No ground is asked for, no reason is required, no insistence is made.

**And this is a thing worth recording:** the verse lays no obligation upon the householder to "say why". **The refusal is valid without being explained.**

**I record this as my own reading;** its ground is that the verse lays down no condition of a reason.

### هُوَ أَزْكَىٰ لَكُمْ — purification

**The root ز-ك-و was analysed at the twenty-first verse.** Its concrete sense: **to increase, to grow; and to be cleansed.**

**The form is an elative: *azkā* — "more purifying, cleaner".** And the other side of the comparison is not stated: more purifying than what?

**The rule the classical lexicographers record is this: the omission of the compared term after an elative leaves the comparison wide.** "Than insisting", "than being hurt", "than resenting" — all fall within its scope.

**And the placing of the word *azkā* here says something worth recording, which I give as my own reading:**

To be turned back from a door touches a man's pride. **And the verse gives this situation the name of a *purification*.** Going back, that is, is placed not as something lost but as **something gained.**

**And the same word will come again two verses later:** *dhālika **azkā** lahum* (30) — the outcome of lowering the glance. **Both commands are commands to *hold back*, and both are rewarded with the same word.**

| Verse | What is held back from | The outcome |
|---|---|---|
| **28** | Insisting — at the door | *azkā lakum* |
| **30** | The glance | *azkā lahum* |

**I record this parallel as my own reading;** the sameness of the words is the wording of the text.

### وَٱللَّهُ بِمَا تَعْمَلُونَ عَلِيمٌ — the verse-ending

**The verse-ending is almost the same as that of `049-hucurat.md`, 49/18:** *wa'llāhu baṣīrun bimā taʿmalūn.*

**The difference is a single word: al-Ḥujurāt has *baṣīr* (seeing), al-Nūr *ʿalīm* (knowing).**

| Sūra | Verse-ending | The divine name |
|---|---|---|
| **al-Ḥujurāt 49/18** | *wa'llāhu **baṣīrun** bimā taʿmalūn* | **Seeing** |
| **al-Nūr 24/28** | *wa'llāhu bimā taʿmalūna **ʿalīm*** | **Knowing** |

**And the difference agrees with the subject of the two sūras, and I record this as my own reading:**

The last verse of al-Ḥujurāt took as its subject a man's **claim about himself** — something that cannot be told apart from outside. There *baṣīr* is fitting: **the one who sees what is not seen.**

al-Nūr 24/28 takes as its subject something that happens before a door — a moment no one sees: the door is knocked at, no answer comes, and the man decides alone what to do. **There *ʿalīm* is fitting: the one who knows what is not known.**

**This is not a claim of preference but an observation of reading.**

---

## 24/29 — لَّيْسَ عَلَيْكُمْ جُنَاحٌ أَن تَدْخُلُوا۟ بُيُوتًا غَيْرَ مَسْكُونَةٍ فِيهَا مَتَٰعٌ لَّكُمْ وَٱللَّهُ يَعْلَمُ مَا تُبْدُونَ وَمَا تَكْتُمُونَ

*Laysa ʿalaykum junāḥun an tadkhulū buyūtan ghayra maskūnatin fīhā matāʿun lakum, wa'llāhu yaʿlamu mā tubdūna wa mā taktumūn*

"There is no fault in your entering houses that are not lived in, in which there is something of use to you. God knows what you make public and what you conceal."

### The logic of the exception

**The verse brings an exception to the prohibition laid down two verses before, and the exception has two conditions:**

| Condition | Expression | What it conveys |
|---|---|---|
| **1** | *ghayra maskūna* | **Not lived in** — no one's life is within it |
| **2** | *fīhā matāʿun lakum* | **Something of yours is in it** — there is a reason to enter |

**And that the two conditions are required together is worth noting.** The first alone is not enough: it is not said that every uninhabited house may be entered. **The second condition asks for a *reason*.**

**I record this as an observation about the word order.**

### مَسْكُون — root س-ك-ن

**Root: س-ك-ن.** Its concrete sense is **the stopping of motion, coming to rest.** *Sakana* — it stopped, it grew still. *Sakīna* — tranquillity. *Maskūn* — dwelt in, inhabited. *Sakīna* and *sukūn* are from the same root.

**And the choice of the word gives a definition: what makes a place a "house" is someone's *coming to rest* within it.**

**The verse's exception, that is, looks not at a building but at a life.** Not every structure with walls is a protected space; what is protected is someone living there.

**I record this as my own reading;** the sense of the root stands in the dictionary.

### جُنَاح — root ج-ن-ح

**Root: ج-ن-ح.** Its concrete sense is **to lean, to incline to one side.** *Janāḥ* — a wing (the limb that folds to the side of the body). *Janaḥa* — he inclined.

**And *junāḥ*: a straying from the right road by leaning aside; whence "fault, objection, blame".**

**The picture in the root of the word is worth noting:** *junāḥ* is a **swerving aside** from the straight road. Its first sense, that is, is an error of direction, not a weight.

**And the word occurs four times in the sūra:**

| Verse | Clause | What is lifted |
|---|---|---|
| **29** | *laysa ʿalaykum junāḥun an tadkhulū* | Entering a house that is not lived in |
| **58** | *laysa ʿalaykum wa lā ʿalayhim junāḥun baʿdahunna* | Asking leave outside the three times |
| **60** | *fa-laysa ʿalayhinna junāḥun* | Elderly women's laying aside the outer garment |
| **61** | *laysa ʿalaykum junāḥun an taʾkulū* | Eating together or apart |

**All four usages are the *lifting* of an obligation.** And all four stand in the sūra's blocks of manners.

**This is a characteristic of the sūra, and I record it as my own reading:** in laying down rules of manners the sūra writes beside every rule **the place where that rule does not apply.** **A rule is set, and then the rule's limit is set.**

### وَٱللَّهُ يَعْلَمُ مَا تُبْدُونَ وَمَا تَكْتُمُونَ — the verse-ending

**Two verbs stand face to face:**

**أَبْدَىٰ — root ب-د-و:** to come out into the open, to appear. *Bādī* — appearing; *badā* — it came out. **The root will return in the thirty-first verse:** *lā yubdīna zīnatahunn* — "let them not display their adornment". **The same verb, in the same form.**

**كَتَمَ — root ك-ت-م:** to hide, to conceal, to keep within.

**And the placing of the verse-ending here enters a note upon the whole block.**

The block was speaking of a **physical boundary**: the door, the house, the entering. **And the verse-ending names the thing behind the physical boundary: intention.**

**The verse says this: the form of what is done before the door is visible; the purpose behind that act is not. And the party that holds the measure knows them both.**

**I record this as my own reading;** the opposition of the two verbs is the wording of the text.

---

## 24/30 — قُل لِّلْمُؤْمِنِينَ يَغُضُّوا۟ مِنْ أَبْصَٰرِهِمْ وَيَحْفَظُوا۟ فُرُوجَهُمْ ذَٰلِكَ أَزْكَىٰ لَهُمْ إِنَّ ٱللَّهَ خَبِيرٌۢ بِمَا يَصْنَعُونَ

*Qul li'l-muʾminīna yaghuḍḍū min abṣārihim wa yaḥfaẓū furūjahum, dhālika azkā lahum, inna'llāha khabīrun bimā yaṣnaʿūn*

"Say to the believing men that they **lower part of** their glances and guard their chastity. That is more purifying for them. God is aware of what they do."

### First, a note of method

**This verse and the one that follows are among the places in this commentary that must be written with the greatest care. I therefore write at the outset what is not done:**

- **No legal ruling is issued.** The rulings drawn from these two verses diverge among the schools and within each school. The disagreements are **transmitted**, none is preferred, and **none is binding.**
- **No current controversy and no politics is entered.** How these verses are applied today, who does what, and the public arguments around it are not the subject of this text.
- **The reader is not scolded.** These verses are among the places that have produced the most scolding in the language of preaching. This section does not do that.

**What will be treated: the roots of the words, their forms, the particles, and the word order.**

### The first observation on the arrangement: the command is given to the men first

**The thirtieth verse addresses men, the thirty-first women. And that is the order.**

**This is a fact that can be verified from the text and needs recording.** In the tradition of commentary and of preaching the subject has been taken up mainly by way of the thirty-first verse. **But the text's own order is this: the command is given to the men first.**

**And the first clauses of the two verses are word for word the same:**

| | 24/30 (to the men) | 24/31 (to the women) |
|---|---|---|
| The address | *qul li'l-muʾminīn* | *wa qul li'l-muʾmināt* |
| The first command | *yaghuḍḍū min abṣārihim* | *yaghḍuḍna min abṣārihinna* |
| The second command | *wa yaḥfaẓū furūjahum* | *wa yaḥfaẓna furūjahunna* |

**The first two commands, in both verses, are the same verb, the same form, the same particle.** The only difference is the gender of the pronoun.

**And the thirty-first verse continues from there;** the thirtieth does not. This difference will be taken up at the thirty-first verse.

**I offer no interpretation of the order itself;** this is the text's order, and I content myself with recording it. **As an observation about the word order this may be said:** the pair of verses does not frame the regulating of the glance as a one-sided obligation.

### يَغُضُّوا۟ — the root غ-ض-ض

**Root: غ-ض-ض.** This root was analysed at `031-lokman.md`, 31/19, and at `049-hucurat.md`, 49/3; I rely on those treatments.

**The note in Luqmān:** *"The root غ-ض-ض: **to lessen, to reduce, to lower.**"*

**The note in al-Ḥujurāt** (49/3 — *yaghuḍḍūna aṣwātahum*): there the word was used of the voice.

**The concrete sense of the root: to lessen the amount, the force or the loudness of a thing.** In Arabic the word is used both of the voice and of the glance. And *ghaḍīḍ* — fresh, tender (not yet fully opened).

**And the verse's choice of word is worth recording:**

In Arabic there are verbs for "to shut the eye" (*ghamaḍa*), for "not to look" (*lā yanẓur*), for "to turn one's face away" (*aʿraḍa*). **The verse uses none of them.**

**The verb it uses is a verb of *lessening*.**

### مِنْ أَبْصَٰرِهِمْ — the particle *min* and the partitive

**This is the most important point in the verse in point of language, and exactly the same structure was treated in `031-lokman.md`.**

**The note at Luqmān 31/19 was this:**

> **And the particle is worth recording: *ighḍuḍ min ṣawtik* — "lower part of your voice".** Here *min* conveys the **partitive** (a part of it). **The verse, that is, asks not for silence but for the lowering of part of the voice.**

**And at al-Nūr 24/30 the same verb comes with the same particle and in the same structure: *yaghuḍḍū min abṣārihim*.**

**A comparison of the two verses:**

| | **Luqmān 31/19** | **al-Nūr 24/30-31** |
|---|---|---|
| **The verb** | *ighḍuḍ* (imperative) | *yaghuḍḍū / yaghḍuḍna* (jussive — with the force of a command) |
| **The root** | غ-ض-ض | غ-ض-ض |
| **The particle** | ***min*** — partitive | ***min*** — partitive |
| **The object** | *ṣawtik* — **the voice** | *abṣārihim* — **the glance** |
| **What is asked** | Not silence, but **the lowering of part of the voice** | Not blindness, but **the lowering of part of the glance** |
| **What is not asked** | The cutting off of the voice altogether | The shutting of the eye altogether |
| **The context** | The manners of walking and of speaking in general | The manners of looking at one another |

**That the two verses use the same pattern is a fact that can be verified from the text.** And the conclusion that follows from it is this:

**The Qurʾān uses the root غ-ض-ض twice, for two different senses, with the same particle. And in both places what it commands is not a *cutting off* but a *measure*.**

**I record this as my own reading;** the structure and the particle of the two verses are the wording of the text and can be compared.

**And about the function of the particle *min* there is more than one explanation in the classical sources, and it needs transmitting:**

| Explanation | Its content |
|---|---|
| **1. Partitive** | "A part of it" — not the whole of the glance but a part of it is lowered |
| **2. Redundant** | The particle adds no sense; the expression means "let them lower their glances" |
| **3. It conveys a starting point** | A lowering that begins from the glance |

**In most of the classical commentaries the first explanation is preferred, and the ground given is this: the scope of the ruling is limited; not every glance but a particular kind of glance is meant.** I transmit this explanation as a view that is widely transmitted. **I impose no preference, and it is not binding.**

**But the comparison with Luqmān 31/19 provides a ground:** there the same particle was used with the same verb, and there it is plain that "cutting off the voice altogether" is not meant — for that verse is regulating the manners of speech. **That the two structures are the same suggests the same function.**

**I record this as an observation of comparison;** not as a ruling.

### بَصَر — the glance, or sight?

**Root: ب-ص-ر.** Its concrete sense is **to see; and to see in such a way as to grasp a thing.** *Baṣīra* is from the same root. *Baṣar* — the sense of sight, and the glance.

**That the word comes in the plural is worth noting: *abṣār*.** The verse speaks, that is, not of a single glance but of **the whole of one's glances.**

**And the word's distribution through the sūra builds a weave:**

| Verse | Expression | What |
|---|---|---|
| **30** | *yaghuḍḍū min **abṣāri**him* | The lowering of the glance |
| **31** | *yaghḍuḍna min **abṣāri**hinna* | The lowering of the glance |
| **37** | *tataqallabu fīhi'l-qulūbu wa'l-**abṣār*** | The day when hearts and **eyes** will be turned about |
| **40** | *lam yakad yarāhā* | So dark that he can **hardly see** his hand |
| **43** | *yakādu sanā barqihī yadhhabu bi'l-**abṣār*** | The flash of the lightning all but takes away the **sight** |
| **44** | *la-ʿibratan li-uli'l-**abṣār*** | A lesson for **those who have eyes** |

**The word occurs six times in the sūra, and four of them after the thirty-fifth verse.**

**This distribution says something, which I record as my own reading:** the sūra first **narrows** the glance (30-31), then **shows** what is to be looked at (35), then tells of the limits of the eye (40, 43), and at the end names those who can see (44).

**The sūra, that is, has a weave built upon the eye.** And this is one of the sides that explain why the sūra's name is *nūr*.

### وَيَحْفَظُوا۟ فُرُوجَهُمْ — the second command, and the absence of the particle

**In the second command there is no *min*. And this is the most striking difference in the arrangement.**

| Command | Particle | Scope |
|---|---|---|
| *yaghuḍḍū **min** abṣārihim* | **Present** — partitive | **Partial** |
| *wa yaḥfaẓū furūjahum* | **Absent** | **Absolute** |

**This difference has been dwelt on in the classical commentaries, and the explanation transmitted is this:** the first command is partial because looking is a necessary part of life; the second is absolute because there is no such necessity there.

**I transmit this explanation as a view that is transmitted.** The one certain thing is that the particle is present in the one and absent in the other, and this is the wording of the text.

**حَفِظَ — root ح-ف-ظ:** to guard, to preserve, to watch over. *Ḥāfiẓ* — one who guards. *Ḥifẓ* — guarding; and memorising (the keeping and preserving of a text).

**فُرُوج — root ف-ر-ج:** its concrete sense is **a gap, an opening, the space between two things.** *Furja* — a rift, a space. The word is used in the Qurʾān of rifts in the sky as well: *mā lahā min furūj* (Qāf 50/6) — "it has no rifts at all".

**The use of the word in this context is a figure of indirect speech**, and the primary sense of the root is "a gap". **The verse does not use the name of an organ directly.**

**I record this as an observation about the style:** all the words the sūra uses in this discussion are indirect — *furūj* (a gap), *zīna* (adornment), *ʿawrāt* (the places left open). **The text does not use the words of the domain it regulates.**

### ذَٰلِكَ أَزْكَىٰ لَهُمْ — purification

**The same word occurred two verses earlier (28).** The root ز-ك-و: **to increase, and to be cleansed.**

**And the placing of the word here shows how the command is positioned.**

**The verse ends the command not as a prohibition but as a *benefit*.** The one addressed is named not as the party that suffers a loss but as the party that gains: *azkā **lahum*** — "more purifying **for them**".

**And it may be recalled that the command *ighḍuḍ min ṣawtik* at `031-lokman.md`, 31/19, also came within a list of counsels, in the setting of a father's advice.** In both verses the command is grounded not on the threat of a penalty but on the man's own good.

I record this as an observation about the style.

### إِنَّ ٱللَّهَ خَبِيرٌۢ بِمَا يَصْنَعُونَ — the choice of the verb

**One word in the verse-ending draws attention: *yaṣnaʿūn* — "what they do".**

**صَنَعَ — root ص-ن-ع:** its concrete sense is **to do a thing with skill, by arranging it.** *Ṣanʿa*, *ṣunʿ*, *maṣnaʿ* (a factory) are from the same root. **The word conveys not a random act but a work that has been contrived.**

**And the Qurʾān uses *yaṣnaʿūn* here instead of *taʿmalūn* (you do). This difference is worth recording.**

| Verb | Root | What it conveys |
|---|---|---|
| **عَمِلَ** | ع-م-ل | To do, in general |
| **صَنَعَ** | ص-ن-ع | To do **with skill, by arranging** |

**And, as was recorded in `049-hucurat.md`, the choice of verb in a sūra's verse-endings agrees with its subject.** The verb chosen here implies that a glance **is not a random movement**: a glance is most often a contrived movement — where, when, how much.

**I record this as my own reading;** the sense of "skill" in the root of the word stands in the dictionary.

---

## 24/31 — وَقُل لِّلْمُؤْمِنَٰتِ يَغْضُضْنَ مِنْ أَبْصَٰرِهِنَّ وَيَحْفَظْنَ فُرُوجَهُنَّ وَلَا يُبْدِينَ زِينَتَهُنَّ إِلَّا مَا ظَهَرَ مِنْهَا وَلْيَضْرِبْنَ بِخُمُرِهِنَّ عَلَىٰ جُيُوبِهِنَّ وَلَا يُبْدِينَ زِينَتَهُنَّ إِلَّا لِبُعُولَتِهِنَّ أَوْ ءَابَآئِهِنَّ … وَتُوبُوٓا۟ إِلَى ٱللَّهِ جَمِيعًا أَيُّهَ ٱلْمُؤْمِنُونَ لَعَلَّكُمْ تُفْلِحُونَ

*Wa qul li'l-muʾmināti yaghḍuḍna min abṣārihinna wa yaḥfaẓna furūjahunna wa lā yubdīna zīnatahunna illā mā ẓahara minhā, wa'l-yaḍribna bi-khumurihinna ʿalā juyūbihinna, wa lā yubdīna zīnatahunna illā li-buʿūlatihinna aw ābāʾihinna aw ābāʾi buʿūlatihinna aw abnāʾihinna aw abnāʾi buʿūlatihinna aw ikhwānihinna aw banī ikhwānihinna aw banī akhawātihinna aw nisāʾihinna aw mā malakat aymānuhunna awi'l-tābiʿīna ghayri uli'l-irbati mina'l-rijāli awi'l-ṭifli'lladhīna lam yaẓharū ʿalā ʿawrāti'l-nisāʾ, wa lā yaḍribna bi-arjulihinna li-yuʿlama mā yukhfīna min zīnatihinna, wa tūbū ila'llāhi jamīʿan ayyuha'l-muʾminūna laʿallakum tufliḥūn*

"And say to the believing women that they lower part of their glances and guard their chastity, and that they not display their adornment **save what appears of it.** And let them draw their coverings over their bosoms. And let them not show their adornment to any but their husbands, their fathers, their husbands' fathers, their sons, their husbands' sons, their brothers, their brothers' sons, their sisters' sons, their own women, those their right hands possess, male attendants who have no need of women, or children not yet aware of women's nakedness. And let them not stamp their feet so that the adornment they conceal may be known. **O believers, turn to God, all of you together, that you may prosper.**"

### The structure of the verse

**After repeating the two commands of the thirtieth verse, the verse adds four new provisions:**

| Order | Expression | Its subject |
|---|---|---|
| **1** | *yaghḍuḍna min abṣārihinna* | **The glance** — the same as in verse 30 |
| **2** | *wa yaḥfaẓna furūjahunna* | **Chastity** — the same as in verse 30 |
| **3** | *wa lā yubdīna zīnatahunna illā mā ẓahara minhā* | **The not displaying of adornment** — the general provision |
| **4** | *wa'l-yaḍribna bi-khumurihinna ʿalā juyūbihinna* | **The placing of the covering** |
| **5** | *wa lā yubdīna zīnatahunna illā li-…* | **The list of exceptions** |
| **6** | *wa lā yaḍribna bi-arjulihinna* | **The act of drawing attention** |
| **7** | *wa tūbū ila'llāhi jamīʿan* | **The close — a call to turn to God** |

**The verse is one of the longest in the sūra and contains six separate provisions.**

### زِينَة — root ز-ي-ن

**Root: ز-ي-ن.** Its concrete sense is **to adorn, to make look fair.** *Zayn* — adornment; *zīna* — adornment, ornament. *Tazyīn* — adorning.

**The word occurred at `049-hucurat.md`, 49/7:** *wa zayyanahū fī qulūbikum* — "and He made it fair in your hearts".

**And there is broad disagreement over the scope of the word** — this is one of the most disputed points in the verse.

### إِلَّا مَا ظَهَرَ مِنْهَا — the exception that is disputed

**This expression is one of those in the classical commentaries over which there is the greatest divergence of view, and the disagreement needs transmitting.**

**ظَهَرَ — root ظ-ه-ر:** its concrete sense is **the back** (*ẓahr*); and from there, **to appear, to come out into the open, to come out on top.** The root was analysed at `033-ahzab.md`, 33/4 (*tuẓāhirūn*).

**And the main explanations of what *mā ẓahara minhā* — "what appears of it" — means are these:**

| Explanation | What *mā ẓahara minhā* is | Its ground |
|---|---|---|
| **1** | **The outer garment** — the clothing worn over and already visible | The wording of the word: what is visible of itself |
| **2** | **The face and the hands** | That covering these would in practice cause difficulty |
| **3** | **The part that appears of itself, without being meant to** | That the verb is not passive: *ẓahara* — it appeared of itself |
| **4** | **The part that varies according to custom** | That the expression has been left indefinite |

**This disagreement takes up wide space in the classical literature of commentary and of jurisprudence; it diverges among the schools and within each school.** In this section **no preference is made, no legal ruling is issued, and none of these is binding.**

**What can be said with certainty is the linguistic structure of the expression, and I record it as an observation:** the verb *ẓahara* is active and intransitive. **That is, not "to show" but "to appear".** And this suggests that the exception has to do not with a person's **intention** but with a thing's **appearing of itself.**

**I give this as an observation about the language; not as a ruling.**

### وَلْيَضْرِبْنَ بِخُمُرِهِنَّ عَلَىٰ جُيُوبِهِنَّ — the dictionary senses of the words

**This clause is the best known part of the verse, and the dictionary sense of two words is decisive.**

#### خُمُر — the plural of *khimār*

**Root: خ-م-ر.** This root was analysed at `047-muhammed.md`, 47/15; I rely on that treatment. The note there:

> **خَمْر — root خ-م-ر: to cover, to hide.** From the same root **خِمَار** (*khimār*) — a covering. **And wine bears this name because it covers the mind.**

**The derivatives of the root:**

| Word | Sense |
|---|---|
| **خَمْر** (*khamr*) | Wine — **what covers the mind** |
| **خِمَار** (*khimār*) | **A covering** — especially one drawn over the head |
| **خُمُر** (*khumur*) | The plural of *khimār* |
| **خَمِيرَة** | Leaven — what is mixed into dough and hidden within it |
| **مُخَامَرَة** | To mix, to enter into |

**And there is a point that must be recorded about the concrete sense of the word:**

***Khimār*, by its root, is "the thing that covers".** The word became established in Arabic as the name of the cloth drawn over the head; **the lexicographers and the exegetes agree on this sense.**

**And the note in the verse's arrangement is important: the verse does not command the wearing of a *khimār*.**

**The structure of the sentence is this:** *wa'l-yaḍribna **bi-khumurihinna** ʿalā juyūbihinna* — "let them draw **their coverings** over their bosoms".

***Khimār*, that is, occurs in the sentence as an object already present** — as a piece of clothing the addressees already have. What is commanded is not to acquire it but **where to bring it.**

**This is an observation about the word order, read from the wording of the text:** *bi-khumurihinna* — "with their own coverings". The possessive pronoun conveys that the object is already there.

**And at this point a piece of historical information is widely transmitted in the classical commentaries, and I transmit it as information that is transmitted:** in that period the ends of the *khimār* the women drew over their heads were thrown back, and the opening of the garment at the breast was left uncovered. What the verse commands is that the ends of the covering be brought forward, over the opening at the neck.

**I transmit this as a historical note widely transmitted in the classical commentaries; I give no chain of transmission and no attribution to a source, because I cannot give one with certainty.**

#### جُيُوب — the plural of *jayb*

**Root: ج-ي-ب.** The sense the lexicographers record: **the opening at the neck and breast of a garment; the place the neck-opening leaves bare.**

**The word names the opening of a garment through which the head is passed and which runs down towards the breast.** From the same root *jawb* — to pierce, to cut open; **that is, a *jayb* is the *cut* place of a garment.**

**The Turkish *cep* ("pocket") comes from this word** — an opening made in a garment.

**And the Qurʾān uses the same word in another place:** *usluk yadaka fī **jaybik*** (al-Qaṣaṣ 28/32; al-Naml 27/12) — "put your hand into **your bosom**". This verse was treated in `028-kasas.md`. **There too the word conveys the neck-opening of a garment.**

**What the two words build together:**

| Word | Root | What |
|---|---|---|
| **خِمَار** | خ-م-ر — to cover | **The covering thing** — already in hand |
| **جَيْب** | ج-ي-ب — to cut open | **The place left open** — the cut of the garment |

**The verse brings the covering thing over the place left open.** This is the literal work of the sentence.

**And the verb is worth noting: *ḍaraba bi-… ʿalā* — "to bring one thing down over another, to let it fall over it".** The concreteness of the verb conveys the actual moving of the garment.

### The rulings the schools have drawn

**The rulings drawn from this verse diverge among the schools and within each school. The table below is meant to show at which points the divergence lies. None of them is this text's preference and none is binding.**

| The point of divergence | The direction of the views transmitted |
|---|---|
| **The scope of *mā ẓahara minhā*** | Whether the face and the hands fall within this exception; whether the outer garment is what is meant |
| **Which parts the covering covers** | Different conclusions have been drawn regarding the head, the neck, the bosom and the feet |
| **The scope of certain classes in the list of exceptions** | Whom the expressions *nisāʾihinna*, *mā malakat aymānuhunna* and *ghayri uli'l-irba* include |
| **The relation between covering in prayer and covering outside prayer** | That the two discussions are taken up separately, and that their measures may differ |
| **The role of custom and of necessity** | To what extent the ruling depends upon custom and upon circumstances |

**This table is not a legal opinion and cannot be read as a guide to practice.**

**And one further note must be entered:** the rulings drawn from this verse have been applied in different ways in different regions through history. **This arises not from any vagueness in the verse but from the verse's leaving an exception such as *mā ẓahara minhā* indefinite, and from the place of the concept of *ʿurf* (custom) in jurisprudence.** This is recorded as an observation; it leads to no preference.

### The list of exceptions — an observation on the arrangement

**The verse enumerates eleven classes. And the structure of the list is worth noting:**

| Group | Who |
|---|---|
| **The spouse** | *buʿūlatihinna* |
| **Kinship — upward** | *ābāʾihinna*, *ābāʾi buʿūlatihinna* |
| **Kinship — downward** | *abnāʾihinna*, *abnāʾi buʿūlatihinna* |
| **Kinship — collateral** | *ikhwānihinna*, *banī ikhwānihinna*, *banī akhawātihinna* |
| **Women** | *nisāʾihinna* |
| **The household** | *mā malakat aymānuhunna* |
| **Those without intent** | *al-tābiʿīna ghayri uli'l-irba*, *al-ṭifl* |

**The last two items of the list give a measure, and I record this as an observation about the word order:** *ghayri uli'l-irba* (those who have no need of women) and *alladhīna lam yaẓharū ʿalā ʿawrāti'l-nisāʾ* (children not yet aware of women's nakedness).

**The list, that is, is not built on kinship alone;** the last two items look at **whether an intent is present or not.**

**إِرْبَة — root أ-ر-ب:** need, want; and skill. **There is disagreement in the classical sources over the sense of the word in this context, and I content myself with transmitting it.**

**The expression *mā malakat aymānuhunna* in the list names an institution belonging to the social structure of that period.** The sūra's thirty-third verse will regulate the way out of that institution, and it will be taken up there.

### وَلَا يَضْرِبْنَ بِأَرْجُلِهِنَّ — the act of drawing attention

**This is one of the finest notes in the verse, and it is generally passed over.**

**The verb is the same as the verb in the fourth provision: *ḍaraba*.** And the arrangement sets up a symmetry:

| Provision | Verb | Object | Its direction |
|---|---|---|---|
| **4** | *wa'l-**yaḍribna** bi-khumurihinna* | The covering | **A command** |
| **6** | *wa lā **yaḍribna** bi-arjulihinna* | The feet | **A prohibition** |

**The same verb, once as a command and once as a prohibition.** This is a repetition that can be verified from the text, and I record it as an observation about the word order.

**And the ground of the second prohibition is given in the verse: *li-yuʿlama mā yukhfīna min zīnatihinna* — "so that the adornment they conceal may be known".**

**What is forbidden, that is, is not the sound of the foot — it is the *purpose*.** Not the act itself but why the act is done.

**This says something about the verse as a whole, which I record as my own reading:** when a rule of covering is laid down, it is possible to keep to the letter of the rule and get round its spirit. **The verse closes off that possibility from within:** every act that **makes known** what has been concealed falls within the scope of the command to conceal.

**And this provision shows that the verse's ruling is framed not as a matter of *outward appearance* but as a matter of *purpose*.**

### وَتُوبُوٓا۟ إِلَى ٱللَّهِ جَمِيعًا — the rulings closing with a call to turn

**The last sentence of the verse is the most striking place in this section and deserves separate treatment.**

**And the first observation is in the arrangement: the address changes.**

The verse had come from beginning to end in the **feminine plural**: *yaghḍuḍna*, *yaḥfaẓna*, *yubdīna*, *yaḍribna*. **And in the last clause the form changes: *tūbū* — a masculine plural imperative. And the addressee is named openly: *ayyuha'l-muʾminūn* — "O believers".**

**At the end of the commands given to women, that is, the verse opens the address to the whole of the community.**

**This is a fact that can be verified from the text and is recorded in the classical commentaries as well.** And its consequence is this:

**The addressees of the two verses (30-31) had been fixed separately; the close is shared.**

| Verse | Addressee |
|---|---|
| **30** | *al-muʾminīn* — men |
| **31 (its beginning)** | *al-muʾmināt* — women |
| **31 (its end)** | *ayyuha'l-muʾminūn* — **all of them** |

**I record this as my own reading;** the change of forms is the wording of the text and can be verified.

**The second observation: that the commands close with a call to turn to God.**

**And this is a fact of style that must be recorded.** After a series of provisions of manners the verse announces no penalty, sets no threat, offers no rebuke. **It makes a call to turn.**

***Tawba*, as in the twenty-second verse, is by its root a turning.** Not a feeling but a movement.

**And the scope of the call: *jamīʿan* — "all together".** The call, that is, is not to those who fail to keep the command; **it is to everyone.**

**Its meaning is worth dwelling on, and I record this as my own reading:**

**When a provision of manners is laid down, its commonest misuse is to turn it into an *instrument of measurement*.** Who keeps it, who does not. And the verse closes off that possibility immediately after the provisions: **the call is to everyone.**

**And the measure recorded in the section on the sūra in `049-hucurat.md` works here exactly:**

> *"In all five places the same operation is performed: the measuring instrument is taken out of a man's hand."*

**The last sentence of al-Nūr 24/31 performs the same operation.**

**The third observation: the verse-ending.**

*Laʿallakum tufliḥūn* — "that you may prosper". **The root ف-ل-ح:** its concrete sense is **to split the soil, to till.** *Fallāḥ* — a ploughman. **And *falāḥ*: to split through and come out, to attain what one seeks.**

**The concrete sense of the root fits the close:** for a seed to split the soil and come out, it must first stay under the soil. **And the root ز-ك-و (in the thirtieth verse: *azkā*) came from the same field: to increase, to be fruitful.**

**Both roots come from the field of agriculture, and both convey the outcome of *holding back*.**

I record this as an observation about the root.

### What this says to the present

**In this section the verse's place in today's controversies is not entered. The three things to be recorded come from the verse's own arrangement.**

**One. That the command is given to both sides.**

The first two commands of the thirtieth verse and of the thirty-first are **the same.** This is a fact that can be verified from the text, and the text itself gives no room for the discussion to be taken up one-sidedly.

**Two. The taking of purpose into the ruling.**

The provision *wa lā yaḍribna bi-arjulihinna li-yuʿlama mā yukhfīna* closes off the possibility of keeping to the letter of a rule and getting round its spirit. **And this provision makes it hard to read the ruling as a matter of outward form.**

**Three. And to whom the close is addressed.**

Having enumerated the provisions, the verse opens the address to all the believers and calls them to turn to God. **This is a barrier, set from within the text itself, against the verse's being used to measure a group.**

**All three of these notes are read from the wording of the text, and none of them announces a ruling upon either side.**

---

## 24/32 — وَأَنكِحُوا۟ ٱلْأَيَٰمَىٰ مِنكُمْ وَٱلصَّٰلِحِينَ مِنْ عِبَادِكُمْ وَإِمَآئِكُمْ إِن يَكُونُوا۟ فُقَرَآءَ يُغْنِهِمُ ٱللَّهُ مِن فَضْلِهِۦ وَٱللَّهُ وَٰسِعٌ عَلِيمٌ

*Wa ankiḥu'l-ayāmā minkum wa'l-ṣāliḥīna min ʿibādikum wa imāʾikum, in yakūnū fuqarāʾa yughnihimu'llāhu min faḍlih, wa'llāhu wāsiʿun ʿalīm*

"Marry off those among you who are unmarried, and those of your male and female slaves who are fit for it. If they are poor, God will enrich them of His bounty. God is of ample bounty, knowing."

### The verse's place in the block

**The thirty-second verse comes immediately after the thirtieth and thirty-first, and the arrangement is worth noting.**

**The two verses had given a command to *hold back*. The thirty-second gives a command to *open a way*.**

| Verse | Command | Its direction |
|---|---|---|
| **30-31** | Lower the glance, guard chastity | **Holding back** |
| **32** | Marry them off | **Opening a way** |

**And this ordering carries a logic, which I record as my own reading:** when a boundary is laid down, a way must also be opened that makes it possible to stay within it. **The verse shows the way immediately after the restriction.**

**And the addressee of the command is worth noting: *ankiḥū* — a plural imperative.** The address is not to the individual but **to the community.** It is not said to the one who wishes to marry, "marry"; it is said to the community, "marry them off".

**The verse, that is, places what looks like an individual decision as a *social responsibility*.**

### ٱلْأَيَٰمَىٰ — root أ-ي-م

The plural of **أَيِّم (*ayyim*)**. The sense the lexicographers record: **one who has no spouse.**

**And the scope of the word matters:** *ayyim* is used both of one who has never married and of one who has lost a spouse; **and both of men and of women.**

**The word, that is, covers not one condition but every condition of "having no spouse at present".** This is a scope the lexicographers record.

**And that this word is chosen is worth noting:** the verse does not use *al-abzār* or any other narrow word; **it uses the inclusive one.**

### إِن يَكُونُوا۟ فُقَرَآءَ يُغْنِهِمُ ٱللَّهُ مِن فَضْلِهِۦ — the note about poverty

**This is the most striking clause in the verse, because it meets an *objection* in advance.**

**The objection is plain and is the same in every age: "he has no means."** The verse does not deny it; **it accepts the poverty and ties the answer to somewhere else.**

**فَقِير — root ف-ق-ر:** its concrete sense is **the vertebra** (*faqar*). The connection the lexicographers draw: a *faqīr* is one whose backbone is broken — one who cannot straighten his back. The root was taken up at `035-fatir.md`, 35/15 (*antumu'l-fuqarāʾu ila'llāh*).

**أَغْنَىٰ — root غ-ن-ي:** to be without need, to suffice. *Ghanī* — one who needs no one.

**And the conditional structure of the sentence is worth noting: *in yakūnū … yughnihim*.** The answer of the condition is a promise, and the agent of the promise is God.

**This is the place in the verse most open to misreading, and a note must be entered:**

**The sentence is not a formula of guarantee.** The Qurʾān's general language about provision, as has been recorded at many places in this commentary (`029-ankebut.md` 29/17, `042-sura.md` 42/27), **sets up no undertaking of accounts.** What the verse does is to ask that **poverty not be counted an obstacle by itself.**

**And the qualification *min faḍlih* supports this:** the enriching is named as coming not from a man's own account but **from bounty.**

**I record this as my own reading;** the conditional structure of the sentence and the qualification *min faḍlih* are the wording of the text.

### وَٱللَّهُ وَٰسِعٌ عَلِيمٌ

**وَاسِع — root و-س-ع:** breadth. The same root as *al-saʿa* in the twenty-second verse.

**And the link between the two verses is worth noting:** in the twenty-second verse it was **man's** breadth that was named (*ulu'l-faḍli minkum wa'l-saʿa*); in the thirty-second, **God's.** **The same root, ten verses apart, with two different subjects.**

I record this as an observation about the word order.

---

## 24/33 — وَلْيَسْتَعْفِفِ ٱلَّذِينَ لَا يَجِدُونَ نِكَاحًا حَتَّىٰ يُغْنِيَهُمُ ٱللَّهُ مِن فَضْلِهِۦ وَٱلَّذِينَ يَبْتَغُونَ ٱلْكِتَٰبَ مِمَّا مَلَكَتْ أَيْمَٰنُكُمْ فَكَاتِبُوهُمْ إِنْ عَلِمْتُمْ فِيهِمْ خَيْرًا وَءَاتُوهُم مِّن مَّالِ ٱللَّهِ ٱلَّذِىٓ ءَاتَىٰكُمْ وَلَا تُكْرِهُوا۟ فَتَيَٰتِكُمْ عَلَى ٱلْبِغَآءِ إِنْ أَرَدْنَ تَحَصُّنًا لِّتَبْتَغُوا۟ عَرَضَ ٱلْحَيَوٰةِ ٱلدُّنْيَا وَمَن يُكْرِههُّنَّ فَإِنَّ ٱللَّهَ مِنۢ بَعْدِ إِكْرَٰهِهِنَّ غَفُورٌ رَّحِيمٌ

*Wa'l-yastaʿfifi'lladhīna lā yajidūna nikāḥan ḥattā yughniyahumu'llāhu min faḍlih, wa'lladhīna yabtaghūna'l-kitāba mimmā malakat aymānukum fa-kātibūhum in ʿalimtum fīhim khayrā, wa ātūhum min māli'llāhi'lladhī ātākum, wa lā tukrihū fatayātikum ʿala'l-bighāʾi in aradna taḥaṣṣunan li-tabtaghū ʿaraḍa'l-ḥayāti'l-dunyā, wa man yukrihhunna fa-inna'llāha min baʿdi ikrāhihinna ghafūrun raḥīm*

"And let those who find no means of marriage keep themselves chaste until God enriches them of His bounty. And if any of those your right hands possess seek **a deed of manumission**, make that deed with them if you know some good in them, and give them of the wealth of God that He has given you. And do not compel your slave-girls to prostitution, when they desire to keep chaste, in order to seek the passing goods of the life of this world. And whoever compels them — God, after their being compelled, is forgiving, merciful."

### The verse's three distinct provisions

**The long verse takes up three distinct subjects, and all three look at a situation of *impossibility*:**

| Provision | Whose condition | What is done |
|---|---|---|
| **1** | One who finds no means of marriage | *Istiʿfāf* — waiting |
| **2** | One who wishes to buy his freedom | **The making of a deed is commanded** |
| **3** | One who is compelled | **Compulsion is forbidden** |

**What the three have in common: all are about people in a position where they cannot change their own condition by themselves.**

### ٱسْتَعَفَّ — root ع-ف-ف

**Root: ع-ف-ف.** The sense the lexicographers record: **to withdraw one's hand from a thing, to keep away from it; to hold oneself back.** *ʿIffa* is from the same root.

**The form: Form X — *istaʿaffa*: to strive to be chaste, to make an effort to hold oneself back.**

**And the choice of the form is worth noting:** Form X conveys **the seeking of a thing and the effort made for it.** The verse does not say "be chaste"; **it says "strive to stay chaste".** The verb conveys an effort.

**And the word will return in the sixtieth verse:** *wa an yastaʿfifna khayrun lahunn.*

### وَٱلَّذِينَ يَبْتَغُونَ ٱلْكِتَٰبَ — the deed of manumission

**This is a place among the Qurʾān's social provisions that needs recording separately.**

***Kitāba* (كِتَابَة) is the name of an institution in classical jurisprudence:** a written contract made by a person of slave status with his owner, by which he gains his freedom in return for paying a fixed price.

**And what the verse establishes has three stages:**

| Stage | Expression | What |
|---|---|---|
| **1** | *alladhīna yabtaghūna'l-kitāb* | **The request comes from the person himself** |
| **2** | *fa-kātibūhum in ʿalimtum fīhim khayrā* | **The command to make the deed** — with a condition |
| **3** | *wa ātūhum min māli'llāhi'lladhī ātākum* | **The command to give property** |

**The third stage is worth recording and draws attention:** the person is not only granted the right to a deed; **it is also commanded that property be given him so that he may pay the price of the deed.**

**The provision, that is, does not merely open the door of exit — it takes upon itself part of the cost of passing through the door.**

**I record this as an observation about the word order;** the three stages are in the wording of the text.

**And the expression *min māli'llāhi'lladhī ātākum* is worth noting:** the property to be given is named not "your property" but **"the wealth of God that He has given you".** The source of ownership is called to mind.

**Over the juristic details — the binding force of the deed, whether the command conveys obligation or recommendation, the amount of property to be given — there is disagreement among the schools. In this section no preference is made and none is binding.**

**And a historical note may be entered:** in a world in which it found the institution of slavery already in place, the Qurʾān framed its provisions concerning it **always in the direction of exit** — the freeing of a slave in expiations, *fi'l-riqāb* among the categories of alms, and here *kitāba*. **This is an observation that can be read from the text about the direction of the verses**, and I record it as an observation; I do not present it as a historical assessment.

### وَلَا تُكْرِهُوا۟ فَتَيَٰتِكُمْ عَلَى ٱلْبِغَآءِ — the prohibition of compulsion

**The verse's third provision is the most concrete and the sternest.**

**أَكْرَهَ — root ك-ر-ه:** to dislike; in Form IV: **to make someone do what he dislikes, to compel.** The root was analysed at `002-bakara.md`, 2/256 (*lā ikrāha fi'l-dīn*); I rely on that treatment.

**بِغَاء — root ب-غ-ي:** the primary sense of the root is **to want, to seek; and to overstep the bound.** The root was analysed at `049-hucurat.md`, 49/9 (*baghat iḥdāhumā*).

**And the sentence the verse builds must be read with care.**

**The qualification *in aradna taḥaṣṣunan* — "when they desire to keep chaste" — has been explained in the classical commentaries, and the explanation transmitted is this:** the qualification is not a condition of the prohibition; **it is a qualification describing the actual situation of that period.** It does not mean "if they do not desire it, they may be compelled".

**Two things are given as grounds, and both can be read from the text:**

**One. The logic of the sentence.** Compulsion (*ikrāh*), by its very definition, can be applied only to someone who does not want. **One does not "compel" a person who wants.** To understand the qualification as a condition, then, contradicts the sentence's own word.

**Two. The end of the sentence.** *Wa man yukrihhunna fa-inna'llāha min baʿdi ikrāhihinna ghafūrun raḥīm.* **The verse names the condition of the one compelled with forgiveness** — that is, the compelled party is not held responsible for the act.

**I transmit this explanation as one widely transmitted in the classical commentaries; and I record that both grounds can be read from the text.**

**تَحَصُّن — root ح-ص-ن:** the same root as *muḥṣanāt* in the fourth verse. **Protection like a fortress.** That the word returns in the sūra is worth noting: in the fourth verse an attribute, here a **will.**

### عَرَض ٱلْحَيَوٰةِ ٱلدُّنْيَا — "the passing goods"

**عَرَض — root ع-ر-ض:** breadth, width; and **what does not last, what comes and goes.** In the language of philosophy *ʿaraḍ* (accident) is the opposite of *jawhar* (substance): what cannot stand on its own, what is transient.

**And the choice of the word carries a judgement:** the gain that is the cause of the compulsion is named **what does not last.** What is got in return for what a person is subjected to is named *ʿaraḍ*.

**I record this as an observation about the word.**

### فَإِنَّ ٱللَّهَ مِنۢ بَعْدِ إِكْرَٰهِهِنَّ غَفُورٌ رَّحِيمٌ — the close

**Whom the verse-ending is directed at has been debated in the classical commentaries; but the explanation widely transmitted is that it is directed at the party compelled.**

**And the arrangement of the sentence supports this: *min baʿdi ikrāhihinna* — "after their being compelled".** The pronoun is feminine, and the expression states the condition of the party compelled.

**And the sense of "covering" in the root *gh-f-r* shows its work here again.** In the fifth verse too the same pair of names came after a matter of laying something bare.

---

## 24/34 — وَلَقَدْ أَنزَلْنَآ إِلَيْكُمْ ءَايَٰتٍ مُّبَيِّنَٰتٍ وَمَثَلًا مِّنَ ٱلَّذِينَ خَلَوْا۟ مِن قَبْلِكُمْ وَمَوْعِظَةً لِّلْمُتَّقِينَ

*Wa laqad anzalnā ilaykum āyātin mubayyinātin wa mathalan mina'lladhīna khalaw min qablikum wa mawʿiẓatan li'l-muttaqīn*

"We have surely sent down to you clear verses, and an example from those who passed away before you, and an admonition for those who guard themselves."

### The seal of the block and the door of the next

**This verse does two things at once, and its place in the arrangement is worth noting.**

**One. It closes the fourth block (27-34)** — in the language of the first verse: *anzalnā … āyātin mubayyināt.* **In the first verse it was *anzalnā fīhā āyātin bayyināt*.** The same verb, the same object, a different adjectival form.

**Two. And it says one word: *mathal*. The next verse is the sūra's most famous *mathal*.**

### مَثَلًا — the parable named in advance

**مَثَل — root م-ث-ل:** to resemble, to be an example. *Mithl* — a like; *timthāl* — an image. **And *mathal*: a comparison framed in order to tell of something, an example.**

**And the placing of the word here opens the door of the thirty-fifth verse.**

| Verse | The root *mathal* |
|---|---|
| **34** | *wa **mathalan** mina'lladhīna khalaw* |
| **35** | ***mathalu** nūrihī ka-mishkātin* |
| **35 (its end)** | *wa yaḍribu'llāhu'l-**amthāla** li'l-nās* |
| **39** | *ka-sarābin bi-qīʿa* — (a comparison, but not the word) |

**The root three times, within two verses.** And the last clause of the thirty-fifth verse says so outright: *wa yaḍribu'llāhu'l-amthāla li'l-nās* — "God coins likenesses for people".

**I record this as an observation about the word order.**

### مُّبَيِّنَات — the difference of form

**In the first verse *bayyināt*, here *mubayyināt*.**

| Form | Its kind | Its sense |
|---|---|---|
| **بَيِّنَات** (*bayyināt*) | An assimilate adjective | **What is itself clear** |
| **مُبَيِّنَات** (*mubayyināt*) | The active participle of Form II | **What makes clear, what makes plain** |

**The difference is one of transitivity:** the one conveys its own clarity, the other the work of **making something else clear.**

**And in the classical sources on the variant readings it is also transmitted that the word is read *mubayyanāt* (a passive participle — "made clear").** Since I cannot state with certainty which reader read which, I write no names.

**What the three forms together say may be recorded:** the verses are clear in themselves, they make other things clear, and they have themselves been made clear.

I record this as an observation about the form.

### Three things: *āyāt*, *mathal*, *mawʿiẓa*

**The verse divides what has been sent down into three, and the addressee of each of the three differs:**

| What | To whom | What it does |
|---|---|---|
| ***āyātin mubayyināt*** | General | **It makes clear** |
| ***mathalan mina'lladhīna khalaw*** | General | **It gives an example** |
| ***mawʿiẓatan li'l-muttaqīn*** | **To those who guard themselves** | **It admonishes** |

**And the narrowing of the third one's addressee is worth noting.**

*Mawʿiẓa* — root **و-ع-ظ**; analysed at the seventeenth verse: **reminding and holding back.** And an admonition is an admonition only for one who will take it. **The verse says this with a word: *li'l-muttaqīn*.**

**This agrees with a measure recorded in the classical commentaries:** the same text does different work according to its addressee. It was recorded at `046-ahkaf.md`, 46/12: *"the same text does two distinct works according to its addressee"* — warning and glad tidings.

---

## 24/35 — ٱللَّهُ نُورُ ٱلسَّمَٰوَٰتِ وَٱلْأَرْضِ مَثَلُ نُورِهِۦ كَمِشْكَوٰةٍ فِيهَا مِصْبَاحٌ ٱلْمِصْبَاحُ فِى زُجَاجَةٍ ٱلزُّجَاجَةُ كَأَنَّهَا كَوْكَبٌ دُرِّىٌّ يُوقَدُ مِن شَجَرَةٍ مُّبَٰرَكَةٍ زَيْتُونَةٍ لَّا شَرْقِيَّةٍ وَلَا غَرْبِيَّةٍ يَكَادُ زَيْتُهَا يُضِىٓءُ وَلَوْ لَمْ تَمْسَسْهُ نَارٌ نُّورٌ عَلَىٰ نُورٍ يَهْدِى ٱللَّهُ لِنُورِهِۦ مَن يَشَآءُ وَيَضْرِبُ ٱللَّهُ ٱلْأَمْثَٰلَ لِلنَّاسِ وَٱللَّهُ بِكُلِّ شَىْءٍ عَلِيمٌ

*Allāhu nūru'l-samāwāti wa'l-arḍ, mathalu nūrihī ka-mishkātin fīhā miṣbāḥ, al-miṣbāḥu fī zujāja, al-zujājatu ka-annahā kawkabun durriyyun yūqadu min shajaratin mubārakatin zaytūnatin lā sharqiyyatin wa lā gharbiyyatin yakādu zaytuhā yuḍīʾu wa law lam tamsashu nār, nūrun ʿalā nūr, yahdi'llāhu li-nūrihī man yashāʾ, wa yaḍribu'llāhu'l-amthāla li'l-nās, wa'llāhu bi-kulli shayʾin ʿalīm*

"God is the light of the heavens and the earth. The likeness of His light is as a **niche** in which there is a **lamp**; the lamp within a **glass**; the glass as it were a **star shining like a pearl**, kindled from a **blessed olive tree, neither of the east nor of the west**, whose oil would almost give light though no fire had touched it. **Light upon light.** God guides to His light whom He wills. God coins likenesses for people. God knows everything."

### A note about this verse

**This verse is the centre of the sūra, and it is the most detailed part of this section.**

The verse is one of those most written about in the history of Islamic thought: it has been taken up separately in the literatures of commentary, of theology, of philosophy and of Sufism, and independent treatises have been written on it.

**What will be done in this section is this:** the elements of the parable will be worked out one by one, what each of them does will be shown in a table, and the classical explanations of the first sentence will be transmitted **together with their disagreements.** **No preference will be imposed and no binding ruling will be issued.**

### ٱللَّهُ نُورُ ٱلسَّمَٰوَٰتِ وَٱلْأَرْضِ — a note of *tanzīh*

**The verse's first sentence must be handled with care, and a note needs entering at the very start.**

**This expression cannot be understood in the sense of a bodily light.**

**The reason is established by the Qurʾān's own language, and it agrees with the series of *tanzīh* recorded at `002-bakara.md`, 2/255 (the Verse of the Throne).** There a series of ten clauses of *tanzīh* stood, and each clause removed a bodily or corporeal attribute. The verse is read together with the principle *"there is nothing like Him"* (al-Shūrā 42/11).

**Light, in the physical sense, is a created thing.** Indeed the Qurʾān states that light is created: *wa jaʿala'l-ẓulumāti wa'l-nūr* (al-Anʿām 6/1) — "and He **made** the darknesses and the light". **What has been created cannot be the essence of the Creator.**

**This note is entered openly in the classical tradition of commentary as well.** And the exegetes have taken different roads in explaining the expression.

### The classical explanations of the first sentence

**The main directions in the classical literature of commentary are these. None of them is preferred and none is binding:**

| Direction | The sense of the expression | Its ground |
|---|---|---|
| **1. *Munawwir*** | "God is the **illuminator** of the heavens and the earth" — a governed noun is understood | Such an ellipsis is common in Arabic; and the rest of the verse says *nūrihī* (His light), thus **ascribing** the light to God — that is, the light is not the essence but something ascribed to the essence |
| **2. The light of guidance** | "God is the **source of guidance** for the people of the heavens and the earth" | The Qurʾān uses *nūr* again and again of guidance (al-Māʾida 5/15-16; al-Anʿām 6/122; al-Ṭalāq 65/11) |
| **3. The source of being, that which makes manifest** | "The visibility and the being of everything is from Him"; as light makes things visible | An explanation developed in the literature of theology and of philosophy, by way of the concept of *ẓuhūr* |
| **4. *Tafwīḍ*** | The sense of the expression is referred to God; its how is not entered into | The general stance adopted with ambiguous verses |

**And all four are represented in the classical sources.**

**An observation about the word order may be recorded, and I give it as my own reading:** the verse itself enters a qualification immediately after the first sentence: ***mathalu nūrihī*** — "the **likeness** of His light".

**Directly after the first sentence, that is, the verse passes into the language of a *mathal*.** And a *mathal* is, by definition, not the thing spoken of but **a comparison framed in order to speak of it.**

**This means that the verse carries a note of *tanzīh* within itself:** the text itself says that what it will describe is a likeness. **And the last clause of the verse repeats it: *wa yaḍribu'llāhu'l-amthāla li'l-nās*.**

**Moreover the genitive in the expression *nūrihī* — "His light" — is worth noting:** in the second clause the light is named as something **ascribed** to God. **This provides a ground for the first and second explanations of how the first sentence is to be understood.**

**I record this as an observation about the word order; not as a preference.**

### The elements of the parable — one by one

**The parable is made of six elements, and each is either inside the one before it or feeds it.**

#### مِشْكَاة — *mishkāt*

**The sense the lexicographers record: a hollow opened in a wall which does not go through to the other side; the niche in which a lamp is set.**

**And the qualification "which does not go through to the other side" in the definition is decisive:** a *mishkāt* is not a window. A window lets light through; **a niche holds the light and throws it back.**

**A note about the word's origin:** some of the lexicographers record that the word came into Arabic from another language. **I transmit this view as a note that is transmitted; I say nothing definite.**

**Its work in the parable:** to **gather** the light and to prevent its scattering. A lamp may burn in the open air too; but the lamp in a niche does not scatter its light — it gives it in one direction and concentrates it.

#### مِصْبَاح — *miṣbāḥ*

**Root: ص-ب-ح.** Its concrete sense is **morning** (*ṣubḥ*). The root was analysed at `049-hucurat.md`, 49/6 (*fa-tuṣbiḥū*); the note there: *"a thing done at night being seen in the morning."*

**And *miṣbāḥ*: the thing that brings the morning — a lamp.**

**The origin of the word is worth noting, and I give it as an observation about the root:** in Arabic the lamp has been named "the instrument of morning". **A small light, that is, has been seen as a piece of morning within the night.**

**And the distinction recorded at `071-nuh.md`, 71/16, works here:** *nūr* is the light itself; *sirāj* and *miṣbāḥ* are the **body that produces** the light.

**Its work in the parable:** **the source of the light.** The element that stands at the centre of the parable.

#### زُجَاجَة — *zujāja*

**Glass.** The sense the lexicographers record: a transparent substance that lets light through.

**Its work in the parable runs two ways, and both are worth noting:**

| Work | How |
|---|---|
| **To protect** | It guards the flame from the wind; it keeps it from going out |
| **To increase** | It lets the light through and spreads it **concentrated** |

**Glass, that is, is both a barrier and a thing that lets through.** It does not hold the light back, but it does protect the flame.

**And this is the finest element in the parable, which I record as my own reading:** to protect a thing and to shut it in are not the same. **Glass is a boundary that protects without covering.**

**And this agrees with the sūra as a whole:** Sūrat al-Nūr speaks from beginning to end of boundaries — the boundary of the house, of the glance, of speech. **And in the thirty-fifth verse an element that is a boundary enters the parable as something that *increases* the light rather than lessening it.**

#### كَوْكَبٌ دُرِّىٌّ — a star like a pearl

**كَوْكَب — a star.**

**دُرِّىّ — and there is disagreement over the origin of the word:**

| Reading / origin | Root | Sense |
|---|---|---|
| **دُرِّيّ** (*durriyy*) | د-ر-ر | Shining like a **pearl** (*durr*) |
| **دِرِّيء / دُرِّيء** (*dirrīʾ / durrīʾ*) | د-ر-أ | **Driven, shot forth** — a shooting star |

**The different readings of the word are recorded in the classical sources on the variant readings. Since I cannot state with certainty which reader read which, I write no names.**

**The two pictures the two readings give are worth noting:**

- **The pearl reading:** a fixed, pure body shining of itself.
- **The shooting-star reading:** a moving, sudden brightness that catches the eye.

**The root د-ر-أ occurred in the sūra's eighth verse (*yadraʾu* — it averts).** That the two words may come from the same root is the ground of the second reading.

**Its work in the parable:** to give the **quality** of the glass. The glass is no ordinary glass; **it appears to be a source of light itself.**

#### شَجَرَةٍ مُّبَٰرَكَةٍ زَيْتُونَةٍ — the blessed olive tree

**The source of the fuel is named here, and the plant chosen is worth noting.**

**زَيْتُون — the olive.** The Qurʾān names this tree in several places: *wa'l-zaytūni wa ṭūri sīnīn* (al-Tīn 95/1) — treated in `095-tin.md`; *wa shajaratan takhruju min ṭūri saynāʾa tanbutu bi'l-duhn* (al-Muʾminūn 23/20).

**مُبَارَكَة — root ب-ر-ك:** its concrete sense is **a camel's kneeling down, its settling and staying in a place** (*burūk*). *Birka* — a pool, the place where water gathers and stays. **And *baraka*: a thing's staying in a place and increasing, its continuance.**

**The concrete sense of the root explains the work of the attribute here, and I record this as my own reading:** *baraka* is not a sudden abundance — it is **a lasting and continuing increase.** And the olive tree is the concrete example of that description: it grows slowly, it lives long, and it bears fruit for generations.

**Concrete reasons for the choice of the olive can be enumerated, and these are historically verifiable pieces of information:**

| The property | Its counterpart in the parable |
|---|---|
| Olive oil is **the commonest lamp fuel** of that age | The parable is built with something the hearer knows |
| The tree **lives very long** — for centuries | The concrete counterpart of continuance |
| It **grows in arid soil** | Being able to exist with little |
| Its oil **burns without soot** and gives a clean light | The quality of the light |

**These properties were known to everyone living in that country.** The force of the parable comes from its being familiar.

#### لَّا شَرْقِيَّةٍ وَلَا غَرْبِيَّةٍ — "neither of the east nor of the west"

**This is the most disputed element in the parable, and several explanations are transmitted in the classical commentaries:**

| Explanation | Its content |
|---|---|
| **1. Standing in open ground** | The tree is in a place that gets neither the morning sun alone nor the evening sun alone; **it gets the sun all day.** It is recorded that the oil of such a tree is purer |
| **2. Belonging to no place** | The tree is not the property of a particular region or of a particular direction |
| **3. A pointer to a definite region** | Reports to the effect that the expression means a particular region |

**I impose no preference, and it is not binding.**
